FOREWORD 

This study was prepared by Eugene W. Geniesse for the Subcommittee on Patents, Trademarks, and Copyrights as part of its study of the U.S. patent system, conducted pursuant to Senate Resolution 

240 of the 86th Congress. It is one of a series being prepared under the supervision of John C. Stedman, associate counsel of the subcommittee.

One of the major problems that has perennially plagued the patent system has been that of Patent Office administration. It is a matter of general knowledge and concern that the Patent Office lacks sufficient staff, equipment, and budget. The distressing situation under which the Patent Office issues patents, which often ends in invalidation if and when they get into litigation because of prior art that the Patent Office examiner apparently overlooked-is a matter of common knowledge. A backlog of 200,000 pending applications and a timelag averaging several years (and sometimes amounting to a decade or more) between the initial application and final disposal are frequently decried, as are the attendant pressures on the examiners. 

What is often lacking in these comments and criticisms is a real understanding of the complex Patent Office mechanisms that attend the processing of applications and that give rise to the resulting problems. One easily sees the shortcomings, but not so easily the circumstances that cause them.

Mr. Geniesse has filled these gaps in our knowledge, providing us with a detailed and perceptive review of Patent Office processes. He points out the things that must be done if the examiner is to do a competent and responsible job, and exposes some of the unnecessary and antiquated practices that contribute to the difficulties. The net result is a sympathetic and yet perceptive and critical study of Patent Office operations-an analysis that provides both a major contribution to our understanding of Patent Office operations and some concrete, down-to-earth suggestions for improving them. 

One would be hard put to find a person better qualified than Mr. 

Geniesse to perform this task. His study stems from a long and valuable career as a Patent Office official which included duties as a primary examiner and as a member of the Board of Appeals, in which latter capacity he was serving at the time of his retirement 312 years ago. This long and observant service is reflected in the collection of data and acute analysis that characterize the present study. 

In publishing this study, it is important to state clearly its relation to the policies and views of this subcommittee. The views expressed by the author are entirely his own. The subcommittee welcomes the report for consideration, but its publication in no way signifies acceptance by the subcommittee of the statements contained

in it. Such publication does, however, testify to the subcommittee's belief that the study represents a valuable contribution to patent and related literature and that the public interest will be served by its publication.

THE EXAMINATION SYSTEM IN THE U.S. 

PATENT OFFICE By Eugene W. Geniesse 

This is a study of the examination system as presently administered, relative to the activities and duties of the Patent Office examiner, the activities of patent attorneys, the problems presented, and some possible remedies.

I. INTRODUCTION 

The U.S. patent system is governed by the Patent Act of 1952 (Title 

35, United States Code), which became effective January 1, 1953.

This act is essentially a codification and restatement, with some minor exceptions, of the law as previously interpreted. 

The system requires the inventor to file an application for a patent, and requires the Patent Office to examine the application to determine whether the subject matter is novel and otherwise complies with the requirements of law, before the patent is issued. The patent as issued, at least in theory, represents inventive subject matter which is adequately described and claimed, and is presumed to be valid (35 U.S.C. sec. 282).

The patent grants the inventor a right to exclude others from using the invention for a period of 17 years, after which time all members of the public may freely use the invention. The 17-year monopoly is therefore the reward received by the inventor for creating something new.

The description of the patent is of significance for two reasons. It must be adequate to describe the subject matter so that the public may be in possession of the invention after the 17-year monopoly has expired. That is the consideration paid by the inventor for his monopoly. It must also be adequate to apprise the public, and especially the inventor's competitors, of the nature of the invention monopolized. 

The claims of the patent define the scope of the monopoly asserted by the inventor, and are therefore the most significant portion of the patent from the inventor's standpoint. The claims state the extent or scope of the subject matter asserted by the inventor, and from which his competitors are to be excluded for the 17 years. The claims thus are the primary subject matter for consideration by the Patent Office in its examination, and also the focal point of controversy in litigation over infringement of the patent. 

The administration of this examination system by the Patent Office has been the subject of various types of criticism. It has been said that too many invalid patents have been issued, that the Office has failed to discover and cite the most pertinent prior art and has failed to maintain adequate standards of invention. Patents have also been 1

criticized because of deficiencies in the description and inadequate definition in the claims. The Office has also been criticized as causing unnecessary delay after the application is filed before grant of the patent. 

These factors are known to all who are familiar with our patent system, but the magnitude and character of the task confronting the Office and the difficulties facing the examiners are not as generally known. It is suggested that criticisms and proposed changes in the examination system may be better understood and discussed if the system as presently administered is well understood. The details of the examination system as presently administered will be described and some of the possible sources of the deficiencies will be pointed out. 

These deficiencies and some of the proposed remedies will then be examined.

This paper will be limited to a discussion of the examination system. Consideration of other systems of reward for inventors, such as a registration system or direct Federal grants, is beyond the scope of this study. The writer firmly believes that the U.S. patent system, as it has been administered in the past, has been a major factor in the industrial development of this country. Modifications are suggested to improve, but not significantly alter, the present system. 

II. THE PATENT OFFICE 

A primary duty of the Patent Office is that of examining applications for patents. To obtain an overall view of this activity the general handling procedure for each application will be described. 

This procedure must be repeated for each application. A review of available statistical data as to such applications shows the magnitude of the task facing the Office. The organization and personnel of the Office, insofar as it relates to patent examining duties, will complete the general picture of examining activities of the Office. 

A. GENERAL PROCEDURE 

The Patent Office (referred to in this paper as the "Office") receives about 70,000 applications for patents annually. The application papers must include a specification (description), a claim or claims, an oath and a drawing if the nature of the invention admits of it (35 U.S.C. secs. 111-115). The Office places these papers in a folder, with identifying data on the face. The back flap serves for the entry of an index of papers as filed. The Application Division prepares duplicate index cards and forwards the application to one of the approximately 70 divisions of the Office, depending on the subject matter. Each of these examining divisions is assigned particular classes of subject matter. There are 12 divisions dealing with chemical subject matter, 7 dealing with electrical inventions, 2 dealing with designs, and the remainder dealing with mechanical patents. Prior patents have been classified into 308 distinct classes, which include 53,567 subclasses. 

This work is maintained by the Classification Division. The primary 

1 For detailed figures, reference may be had to the "Annual Report of the Commissioner of Patents." The figures used in this paper are from the Annual Report of the Commis sioner of Patents for Fiscal Years 1957 and 1958. 

2 See appendix F for illustrative copy of a file.

See appendix A showing the current list of classes of subject matter.

examiner (who is chief of a division), or an examiner delegated to the duty, inspects the application to determine if it is properly assigned to the division, and assigns it to a particular assistant examiner who works on the subject matter of the application on hand. If the primary examiner finds that the application deals with subject matter not assignable to his division, he attempts to obtain the permission of the primary examiner of the appropriate division to transfer the application. Disputes between various divisions are resolved by informal or formal submission to the appropriate classification examiner. It will be appreciated that inventors do not develop subject matter in accordance with previous lines of classification and there are many borderline inventions which defy easy classification, both in patents and in pending applications.

The assistant examiner receiving the application inspects the application, makes such notes as are necessary to conduct any later "interference" search, and places that application in his file of new pending applications to be examined in the order of its filing date. Each assistant examiner receives an average of 70 new applications per year. 

The actual number received by an individual examiner may vary widely from this average, as it is impossible to maintain the workloads of the individual assistants and the divisions uniform. 

On reaching the new application for examination in its proper chronological order, which may be from 6 to 18 or more months after filing, it is examined. Since an application is rarely in completely proper form and completely escapes the prior art, the examiner writes a letter, designated an "action," citing pertinent prior patents and other prior art (foreign patents and publications) and detailing his objections to the application and particularly the claims thereof. The application thereupon becomes a "rejected" application awaiting response by the applicant, usually through his attorney. 

There are currently approximately 100,000 such pending, rejected applications in the Office. With few exceptions, an applicant is permitted 6 months in which he may reply to the examiner's action (35 U.S.C. sec. 133). Failure to reply results in abandonment of the application. A response on the part of the applicant must take up every objection and rejection entered by the examiner, and usually includes an amendment or modification of the claims or description or both and such argument or comments as are appropriate. The application then becomes an "amended" application, awaiting further action by the examiner. The number of such amended applications currently pending is about 55,000. Each assistant examiner thus faces a number of amended applications awaiting consideration at any particular time, and such applications may be reached for further examination in chronological order, at some time within from 6 months to 2 years. As with the new application, it is impossible to maintain the same date in arrears by each examiner and each division. On reaching the amended application in chronological order, the assistant examiner reexamines the application and takes such "action" as in his judgment, after consulting with the primary examiner, the application requires. This may take the form of a further action on the application, raising the same or further rejections or objections, or a notice of allowance, or a "final" rejection. A "final" rejection may be entered only if it is in substance a repetition of a

former action and may not be entered if a new objection or rejection or new prior art is presented. A final disposition of the application is attained by an allowance, a final rejection, or by abandonment. The latter is usually by failure of the applicant-inventor to file a response. Any of these methods of final disposition relieves the examiner of further direct jurisdiction of the application. A final rejection may be met by an appeal to the Board of Appeals (35 U.S.C. secs. 7, 134). An unfavorable decision by the Board of Appeals may be met by an appeal to the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals (35 U.S.C. secs. 141, 142, 143, 144) or by civil action in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia (35 U.S.C. sec. 145). The notice of allowance, which may be issued by the examiner at any time during the prosecution, after compliance with the conditions of a final rejection, or on decision of the Board of Appeals, the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals, or a decree of the district court, entitles the applicant to obtain his patent on the payment of the final fee.

B. TOTAL TASK 

The annual reports of the Commissioner of Patents give statistical information as to the task faced by the Office and the work accomplished. Attention may be directed to the Report for the Fiscal 

Year 1958, pages 6-9. An inventory of pending applications for patent is given on page 6. This table shows approximately 92,000 applications awaiting consideration, and over 108,000 awaiting a response by the inventor. The table bridging pages 7-8 presents a summary of examining activities for the 3 fiscal years 1956, 1957, and 1958, and also an annual average for the previous decade. These figures show that nearly 60% of the applications filed are allowed; the remainder being abandoned during prosecution. The table also shows that about 52% of the allowed cases (a little over 3% of the total number of cases filed) become abandoned by failure to pay the final fee. During the past decade, approximately 56.5% of the applications filed became patents. 

The number of considerations of each application by the examiner averages close to 312. Sample studies of the number of considerations to reach final disposition were made during 1951 to 1953 with the following results.

A specimen work report for the month of February 1959 is presented in appendix B, and a specimen weekly report of the condition of the Office is printed as appendix D. 

The headings in the columns in the work report (App. B) are selfexplanatory. The first column identifies the number of the division and the second column gives the name of the primary examiner. The divisions are placed in groups in accordance with subject matter. (See App. D.) There is a subtotal for each of the groups and a final total at the bottom of the table. Columns 3 and 4 give statistical information as to the number of assistant examiners and the number of hours spent in examining applications. Columns 5 and 6 indicate the number of receipts in each of the divisions. Column 5 shows the number of new applications. For example, Division 6, which is at the head of the list, received 108 new applications for patent during the month of February 1959. Since the total receipts were 407, the Division must also have received responsive amendments in 299 applications. Similarly, columns 7 and 8 show the output of the 

Division for the same period. Column 7 shows that Division 6 examined 156 new applications and a total of 469 applications, which indicates that the Division took some kind of official action on 313 amended applications. Columns 9 and 10 show transfers of applications to other divisions. Columns 11 and 12 show the workload faced by the Division. Referring again to Division 6, there were 731 new applications awaiting consideration by the examiners, and a total of 

1,463 applications awaiting consideration. By subtraction there were 

732 amended applications awaiting consideration by the examiners. 

Column 13 shows the total number of pending applications in each division. Since the total awaiting action by the examiners in Division 6 was 1,463, by subtraction it is apparent that there were 2,500 applications that had been examined and were awaiting response from the inventor.

Inspection of the workload column 12 shows that different divisions face markedly different workloads. Division 42 with a total of 2,112 applications awaiting action by the examiners shows the heaviest

numerical workload. Division 34 with only 586 applications awaiting examination shows numerically the lightest workload. Division 

42 includes 21 assistant examiners and Division 34 only 12 assistant examiners. Each of the examiners in Division 42 faces on the average 100 applications awaiting action, while the assistant examiners of Division 34 have only approximately 50 applications each. It should be observed that these figures do not tell the entire story. The subject matter of the applications differs in complexity and difficulty and requires correspondingly different times for examination. 

Column 14 is headed "No. of P.R.'s." This refers to the number of patentability reports, from one division to another. Where an application properly classifiable and pending in a particular division includes subject matter foreign to that division, it is the practice to submit the application to the appropriate division to obtain a patentability report on such foreign subject matter. 

The columns 15 and 16 show the number of applications finally disposed of, with column 15 showing the number of applications allowed during the period in question and column 16 showing the number of applications that have become abandoned. Columns 17 to 20 show the time of pendency of the application in the division. 

Here again, it will be observed that there is a marked difference between different divisions. In Division 53 an inventor need wait only 

4 months before receiving his first report of examination of a new application and a little over 4 months for consideration of any response. On the other hand, in Division 23 a new application is examined only after 12 months and 23 days and a response in an amended application is considered only after 12 months and 24 days. 

The data in column 21 is merely numerical rank of the divisions with respect to the dates of the applications or amendments. Columns 22 to 27 give average data for groups of divisions only. Column 22 gives the average number of actions per assistant examiner per week for each of these groups of divisions. Column 23 is a corrected average based on the actual number of hours spent by the assistant examiners in the examination of applications, subtracting the time spent on other duties. Column 24 gives the average number of actions on each application to reach final disposition. Group 1, which represents the chemical divisions, required an average of 3.91 actions per application for final disposition. On the other hand, Group IV required only 

3.07 actions on the average to dispose of each application. Obviously, part of the difference in these figures represents differences in difficulties in disposing of the applications. The figure for the Office total, 3.47, shows that the examiners must consider and examine or reexamine each application for a patent about 31½ times to reach final disposition. The figures in column 25 show the average number of applications finally disposed of by each assistant examiner per 40-hour week. 

Column 26 shows the percentage of the actions that are new examinations of applications for patent. It will be observed that 30% of the actions of the examiners in Group I, Chemical Arts, were on new applications, whereas only 27% of the actions in Group IV relating to "Material Handling and Treating, Optics, Railways and Amuse ments Devices" were on new applications. Since, according to this particular worksheet, approximately four actions were required to dispose finally of an application in Group I the figure for actions on

new applications should be nearer to 25% and in Group IV, with an average of three actions per application, the figure should be nearer to 33%. The statistics of this character will vary from month to month and year to year. The last column, No. 27, shows the percentage of allowances in the applications finally disposed of. Here again there is a wide variance in the particular groups, the lowest percentage being for Group IV, with 56% of the final dispositions being allowances. On the other hand, the highest percentage was for Group 

II, with 78% of the final dispositions being in the form of allowances. 

A specimen weekly report of conditions in the Office as of January 

30, 1959, taken from pages 287-288 of the Official Gazette of March 

10, 1959, is reproduced as appendix D. This report is intended primarily for public information and includes some of the information found in appendixes A and B. It is consulted by members of the profession to estimate the time of waiting for examination or reexamination of an application for patent on particular subject matter or in a particular division.

This report shows the organization of the divisions into eight main groups according to classes of subject matter and also shows very briefly the subject matter of each division. The dates of the oldest new and amended applications in each division are given. The date of an amended application is the date of the inventor's response or amendment. These dates, like the corresponding information in appendix B, show wide variations between different divisions. 

It will be evident from these reports that the load of work in the Office is not evenly distributed by divisions, nor as to each assistant in the division. The number of pending applications faced by each examiner is not a complete and adequate measure of the workload nor is the time of pendency prior to consideration of the application an adequate measure. The administrative problem of disparity in workloads is in part met by transfers of arts to different divisions and by the transfer of assistant examiners. It is also met in part by recruitment of new assistant examiners whose training is in the specialized field of a rapidly expanding art. Transfers of art and also of assistant examiners is limited by the high degree of specialization employed in the Office. It would be impractical to attempt to transfer art from the 

Chemical Divisions, Group I, to the Electrical Divisions, Group II. 

Even within a particular group of divisions, there is some difficulty and loss of efficiency, involved in such transfers. For example, Divisions 6, 31, 50, 60, and 63 all relate to organic chemistry (Carbon Chemistry, class 260). Divisions 50 and 60 relate primarily to the important fields of Synthetic Plastics and Synthetic Fibres. Division 31 relates primarily to Petroleum Chemistry and Division 63 to Chemistry related to Natural Fats and Oils and other Naturally Derived Organic Compounds. It is evident that transfer of an assistant examiner who has specialized in Synthetic Plastics from Division 50 to Division 63 would involve some loss in efficiency. Transfers of subject matter would encounter similar difficulties. The impracticability of transferring either subject matter or assistant examiners from a Chemical to an Electrical or Mechanical Division, or the reverse, should be obvious. 

In view of the very high degree of specialization maintained in the Office and the various rates of activity and growth in different classes of subject matter, it seems to the writer that a goal of not more than 90 days in arrears is the best that the Office can hope for.

C. EXAMINING DIVISION ORGANIZATION 

There are currently about 70 examining divisions (including 2 design divisions) in the Office. Each division is assigned particular classes of subject matter of inventions. There are currently approximately 1,000 assistant examiners. Each division includes 10 to 20 assistant examiners and a "primary" examiner who acts as the chief of the division. Appendix B may be consulted for the current number of examiners in each division. The division organization includes the chief or primary examiner, and an assistant primary examiner. The latter acts as primary examiner in the primary examiner's absence and assumes such other supervisory duties as are assigned to him. 

Assistant examiners range all the way from the experienced journeyman to the newly hired, completely inexperienced college graduate. 

Their capabilities may range from the thoroughly capable and competent to the impractical and incompetent. 

The examiners are ordinarily recruited as graduates of engineering or scientific departments of colleges but with no legal training. Most of the new examiners begin the study of law evenings at one of the evening law schools in the District of Columbia. This law school work is completed in 4 years of evening study and leads to an LL.B degree. Ordinarily, an examination is taken leading to admission to the District of Columbia bar or that of some State.

Many examiners, at the time of completion of their law study, leave the Patent Office to work as patent attorneys or patent agents in private practice or with a corporation, as the remuneration is greater than in the Patent Office. The Office loses many assistant examiners as soon as they have become experienced and competent in thir work. The Office thus acts as a training school for patent attorneys and agents. This function of the Office is inescapable and while it is of immediate disadvantage to the progress of work in the Office, it is of long-range benefit to the patent system, as it provides capable practitioners familiar with Patent Office procedure. This advantage provides some compensation for the loss suffered through separation of trained, competent personnel. Those who remain in the 

Office remain for a variety of reasons. These include satisfaction in present occupation, liberal government employment policies with respect to vacation and retirement, reluctance to change, and lack of opportunity for outside employment. Civil service tenure laws and rules facilitate the retention of a relatively minor number of incompetent or otherwise inadequate assistant examiners. Reluctance on the part of supervisory officials to condemn unsatisfactory personnel results in satisfactory ratings in instances where an unsatisfactory rating would be appropriate. The provisions for safeguarding tenure and for presenting appeals from unsatisfactory ratings may degenerate into a comparison of the relative competence of the supervisor and the person involved. Happily, the number of incompetent assistant examiners retained is relatively low, but this factor must be considered in the assessment of Office personnel. 

The primary examiner, who acts as chief of a division, has an education in both law and science equivalent to a bachelor's degree in each of these subjects. He is familiar with, and usually a specialist in, the particular subjects of inventions covered by his division. He has had long experience in the Office and is familiar with the decisions

of the courts, of the Commissioner, and of the Board of Appeals on questions of patentability and procedure. He takes direct responsibility for the actions of the assistant examiners of his division and enters and signs the decisions as to the patentability and matters of form of all applications for patents assigned to his division. 

The figures given in the Annual Report of the Commissioner for 1957 show that the average division included 15 assistant examiners. 

Assuming an even distribution, each division would include four assistants of less than 1 year's experience, four more with at least 1, but not more than 2 years' experience, and six with at least 5 years' experience. These are approximations and the distribution varies from time to time as well as between different divisions. These figures show that, at the time of the report (June 30, 1957), at least half of the assistant examiners were too inexperienced for competent, efficient work without detailed supervision.

There has been some improvement in this factor of experience since the time of the 1957 report. The figures in the fiscal 1957 report reflect the immediate result of the recruitment program instituted in an attempt to bring the Office work up to date. This report shows that there were 480 examiners of more than 2 years' experience and 585 with less than 2 years. At the end of fiscal 1958 (June 30, 1958) there were 761 examiners with over 2 years' experience and 371 with less than that time in the Office. The figures as of December 31, 1958, were 804 with more than 2 years' experience and 305 with less.

D. EFFICIENCY RATINGS AND PROMOTIONS 

The Office is compelled to present efficiency ratings of all civil service employees, which, of course, includes all examiners. It must also maintain such ratings to determine promotions through the various grades of assistant examiners and to select personnel to fill the positions of primary examiners, supervisory examiners, examiner-in-chief on the Board of Appeals, and other responsible positions in the Office. 

Statistics are maintained as to the work of each assistant examiner. 

These statistics include a record of the number of "actions" completed by each assistant. These actions include allowances as well as actions unfavorable to the inventors. In an attempt to evaluate the effectiveness of these "actions" statistically, a record is kept of the ratio of the number of such actions to the number of final dispositions. Final disposition is attained by allowance or by abandonment. The examiner's rating also takes cognizance of the general character and competence of his work and the energy displayed, but it is evident that statistical figures are much more positive and noncontroversial than any other rating basis.

Greater liberality and consequent increase in the number of allowances tends to make the ratio of actions to final disposals lower and thus more favorable. The discovery of more pertinent references, and a clear and persuasive presentation of reasons for refusal, also improves the ratio of disposals. While these figures are concrete and indisputable, such figures are not the only, nor the most important, criteria in the examiner's rating for promotion purposes. The official requirements are set forth in Commissioner's Administrative Order

No. 21 (Amended) effective January 1, 1957, entitled "Patent Office 

63416-61—2

Policy with Respect to Promotions." The entire performance of the assistant examiner must be evaluated. Too great liberality, shown by a disproportionate ratio of allowances to abandonments, or an unduly harsh attitude, having in view the type of art handled, are considered. 

In the writer's experience, the supervisory officials of the Office make every effort to evaluate fairly the work of assistant examiners. 

The Commissioner's Administrative Order No. 21 is in the form of a printed pamphlet of 18 pages giving in detail the factors to be considered and the procedures to be employed for promotions within the Office. Such policy must comply with the provisions of the Classification Act of 1949 and the Federal Personnel Manual, and the general policies of the Department of Commerce. Section 1 (p. 1) includes a general statement reading: (2) Policy.-Operating within this broad delegation of authority, it shall be the policy of the Patent Office to effect. by all practicable means, maximum utilization and development of employee skills and qualifications, and to provide employees with opportunities for advancement to positions for which they are best qualified. The best qualified person available shall be secured for each vacant position. Persons who are not employees of the Patent Office will be considered concurrently with employees from all organization units of the Patent Office and may be appointed to positions above the entrance grade in any line of work when it appears that such action will materially improve the efficiency or quality of services rendered by the Office. Preference shall be given employees in the immediate organization unit whenever candidates are equally qualified. The entire Patent 

Examining Operation shall be regarded as an immediate organization unit when positions are to be filled. Other factors being equal, length of service will govern.

The pamphlet then presents a detailed discussion of the factors to be considered. For promotion to a supervisory position such as chief or assistant chief of a division, a law degree or membership in the bar of any State is required in addition to the scientific education required of an assistant examiner. A minimum time in a previous grade as well as a minimum total professional experience is specified for promotion to each grade. In considering performance, which is a vital factor, the quality of the work as well as productivity are weighed. 

Performance records are to be considered, but they are to be used only with good judgment in evaluating all of the factors involved. In promotions to supervisory positions of the grade of assistant chief of à division or higher, administrative and judicial characteristics as well as personality are to be considered.

A supplement presents a checklist of characteristics desired for supervisory positions.

E. THE PERSONNEL PROBLEM 

The problem of a fully adequate and competent staff is not unique with the Patent Office, but it is perhaps aggravated by the dual type education required, and by the extreme importance of experience. 

Competence in both the legal field and a particular scientific field is

not easily attained. In addition to this dual competence, the division chiefs, who are the key officials of the examination system, must possess fair administrative ability and some judicial capacity. Obviously the Office cannot expect to obtain for all of these positions incumbents who meet ideal qualifications in all respects. 

The writer served, in a number of instances, on panels making recommendations for promotions. The discussion in every instance was of the knowledge, competence, and ability of the possible candidates. At no time was there any discussion of personal views, or of possible disagreement with any member of the panel in matters of practice or law. The search in every instance was for the best available man for the position. In some instances the choice was easy, there being an outstanding man available. In other instances, the choice was less easy as there was no truly outstanding person available. Under such circumstances, the Office, like all other organizations, must make do with the competence it has. 

The promotion policy just described and presently in effect is an improvement over past policies on promotion. Seniority has been at times a controlling factor and some individuals without administrative ability or without judicial temperament have been promoted to division chief solely because they lived long enough and had a satisfactory record. Shortly after the First World War questions of "efficiency" became important. With the Office in arrears and with the recruitment of many new assistant examiners, various efforts were made to bring the Office more nearly up to date. There is no doubt that some promotions to chief or assistant chief of a division took place primarily because of quantity of work performed without much regard to quality and even less regard to possible administrative or judicial ability.

The average tenure as primary examiner is relatively brief, possibly 10 to 12 years, so that replacement must be found at relatively frequent intervals. It is not surprising that the writer, looking back over the years, can remember several appointments which should not have been made. All were at least good as assistant examiners, but the mistake was made in assuming that a good workman was also necessarily a good administrator and good judge. One remembers a division chief who was cranky and erratic, another who was thoroughly competent, but lazy and tending to be liberal for that reason, and a third who disliked controversies to such an extreme extent that he rarely entered a final adverse decision. Another one had a profound scientific knowledge but no facility in applying the simplest legal principles.

These appointments were the exceptions among the large number of primary examiners (well over a hundred) that the writer became acquainted with during his service in the Office. In contrast to such exceptions, one also remembers the many able and conscientious men who devoted not only their working hours but uncounted additional hours to a capable and fair administration of their duties and a proper determination of every question presented. The wonder is that the 

Office does so well in its selections for these important tasks where the primary reward for outstanding ability is a feeling of having done a task properly. Real competence is rare in each of the activities of life, still rarer where several of these fields must be combined.

It is fortunate that the Office has found so many competent lawyersscientists-judges and not surprising that some few of them have failed to be competent administrators at the same time. 

III. THE EXAMINATION PROCESS A. EXAMINATION OF A NEW APPLICATION 

When the assistant examiner to whom an application has been assigned reaches the application in chronological order, he first studies the application itself to determine what is disclosed. He notes any informalities or inadequacies in the papers. He then studies the claims to determine what is sought to be protected. The claim purports to be an exact definition of the asserted invention, and indicates by the terms used the subject matter and scope of the invention to be included in the patent.

His study may develop certain preliminary inadequacies or other obstacles to complete examination: (a) The subject matter may be improperly classified, in which case he seeks to transfer the application to another division. (b) The specifications and claims may be so completely inadequate that the application cannot be properly examined and the patent must be refused for that reason. (c) The claims may cover a plurality of distinct inventions, and a requirement for restriction to a single invention is in order (35 U.S.C. sec. 121). In that event the examiner requires restriction to a single invention, citing patents illustrative of the several classes of inventions involved, prior to consideration of the merits.

B. SEARCH OF PRIOR ART 

After the study of the application, the assistant examiner makes his search of the prior art. This includes the U.S. patents in the subclass or subclasses deemed significant for the claimed subject matter. Familiarity with the patents in the particular subclasses is essential in making this search. Such familiarity comes with experience in that particular field. A search of the foreign patents in the same field, and which are ordinarily placed in the same subclass as the U.S. patents on the same subject matter, follows the search in the U.S. patents. Since a great many inventors take out patents on the same invention in many of the leading industrial countries, search of the foreign patents usually involves some degree of duplication of search. A search in significant periodicals and treatises usually completes the prior art search. 

A search of the prior art involves distinct steps. The first is the determination of the possible field or fields of search. The second step is isolation or separation of possibly pertinent items and the final step involves a detailed study of the isolated items to determine their significance and relationship to the subject matter of the claims of the application under consideration.

As shown in appendix A, particular classes of subject matter are assigned to each of the divisions. Each class is divided into a series of subclasses, arranged in groups and subgroups, following established principles and rules of classification. A determination of the

classification of the invention being examined determines the primary field of search in the prior patents in the same subclass. The organization of the class, and the definitions and search notes of that organization, determines the possible secondary fields of search in the 

U.S. patents. Copies of British, French, German, Austrian, Swiss,

Danish, Norwegian, and Swedish patents are placed in the same subclasses as the U.S. patents on the same subject matter in the examining division.

Examiners are not in general multilingual, and foreign language patents, as well as other foreign language references, present a difficulty. As the primary object of the search is the isolation of possibly pertinent references, the difficulty is not as great as might be supposed. In mechanical fields of invention, the drawings are relied upon to isolate possibly pertinent references. In the nonmechanical fields-i.e., chemical, medicinal, etc.-the scientific names are the same or at least recognizably similar in French and German, so as to enable the examiner to isolate references for further study. Abstracts of foreign language patents are often attached to the patent but this practice is not universal, due to the immensity of the task and the pressure of work. Informal notes and reference to corresponding U.S. patents are also used.

The Office relies on specialization of divisions and also of the assistants in the division for the search. Referring to appendix A, it will be evident that each division is concerned with a limited number of classes which are generally closely related. Division 7, with currently 12 assistant examiners, is confined solely to inventions relating to optics. Division 9, with 14 assistant examiners, has the 3 closely related classes of "Pumps," "Gas Pumps and Fans," and "Fluid Motors."

Further specialization is used in each division. Again referring to Division 7, confined to Class 88, Optics, this class is subdivided into 131 subclasses which fall into 22 main groups. The organization of Class 88 is reproduced in appendix C. The main group subclasses are printed in boldface type and subordinate subclasses indented thereunder. It will be noted that certain of the main group subclasses are isolated and others contain a large number of subordinate subclasses. There were, in February 1959, 12 assistant examiners in this division. Two of these assistants were hired in June 

1957 and three more came into the Office in the middle of 1956. A sixth assistant had been in the Office a little over 5 years, and the other six assistant examiners in the division have been in the Office more than 10 years. This is a rather high proportion of experienced assistants, and suggests that the class of optics is not a rapidly expanding art. Divisions dealing with more rapidly expanding arts, such as organic chemistry and electronics, have a much smaller proportion of experienced assistants. Subclasses 29 to 40 are assigned to a single assistant examiner. Subclasses 2.2 to 2.7, geometrical instruments, are assigned to another assistant examiner. Subclass 14, testing instruments, is presently very active and requires the full time of five assistant examiners. This subclass should be further subdivided or reclassified. Subclasses 14.1 to 14.92, indented under main 

Subclass 14, are devoted to "Egg Candling," a relatively inactive art which requires only a part of the time of a single assistant examiner.

Subclasses 41 to 55 are devoted to eyeglasses and spectacles, and represent relatively simple inventions. Applications in this group of subclasses are sometimes used for the initial training of inexperienced assistant examiners.

The assistant examiner is expected to familiarize himself with the developments in the restricted field assigned to him and to acquire knowledge of the location of patents and other publications in this restricted field.

The extent of search of the prior art involves matters of judgment on the part of the examiner. This judgment can only come with experience. The competent examiner invariably feels that the search is inadequate if nothing related to the application has been discovered. 

On the other hand, the Office cannot undertake what has become known as a “validity search." In the "validity search" on a granted patent, an alleged infringer may undertake a search of several weeks' duration, including an inspection of every possible publication, however remote, from any country in the world, at the cost of many thousands of dollars. This the assistant examiner cannot do. Having due regard to the press of work and other applications awaiting examination, he must limit his search to what his judgment and experience suggest might be significant fields.

The extent and adequacy of search of the prior art is dependent on a number of factors including the following: (a) Experience of the examiner.-This factor requires no discussion other than the obvious comment than anyone familiar with the prior developments can far more quickly uncover pertinent references than can anyone who has no previous familiarity with the field. (b) Competence of the examiner. This factor involves capability of recognizing relationships between the invention claimed and prior developments. (c) Adequacy of classification. The advantages of adequate classification of all prior patents, both foreign and domestic, are obvious. 

The difficulties as well as the efforts involved are not so obvious. Some fields of art are more readily classifiable than others. For example, organic compounds (carbon compounds) may be readily classified on the basis of the number of carbon atoms, or the presence of functional groups, or the character of the nucleus. A German compendium, "Beilstein," listed all of the then known organic compounds. The 

American Chemical Society publishes abstracts with appropriate annual and decennial indexes of organic compounds. This, together with the excellent classification of organic compounds in Office Class 260, makes determination of the novelty of a given compound a relatively simple matter, if it be assumed that the searcher has an adequate knowledge of the fundamentals of organic chemistry and its nomenclature and a reading knowledge of German. 

As a different illustration, polishing compositions may include characteristics and functions of cleaning compositions, coating compositions, metal-plating compositions, and abrasive compositions. Such compositions might be classified on the basis of the components thereof, giving precedence to any of the several functions possible that are present, or on the basis of the type of article to be treated. 

Classification of such compositions is difficult, and any scheme of classification has its defects.

The increase in complexity and volume of prior art developments and the consequent increase in difficulty of both classification and search may be illustrated by the growth of knowledge of organic compounds. Chamberlain's textbook of "Organic Chemistry" (3d ed., revised, Blakiston, Philadelphia, 1934) includes the following statement:

The magnitude of the number of organic compounds will be realized when we state that in Richter's "Lexikon der Kohlenstoffverbindungen," 3d ed. (1910) and Stelzner's "Literatur-Register," (1911-21) there are about 200,000 known compounds. At the present time (1934) it is estimated that in the new 4th ed., Beilstein's "Handbuch der organischen Chemie" there will probably be between 250,000 and 300,000 compounds.

In answering a question as to the number of presently known organic compounds, Dr. Leonard T. Capell, Nomenclature Director and Executive Consultant for "The Chemical Abstracts Service," quoted Dr. Crane, editor of "The Little C.A.," as follows: 

As you will note, I reported that Chemical Abstracts around 1950 was announcing at least 30,000 new compounds each year. I would say that now this figure should be someplace between 40,000 and 50,000 new compounds. Perhaps a proper rough estimate would be that at least 200,000 organic compounds have been announced since that 600,000 estimate was made.

Dr. Capell added, "It would seem that a good guesstimate would be better between 800,000 and 1,000,000 chemical compounds." This letter was dated December 4, 1958. In a letter dated December 15, 1959, Dr. Capell increased the estimate to 1 million, but included 

50,000 inorganic compounds.

A different estimate was presented in a proceeding before the Federal Trade Commission involving the tetracycline-type antibiotics, In re American Cyanamid Co. et al, F.T.C. Docket No. 7211. Prof. 

Robert Burns Woodward, Harvard University, testifying on behalf of Pfizer Co. (one of the respondents) as to the chemical complexities involved in the analysis of mixtures containing tetracycline, stated in part "there may be, there are now known something like one and a half million organic substances, ***"

The above comments are illustrative of the problem of classification. Proper classification requires knowledge of principles of classification as well as a reasonably extensive knowledge of the art to be classified. In meeting this problem, the Office is faced with a dilemma. Examiners experienced in the art to be classified must be taken from the regular work of examining applications, trained in the principles of classification, and then assigned to classification work. The examining division loses the services of an experienced assistant. On the other hand, failure to keep the classification of patents and other technical literature current, especially in rapidly expanding arts, renders the examiner's work more difficult and time consuming, and also less certain. The task of classifying patents is a part of the larger and more formidable task of gathering and classifying all scientific information, including translating foreign lan-

guage publications. Discussion of this tremendous task is beyond the scope of this paper.

In partial avoidance of the above-mentioned dilemma, the work of examining applications classified in arts undergoing extensive reclassification is carried on by a classification divison. There is currently a group of five such divisions having combined duties of reclassifying particular arts and examining the applications currently pending in such arts. Current subject matter thus undergoing reclassification is shown in appendix A. Assistant examiners having specialized knowledge of the art are transferred from the regular examining division to a classification division, with the transfer of the class of subject matter. The current status of examining work of these classification divisions is shown in appendix B. The specialized knowledge of the examiner familiar with the particular art is thus available to the classifiers. This is a relatively recent development. (d) Pressure of work.-Confronted with an accumulation of applications awaiting examination and the necessity of disposing of at least a reasonable number of such applications, the examiner necessarily limits his search to the minimum that he considers satisfactory. 

It is impossible to evaluate exactly the adequacy of search in a given application, but it is always easy to count the number of applications examined in a given period of time. In the rare instances where one discovers a completely anticipating prior art reference, the search is complete, and more would be a waste of time. The difficulty of determining adequacy of search comes in the usual cases where only art related in some degree to the invention claimed has been found. 

The examiner's competence and judgment in analyzing the subject matter of the invention, and its possible relationship to prior development, determines the extent and quality of the investigation of the prior art.

The assistant is required to record the fields of search on the front flap of the file wrapper.

C. EXAMINER'S PRELIMINARY DECISION 

After completion of his study of the application and his search of the prior art, the assistant examiner prepares his first "Action" on the application. A copy is sent to the inventor-applicant, usually through the patent attorney or agent, the original remaining in the Office file. 

The action is signed by the primary examiner. The primary examiner supervises the preparation of the decisions (actions) in each application in his division, and by signing such actions takes responsibility for them. He also supervises the assistant examiner's work in studying the application and making the search. The extent of supervision is dependent on the capability and experience of the particular assistant.

It should be evident that the quality of the first action and also all other actions in connection with the processing of an application for a patent are dependent upon the capabilities of the assistant examiner. 

Such capabilities come primarily through experience. Such experience is necessary to obtain a specialized knowledge of the art and also to become familiar with the sources of such knowledge, that is, the prior publications in the particular field. This specialized knowledge

f is necessary to understand the subject matter of the application and also to understand prior art developments. With experience, the examiner acquires ability to read and understand the scope of each of the definitions of the individual claims presented. Such experience also enables the assistant examiner to acquire knowledge of the patent statutes, the rules of procedure, and general patent practice. The retention of experienced and capable personnel should therefore be a prime object of any personnel policy in the Office. 

It is impossible for a single primary examiner to review all of the details of the work performed by these inexperienced assistants. The primary examiner delegates some of his supervisory duties to his more experienced and competent assistants and does the best he can within the time available to supervise generally such work and maintain the highest possible level of competence thereof.

It should be evident that the character of the work done in the Office, in addition to being dependent on the competence and experience of the assistant examiners available, is dependent on the competence and character of the primary examiner of the division. His knowledge of the subject matter, his knowledge and application of the law, his judgment of the character and competence of his assistants, and his ability to enlist cooperation from his assistants determines the character of the work performed in his division. His decision to allow an application for patent is not directly reviewable and, indirectly, is rarely reviewed by way of a court test of validity of the resulting patent. His decision to refuse a patent may be reviewed by appeal, but such review is sought only in a relatively small number of cases, averaging less than 10% of the total number of applications for patent filed. It should be evident from what has been said that the competence and skill of the primary examiners is of the highest importance to the operation of the Office, and that the retention of competent assistant examiners is essential both to assure competent examination and to provide trained personnel for primary examiner 

D. FIRST ACTION 

The first action is in the nature of a preliminary decision. Any informalities in the papers are noted, and appropriate correction is required. Any objections as to the language or arrangement of the claims or descriptions are also noted, as are also any deficiencies in the drawings. If the deficiencies of the description or drawing go to the substance and are such as to prevent the issuance of a valid patent, all of the claims presented are refused (rejected) for this reason. 

The examiner devotes his principal attention to the claims and their relationship to the prior art references discovered in his search. Here, the examiner may be confronted with up to 20 or even more distinct definitions (claims) of the subject matter, wherein the counsel for the inventor has exercised his highest skill or ingenuity. Each claim must be separately studied, and the examiner must enter a decision, refusing or allowing each such claim. The refusal must take the form of a rejection of the enumerated claim or claims as "anticipated by" or "unpatentable over" particular identified reference patents or publications and the reason therefor must be given (35 U.S.C. secs. 

131, 132). The examiner is guided in this work by the "Manual of 

Patent Examining Procedure," a looseleaf manual prepared by the

Office and kept current by appropriate revisions. A checking and initialing of the typewritten action completes the assistant examiner's work in examination of a "new" application. The primary examiner, having reviewed the action either after typing or while in manuscript form, adds his signature.

As already stated, the inventor has 6 months to respond to this initial action on the part of the examiner. This may take the form of insistence on allowance in unchanged form, accompanied by appropriate reasons. Or the inventor may modify (amend) the description or claims or both. While the inventor is confined to subject matter present in the application as filed, he may, by way of amendment, so alter or modify the claims as to include more detailed elements or emphasize facets of the invention not previously emphasized. The application thus altered becomes an amended application, awaiting further examination. It is placed in the examiners' files, awaiting this further examination in chronological order. This waiting time may vary from a few months up to 2 years, depending on the condition of work in the division. At the end of fiscal year 1957, the average time was 5 to 6 months.

Appendix B shows the current (February 1959) waiting periods in various divisions. The Commissioner's annual reports present summarized statistical information as to applications filed, actions by the examiners, allowances, abandonments, etc.

E. REEXAMINATION 

When the amended application is reached for reexamination, the assistant examiner must review the application, the first action, and the inventor's response. If the examiner is the same individual who first examined the application, and he has a good memory, a rapid review of the application and the previous action serves to familiarize him with the case. A different examiner must go over the ground covered by his predecessor. Under current conditions, the time elapsed since the initial examination may be at least a year. The large turnover of personnel and the necessities of changing assignments often require time-consuming assignment of the amended application to a different examiner.

On reexamination of the amended application, the examiner is again confronted with the problem of extent of search. The alterations may be such as to necessitate a new search in fields other than those formerly covered. The different or possibly more experienced original examiner may be aware of significant fields of search not formerly covered. Here, as in the first examination, the question of extent of further search must be determined by judgment based on experience. On completion of his study of the file and the references, and of any further search, the assistant examiner prepares a second action, under the primary examiner's supervision, much as in the first examination. The inventor must have an opportunity to respond to this action, and he is permitted 6 months to make such response.

F. FINAL DISPOSITION 

The process of examination is repeated until the application is finally disposed of. Final disposition may be reached by allowance of the application, which may be, but rarely is, on the initial examination, or it may be on receiving the inventor's first or a subsequent 

Secondly, final disposition may also be reached by the inventor's failure to file a response. This failure may be for a variety of reasons. The inventor may be confronted with prior art (patents or publications) or other reasons for refusal which convince him of the futility of proceeding further. Practical considerations may prompt him to abandon the application. Deficiencies in the description or subsequent discoveries may cause him to file a substitute or amplified application.

Lastly, the final disposition (insofar as the examining division is concerned) may take the form of a "final rejection" which is a final decision on the part of the examiner, refusing, in whole or in part, the claims presented in the application. From this final action by the examiner, the inventor may appeal to the Board of Appeals and obtain a review of the examiner's decision refusing such claims as have been rejected. He may also petition from any final action or requirement relating to the form. In the event of appeal or petition, the examiner is required to state his reasons for such action. The position of the examiner is presented in the form of a written "Examiner's Answer" to the brief filed by the inventor. This paper, which becomes part of the record, must include an adequate explanation of the invention and of the references cited, based on the examiner's specialized knowledge of the field. It must also include an exposition of the reasons for refusal of each of the claims on appeal. Appropriate comments as to the contents of the inventor-appellant's briefs are required. The inventor is accorded a half-hour oral hearing. The examiner is not present at the hearing. An unfavorable decision by the Board of Appeals is subject to further review by way of appeal to the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals (35 U.S.C. 

secs. 141, 142, 143, 144) or by way of a civil action in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia (35 U.S.C. sec. 145). The inventor may, of course, accept the examiner's final decision. In that event, if there has been a refusal of only a part, but not all of the claims, the inventor may remove the refused claims and obtain an allowance of the patent application with the remaining claims. 

If all claims have been refused, agreement with the examiner's position leaves no other recourse but abandonment of the application. 

The examiner's "final" action in theory completes the prosecution of the application before the examiner, but a practice has developed of permitting further attempts to persuade the examiner to adopt a more favorable view, with or without further alteration of the claims and possibly the presentation of further evidence. There has grown up a doctrine that it is never too late to allow an application for a patent. 

A persistent inventor or persistent or contentious counsel will not forego this last possible opportunity to persuade the examiner to a more favorable view.

In such further attempts to prosecute after final action, the inventor seeks to improve his position. This may be by significant restriction of the claims so as to escape the rejections. Factual evidence, in the form of affidavits which purport to establish advantages of the invention or disadvantages of the references, may be offered. 

Frequently an oral conference (interview) is held with the examiner. 

The substance of the interview, including the contentions advanced and any conclusions reached, must be reported by the inventor (Rule No. 133). All of these papers (proposed amendments, arguments, affidavits, reports of interviews) become a part of the record without regard to the results.

G. ALLOWANCE 

At any time during prosecution the examiner may decide to allow the application. He is compelled to take the same step following a favorable decision of the Board of Appeals or of a court. This step of "allowance" takes the form of a notice to the inventor-applicant that the application is in form and substance ready for issuance of a patent and awaits only the payment of the final fee. 

The allowance is less time consuming than any refusal. The examiner need not explain his decision, and it is not subject to review. 

He need only review his records to see if any other inventor has presented an application on the same invention, and if none is found he makes such formal and editorial corrections as seem proper, and issues a formal notice of allowance. 

On discovering that another application on the same subject matter has been presented, the examiner takes such formal steps as are necessary to institute an "interference" procedure to determine which applicant is the true first inventor and then transfers the application to the jurisdiction of the Interference Division. 

It will be seen from this review of the examination process that the proceeding is entirely ex parte and that all pressures are in favor of allowance of the application. Only the examiner's judgment and conscientious devotion to duty prevent the issuance of a larger number of invalid patents.

The Commissioner's Annual Report for 1958, page 7, gives examining activity figures showing annual averages for the preceding decade. These figures show the magnitude of the task facing the Office. 

An average of 71,000 applications are filed annually. The Office disposed of 73,386 applications annually, 43,910 or roughly 60% by allowance, and the remainder (40%) by abandonment. 39,838 patents were issued annually. The discrepancies in the figures are accounted for by differing inventories of pending applications at the beginning and end of the decade.

At the present time, the average number of actions required to reach final disposition is about 31%. While this figure is not constant, the examining force as a whole prepares something like 240,000 formal "actions" annually. These figures include allowances and rejections, as well as other formal papers.

S it

H. PETITIONS 

Petitions may be filed from any unfavorable ruling of the examiner on formal matters (Rules 113, 127, 181, 182, 183). While these petitions are in theory restricted to formal matters, there is an inclination on the part of a small minority of overly contentious attorneys to employ such petitions as a weapon to force the examiner to take a more favorable action in the case. This is a field in which the old axiom that "hard cases make bad law" is applicable. In actual practice most petitions are decided by the supervisory examiner. The latter, confronted with what is to him a harsh ruling in an isolated case, tends to make his interpretation of the rules as liberal as possible in favor of the inventor. The conscientious examiner confronted with such a ruling, perhaps attained by an overly contentious attorney, takes an equally favorable position with respect to all subsequent cases.

The underlying difficulty here is based on the fact that the inventor's interests are at stake, and the Office is reluctant to penalize the inventor by reason of the mistakes made by his attorney. If relief is accorded for one inventor under a particular set of circumstances, other inventors under like circumstances are entitled to the same relief, under the doctrine of stare decisis.

I. APPEALS 

Appeal decisions by the Board of Appeals or the courts which reverse the examiners' decisions also tend to have the same effect of forcing the allowance of applications. Only the examiner's unfavorable rulings are reviewed. As a general rule, only those decisions of the Board of Appeals overruling the examiner in whole or in part become available to the public, since only such decisions result in patents. Many of these decisions are published in the United States 

Patents Quarterly. Those applications in which the Board affirms the examiner's rejections in most instances become abandoned and are thus preserved in secrecy. The individual examiner sees only such affirming decisions as originated in his division and are returned thereto, but Board decisions reversing the examiners are generally distributed throughout the Office. Even those decisions of the Board sustaining the examiner, of which the latter is aware, are not ordinarily available for citation, since the Administrative Procedure Act 

(Title 5, U.S.C. sec. 1002b) forbids the citing as precedents of deci sions which are "required for good cause to be held confidential." It will thus be apparent that the available decisions of the Board of Appeals present a one-sided picture, being generally in favor of allowance, and reviewing only examiners' decisions refusing allowance. 

This situation does not obtain, with respect to decisions of the courts under 35 U.S.C. secs. 141 and 145. In these cases, the record and decision are available to the public, regardless of whether the result is favorable or unfavorable to the inventor. There is here one minor exception to the general rule that only decisions unfavorable to the inventor are reviewed. The decision by the district court (35

U.S.C. sec. 145), being a civil action against the Commissioner, may be appealed to the court of appeals and this appeal is available to the Commissioner if the district court's decision was one ordering the grant of a patent. This right of appeal has been rarely exercised by the Office, and then only in unusual situations. 

J. EVIDENCE 

The examiner may cite and rely on textbooks and treatises to establish facts. The inventor may, of course, also rely on such textbooks, etc., as establishing facts. The usual factual disputes developed in the processing of a patent application involve questions of the comparative advantages of the asserted invention and prior art developments. These disputes are often resolved by the presentation of evidence. The inventor may present exhibits which must be adequately identified and described in a paper placed in the file. Evidence is usually presented in the form of ex parte affidavits which become a part of the record. In re McKenna, 203 F. 2d 717, 40 C.C.P.A. 937, 97 U.S.P.Q. 348, 350 (1953), includes the following comment upon such evidence:

Although there are certain obvious shortcomings in ex parte affidavits as proof, it seems to us that such affidavits are clearly one of the few practical methods of presenting a factual record sufficient to form a basis for proper application of the "history of the art test" in this type of ex parte proceeding. Indeed, in some cases this court has reversed the Patent Office, relying considerably on affidavits presenting facts which convinced us that the invention of the application had some meritorious advantage over the prior art. 

In re Robert Burgess, Jr., 35 C.C.P.A. (Patents) 1076, 167 

F.2d 637, 77 U.S.P.Q. 373; In re Holt, supra (34 C.C.P.A. 

1129, 74 U.S.P.Q. 155). Such ex parte affidavits must, of course, be closely scrutinized and weighed with care, it being kept in mind that they may unconsciously and unintentionally be colored as a result of enthusiasm for the subject matter of the application. We think also that an affidavit by an applicant or co-applicant as to the advantages of his invention is less persuasive than one made by a disinterested person. (See Ex parte Coleman, 29 U.S.P.Q. 378, Board of 

Appeals). However, it is not to be disregarded for that reason alone and may be relied on when sufficiently convincing. See In re Holt, supra, and In re Mock, 28 C.C.P.A. (Patents) 919, 117 F. 2d 745, 48 U.S.P.Q. 545, wherein this court relied on such affidavits in reversing the Board of Appeals.

IV. THE PATENT PROFESSION 

The examination system requires the filing of an application for patent and also the preparation of appropriate responses to each action by the examiner. The preparation of the original application and of the necessary responses is ordinarily done by members of the patent profession. The role of the latter, particularly as it relates to the work of the Office, will be briefly considered.

19 An inventor applying for a patent is usually represented by an attorney or agent who prepares the application and all necessary responses to the examiner's actions. The Office maintains a roster of persons who may represent inventor-applicants before the Office (35) 

U.S.C. secs. 31, 32 and 33; Rules 341-348). To qualify for practice before the Office, a person must pass an examination showing familiarity with patent law and procedure. Former examiners having at least 3 years' Office experience are excused from taking the examination. Those who are attorneys-at-law are placed on the register of "attorneys"; others, otherwise qualified, are placed on the register of "agents." The usual patent attorney or agent has a scientific training, plus special patent training either as an examiner or with the patent department of some corporation or a practicing patent attorney. As has already been suggested, the Office acts as a training school for the patent profession. Insofar as this training contributes to a skilled patent profession, it is a valuable function of the Office. 

In any event, the Office cannot avoid this function. 

A. DUTIES OF PATENT ATTORNEY 

As in all other branches of the law, it is the duty of a patent attorney or agent to represent his client in an ethical manner, but it must not be forgotten that he is necessarily an advocate for his inventor-client and presents and urges the most favorable aspects of his client's case. Since the proceedings are ex parte, the representations are generally one-sided, looking toward the allowance of the application. There are, in the patent profession, some individuals who are overly eager, overly energetic, or overly contentious, as well as more balanced and more reasonable individuals. Inventors are invariably overenthusiastic concerning the significance and value of their inventions. These factors tend in the direction of an overly favorable presentation of the invention for which a patent is sought. 

B. PATENT DIVISIONS OF CORPORATIONS 

As shown in this subcommittee's Study No. 3, "Distribution of Patents Issued to Corporations (1939-55)," somewhat over half of the total patents issued are issued to corporations. There is a similar proportion of pending applications controlled by such corporations. 

Corporations that have significant research organizations usually maintain a patent division to prepare and prosecute applications for patents, and to administer or exploit patents owned by the corporation. These corporate patent divisions encounter problems similar to those encountered by the Office in creating and maintaining a competent staff. The range of compensation, while generally higher than that of the Office, must be, to some extent at least, alined with that of other professional employees on the corporate staff. The personnel may be recruited to a limited extent from Patent Office assistant examiners, but that source is insufficient to supply the needs. 

Corporations having large patent divisions often train their own personnel. Scientifically trained persons from the corporation staff are transferred to the patent division, where they obtain experience by working under the direction of more experienced patent attorneys or agents. Some of these individuals attend evening law schools to

obtain their legal education. In the meantime these inexperienced individuals necessarily carry a part of the burden of work confronting the corporate patent division. As with the Patent Office, the corporate patent division must evaluate the work of each individual, and while quality of work is considered, the number, obtained by counting the applications prepared, responsive amendments filed, and patents obtained, are the most positive and least disputable criteria of production. The writer has no direct personal knowledge of the operation of corporate patent divisions, but he is reliably informed that, in some instances, such production criteria tend to militate against the highest quality of performance. 

C. CORPORATE POLICIES 

The writer's experience in chemical patent examining divisions made him familiar with the general character of the work presented by most corporations in this field. From this experience it was found that corporation patent departments, like individuals, presented work which varied widely in competence but was to a large extent characteristic for a particular corporation. The general characteristics of the work of three of these corporation patent divisions are here described as illustrative of the extremes encountered. 

The work of the patent division of the first corporation was of the highest degree of excellence. The descriptions were clear and adequate, with adequately detailed examples and appropriate explanatory subject matter, and the claims were modest in scope and adequately covered the subject matter disclosed. Such descriptions were prepared in the light of a thorough knowledge of prior developments in the field. Responses to the examiner's actions were competent, dealt directly with the examiner's objections, and generally advanced the prosecution toward allowance or other final disposition. The number of appeals filed were relatively low and generally raised only significant and proper issues. An outstanding characteristic of each description was the presence of detailed examples setting forth the components used, together with amounts and conditions employed and a statement of particular results attained.

The work of the patent division of a second corporation showed generally a lack of competence or a lack of adequate time and effort. 

The descriptions of the application were often found to be insufficient in detail as to examples, explanatory subject matter, and statements of result so as to necessitate refiling of a substitute application. 

Claims often showed evidence of hasty preparation. Prosecution often showed inadequate knowledge of commonly accepted principles of patent law. Appeals were much too frequent and often no significant issues were presented.

The patent division of the third corporation showed a policy of attempting to obtain inordinately broad coverage in their patents. 

The descriptions, while more competent than those of the second corporation, usually included ambiguous or qualified general statements of successively broader scope which could be modified as the exigencies of prosecution and prior art required. Prosecution was ordinarily in the same vein, never conceding or yielding to the examiner's position any more than was absolutely required to meet the

Do 0: lu er ta 0: te situation. Appeals and petitions were employed wherever possible to attain more favorable action.

The above general descriptions of particular corporate patent departments is not to be taken as suggesting that all applications of the particular corporation partook of all of these qualities, but it was surprising to find how uniform the work of a particular corporation appeared to be. The above comments are illustrative of extremes, the work of most corporations falling in a middle ground between the extremes indicated. The time and effort involved for a proper examination and disposition of applications of the type presented by the second and third corporation is much greater than required for applications of the first described corporation. 

D. PRIVATE PRACTICE
