The work of an attorney in private practice also partakes of the qualities and competence of the particular individual. In dealing with any particular application for a patent, the examiner cannot take notice of the source of the application or the competence or character of the individual attorney or agent. It must be evident, however, that incompetent work, as well as overly contentious work, entails more effort on the part of the examiner than does more competent and less contentious work on the part of counsel. Examiners are required to facilitate the grant of patents wherever possible, and incompetent counsel are the beneficiaries of this requirement.

The examiner must regard himself as a public official charged with the duty of granting patents in all proper cases. He must assume that the application and all responses are presented in good faith. 

The public interest and private interest of the inventor are paramount to any interest of an inventor's representative. Incompetent counsel often fails to provide responses of a type that will quickly develop mutual understanding or clear-cut issues, and this forces the examiner to provide a further exposition of his views. The problem of developing agreement on clear-cut issues is a mutual one shared by the examiner and by counsel. It may be suggested that the ethical code of the legal profession might require a higher standard of conduct (especially in telling the whole story) in the ex parte presentation of an application than might be required in an inter partes proceeding where there is no burden on an advocate to present his opponent's case. Furthermore, the vast majority of practicing attorneys do not want a patent to issue that will not stand the test of validity in court. For instance, it is not unusual for an attorney to suggest possible references or other obstacles overlooked by the examiner--and, of course, the steps he has taken in avoidance of such references. Apart from concern to obtain a valid patent, however, attorneys are eager to obtain adequate scope of protection so that their clients are protected not only from exact imitations, but also from those competitors who would copy the substance but not the exact form of the invention. It is in this field, which involves the scope of definitions of the claims, that most of the controversy with the examiner is found. 63416-61- 3

Attorneys, like their inventor-clients, are often unduly enthusiastic about the merits of the invention presented. The writer well remembers his skepticism as an assistant examiner, as well as that of his chief (the late C. L. Blake, Division 50) when a skein of silk-like yarn was placed on the desk by an attorney (the late Ralph Miller, Dupont attorney) with the assertion that the new material for which he was seeking patents would displace the Japanese silk industry. 

The applications became basic patents on the then unknown "nylon. 

There were, of course, many other instances of optimistic assertions, none with the dramatic success of nylon and many that were never heard of again. Overenthusiasm seems to be an occupational affliction of inventors, and this disease is often communicated to their representatives. 974 In the writer's opinion, the members of the profession in general possess a very high degree of competence and display such competence, as well as proper ethical conduct, in their presentation of appli cations before the Office, as well as in their presentation of cases before the courts. It must not be forgotten, however, that the prose cution of applications for patents before the Office is an entirely ex parte proceeding. There is no advocate for the general public interest. The examiner must act as a court of original jurisdiction and at the same time protect the public interest against the grant of invalid patents as well as patents of unduly broad scope. Since there is an unbalanced, continuous pressure in the direction of allowance of patents, and substantially no pressure in the other direction, it is astonishing that the work of the Office is as impartial and well done as it is.

The question of ethics of members of the Patent Office bar was considered in Kingsland v. Dorsey, 338 U.S. 318, 83 U.S.P.Q. 330 (1949). In that case the Commissioner of Patents, acting under section 487 R.S. (now 35 U.S.C. sec. 32), entered an order barring an attorney from further practice before the Office. The attorney had misrepresented the authorship of a technical publication used as evidence in securing a patent. The district court sustained the Commissioner's order, 69 F. Supp. 788, 72 U.S.P.Q. 405 (Dist. Col. 1947), but the court of appeals reversed, 173 F. 2d 405, 80 U.S.P.Q. 186 (D.C. 

Cir. 1949).

The Supreme Court reversed the court of appeals, quoting with express approval from the Patent Office proceedings as follows: 

By reason of the nature of an application for patent, the relationship of attorneys to the Patent Office requires the highest degree of candor and good faith. In its relation to applicants, the Office *** must rely upon their integrity and deal with them in a spirit of trust and confidence. 

V. FACTORS CONTRIBUTING TO ALLOWANCE 

The very nature of ex parte presentation tends to favor the inventor. Continued reiteration of views favorable to the inventor in the repeated presentations permitted by Office practice has an effect. 

4 Five basic patents, all in the name of Wallace H. Carothers and assigned to E. I. du Pont de Nemours Co., covered the nylon development. These patents have expired and the subject matter is now public property. The patent numbers are: 2,071,250; 2,071,251; 

2,071,253; 2,130,523; and 2,130,948.

e The pressure is not as powerful, however, as it might appear to be. 上 ​The assistant examiners find satisfaction in successfully completing 1 their tasks. The discovery of apt references is a source of real satisfaction. The successful denial of claims, or of the entire patent, is considered the winning of a contest. The young assistant examiner regards each application for a patent as a challenge, and exerts his best efforts to defeat it by the discovery of adequate references or the presentation of sound reasons for denial. The younger assistants el tend to be harsh in their judgment, and to some extent the primary examiner informally acts as an umpire, tempering the proposed harsh judgment of the assistant. This very human factor, of regarding each application for patent as a challenge, is a very potent factor in counterbalancing the onesidedness of ex parte prosecution. It appears that the young assistants who react most vigorously to this challenge and propose the harshest rulings on questions presented later become the most competent examiners. Such vigorous assistants invariably become the most vigorous advocates of the inventor if they leave the Office for outside practice. There are, however, powerful factors tending to force the allowance of applications for patents, especially in the borderline cases. 

It should be here noted that the difficulties are all in these borderline cases. Some of the factors tending to force allowance are here briefly reviewed.

A. GENERAL CRITICISM OF THE OFFICE FOR DELAYS 

A factor tending to force allowance of applications for patents results from general criticism of the Office for delay incident to obtaining patents. This delay is due to a variety of causes, including the workload, the insufficiency of the Office force, delaying tactics by some inventors, and other factors. The administrative officials of the 

Office are well aware of this criticism, and exercise their authority as far as possible to accelerate the final disposition of applications. 

This pressure becomes greater as the number of reconsiderations in a given application increases, to the extent that final action is required on the fourth reconsideration unless special permission to extend the prosecution is obtained (Manual of Patent Examining Procedure, sec. 1005, item 1). This final action may be in the form of a final rejection, but it must be remembered that an allowance is always easier at any stage of the proceeding, and need not be justified, while a final refusal must be explained. 

B. LEGAL RULES FAVORING ALLOWANCE 

In addition to this general pressure tending to force allowance of applications, the examiner also encounters certain legal rules tending to favor allowance of applications.

There is a general rule that doubts as to patentability must be resolved in favor of the inventor. In re Davis, 77 F. 2d 640, 25 

U.S.P.Q. 401 (C.C.P.A. 1935); In re Devine, 261 F. 2d 240, 739 Ó.G. 

334 (C.C.P.A. 1958); Berghane v. Radio Corporation of America, 116 

F. Supp. 200, 99 U.S.P.Q. 264 (Del. 1953), affd., 217 F. 2d 490, 103

U.S.P.Q. 406 (2d Cir. 1954). A footnote in the district court's de cision in the last mentioned case includes the following comment: 

Plaintiff is wrong in saying the Patent Office "recognized the existence of the patentable idea McCullough disclosed" because the Patent Office granted patents when it was in doubt as to the existence of patentable invention. That question was left by the Patent Office for the courts to decide. 

C. REVIEW OF EXAMINER'S DECISIONS 

Rule 196, paragraph (b), states that the Board of Appeals may include a rejection of claims on grounds other than those relied on by the examiner. In Watson (Čommisioner) v. Bruns, 239 F. 2d 

948, 714 O.G. 4 (D.C. Cir. 1956), the court held that the Board did not have jurisdiction to review claims that had been allowed by the examiner, and that any new reason for refusal of claims must be restricted to claims already rejected by the examiner. 

The Court of Customs and Patent Appeals stated that the court "does not question the correctness of allowance by the tribunals of the Patent Office of claims in ex parte cases." In re Arbeit, 206 F. 2d 

947, 41 C.C.P.A. 719, 99 U.S.P.Q.123 (1953).

There is thus no direct review of the primary examiner's decision where it is favorable to the inventor.

On the other hand, all final rulings which are unfavorable to the inventor are subject to review. Review in matters of form is attained by way of petition to the Commissioner. Review on matters of merit is obtained by way of appeal to the Board of Appeals and from the Board to the courts as already explained. Decisions of these tribunals are controlling on the examiner, and under the familiar doctrine of stare decisis he attempts to accord equally favorable treatment to all applicants in similar situations. An unduly favorable ruling of the Commissioner, the Board of Appeals, or the reviewing court may be tested only by a test of the validity of the patent which may come only after several years have elapsed. In the meantime, these decisions, which later may turn out to be "erroneous," are compelling precedents for the examiner. A few instances of such errors are here reviewed briefly.

In Ex parte McBean, 1919 C.D. 35, the Commissioner reversed the examiner's rejection for double patenting, where the examiner had previously required division, holding that any doubt in this matter should be resolved in the inventor's favor. The examiner had required division in the first of the McBean applications, but that application had become a patent before McBean's second application was filed. The examiner's reliance on the McBean patent, in rejecting the claims in the second McBean application, was the question reviewed by the Commissioner. The two McBean patents (Nos. 

1,184,254 and 1,289,492) were subsequently held invalid in Gladding 

McBean Corporation v. Clarke and Sons, 16 F. 2d 50 (9th Cir. 1926), the first for want of proper subject matter, and the second by reason of dedication to the public of all disclosed and unclaimed subject matter contained in the first McBean patent.

In Ex parte Chipman, 1929 C.D. 65, 380 O.G. 751, the examiner's rejection of some claims as unduly broad was reversed by the Board of Appeals. The claims were addressed to a class of chemical compositions to be used as weed killers. The broad claims thus allowed were held invalid when the resulting patent (No. 1,694,205) was tested in court. Chipman Chemical Engineering Company v. Reade Manufacturing Company, 62 F. 2d 430 (3d Cir. 1932). The reasons for invalidating the broad claims of the patent were similar to those used by the examiner in rejecting the claims. 

The history of the McDonough patent (No. 2,577,710), issued December 4, 1951, is another instructive example. McDonough's appli- .cation for a patent on compositions for hair curling (home permanents) was denied by the examiner. The latter's decision was affirmed by the Board of Appeals. The claims were then ordered allowed by the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia in a civil suit (unreported, 1951) authorized by section 4915 R.S. (now 

35 U.S.C. sec. 145). The patent thus authorized was held invalid in Helene Curtis v. Sales Affiliates, 121 F. Supp. 490, 101 U.S.P.Q. 

220 (S.D.N.Y. 1954), affirmed at 233 F. 2d 148, 109 U.S.P.Q. 159 (2d 

Cir. 1956). With minor exceptions, the reasons for holding the patent invalid were those employed by the Office in denying the application for the patent. Yet, these interim appellate decisions were controlling precedents for the examiner until the later invalidation by the court.

It is particularly in the field of procedure, and on review by way of petition to the Commissioner, that the old adage that "hard cases make bad law" is peculiarly applicable. The Commissioner, or the supervisory examiner, confronted with what appears to be a harsh procedural ruling in an isolated case, tends to find an exception or otherwise mitigate the harsh ruling. Such precedents ordinarily involve only procedure and thus do not involve the validity of the resulting patent, but they do add to the examiner's task. The McBean case involved issues of both form and substance. The unfairness of first requiring division and later rejecting on grounds of double patenting is apparent to anyone and is now forbidden (35 U.S.C. sec. 121), but in the McBean case the Commissioner did not take into account the inventor's failure to file the so-called divisional case during the pendency of the first application. While McBean is rather extreme, it is to some extent illustrative. 

VI. THE PATENT SPECIFICATION 

The description and drawings (specification) of the patent are essential to an understanding of the asserted invention and also to assure that the invention will be available to the public at the expiration of the patent monopoly.

The basic requirements as to the description of a patent are set forth in 35 U.S.C. secs. 111, 112, and 113. The first paragraph of section 112 is the most significant and reads as follows:

The specification shall contain a written description of the invention, and of the manner and process of making and using it, in such full, clear, concise, and exact terms as to enable any person skilled in the art to which it pertains, or

with which it is most nearly connected, to make and use the same, and shall set forth the best mode contemplated by the inventor of carrying out his invention.

The last clause, relating to the best mode contemplated, is in broader language than the former statute (sec. 4888 R.S.; 35 U.S.C. sec. 69 (1946)), which limited this requirement to inventions relating to machines. Rule 71 of the Rules of Practice elaborates on this requirement in that it requires the inventor to "describe completely a specific embodiment of the process, machine, manufacture, composition of matter or improvement invented."

The examiner's action with respect to attaining an adequate and clear description is determined by the character of the deficiencies, but is subject to severe limitations. It must be remembered that any ruling as to the description, other than a holding that it does not comply with the requirements of the statute and the rules, is in the nature of an ancillary holding which does not advance the application toward final disposition. The ruling is subject to review by way of petition. 

The examiner is not permitted to insist on his own style of description. 

He could not possibly undertake to rewrite the description. In any rewriting or alteration, the applicant is not permitted to introduce any "new matter," that is, any significant subject matter not derivable from the original description. The examiner may, however, suggest improvements in the style of the description. Although such suggestions are usually adopted by the inventor, the difficulties and burden of work involved in any attempt to improve the description discourages the examiner from taking any action with respect to a description which is barely passable under established legal tests, even though the description may later be criticized by the courts. Standard Oil 

Co. of California v. Tidewater Associated Oil Co., 154 F. 2d 579, 69

U.S.P.Q. 41 (3d Cir. 1946). 

If the examiner finds that the description is insufficient to comply with the statutes and rules, it is his duty to reject the claims for that reason. The insufficiency may stem from a variety of causes, and such causes must be spelled out by the examiner as reasons for rejection. The deficiency may be by reason of contravention of wellknown scientific laws, as in the case of a proposed "perpetual motion" machine. In such case the examiner need only refer to the basic scientific principle, such as the law of conservation of energy, and place the burden on the inventor-applicant of explaining the defect or demonstrating operativeness. More detailed mechanical impossibilities, such as proposed rotation of a square shaft in a square housing may be similarly treated. More abtruse situations as to possible operativeness create greater difficulties for the examiner. 

A. SPECULATIVE DESCRIPTIONS 

Two distinct and contrary philosophies have been developed as to speculative descriptions. One of these philosophies is illustrated by 

Moffet v. Fiske, 51 F. 2d 868 (D.C. Cir. 1931). Fiske obtained a patent for a method of dive bombing which later came into use. the time of application for the patent, airplanes had not been developed At

ad S in strength or maneuverability to the extent capable of carrying out the proposed process, but as is now a matter of common knowledge, such planes were later developed. The patent was held to be invalid. 

The following comments are found in the decision:

In fact, the plaintiff (the patentee) testified that his invention "was a pure dream embodied in the description and drawings" and that he was amazed at the facility with which a patent was obtained for a thing so obvious. 

We think his amazement was justified; but a dream, like an idea, is not an invention, and cannot be patented until embodied in a physical contrivance making it practically useful. 

A different philosophy is presented in the recent case of In re Chilowsky, 229 F.2d 457, 108 U.S.P.Q. 321, 43 C.C.P.A. 775 (1956). In this case, Chilowsky presented an application for a patent on a proposed powerplant wherein the energy was to be derived from nuclear (atomic) fission. The description was couched in general and frankly speculative language, there being no pretense that such a powerplant had been constructed. The decision of the Patent Office Board of 

Appeals refusing the patent on grounds that it was based on an insufficient description was reversed by the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals and the application remanded to the Office for further proceedings. The speculative nature of the description and the applicant's failure to demonstrate the construction of an operative powerplant were held to be no bar to allowance of the patent. The general nature of the examiner's objections, and his failure to particularize as to deficiencies, or to point to the contravention of accepted scientific laws, were held to prevent affirmance of his rejection. Since the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals is the controlling tribunal for the Office, the Office must conform to the views expressed by that court. 

These contrasting views are separately exemplified in papers pub lished in the January 1959 issue of the Journal of the Patent Office 

Society. Mr. Arthur M. Smith states that the patent profession should shoulder the blame for invalid patents, and proposes certain remedial precautions, including the following: 5 This suggests, therefore, that we should be increasingly aware of the importance of making our patent specifications fairly bristle with statements of what was done by the inventor rather than to fill them with wishful statements of what may be done with the invention. Remember, the patent law rewards the doer not the dreamer. The Evans case also requires that the patentee must have gone beyond the mere domain of discovery-he must harness his invention or discovery so that it acts upon the material world. Our job as patent solicitors is to be certain that we understand, disclose, and claim this "harness" as well as the more etheral aspect of the invention. 

5 Smith, "Pitfalls in Patent Prosecution," 41 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 5-33 (1959).

Smith became a member of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals on May 4, 1959. Mr.

6 Mr. S. Wolffe, in expressing his views as to the essentials of a description to satisfy the requirements of 35 U.S.C. sec. 112 and Rule 

71, paragraph b, includes the following statement:

You might wonder why we are so concerned about this question. Why should not an applicant for a patent be required to disclose every last detail of the embodiment in order to leave no doubt as to the sufficiency of its disclosure?

One answer to this question, which readily suggests itself, is that it is very difficult and superfluous in many cases to incorporate in the specification all of the chemical know-how that is required for practicing an invention. However, in my opinion, there is another even more compelling reason which all those who are concerned with this question must keep in mind. An inventor is entitled to reduce his invention to practice either by actually practicing the invention or by filing an application for the invention in the Patent Office. 

The latter method of reducing an invention to practice is called constructive reduction to practice. The right to reduce the invention to practice constructively is very important and inventors in the chemical arts should not be deprived of this advantage. The requirement as to the details which an inventor must furnish in describing the embodiment of his invention must therefore not be so exacting as to make it necessary for him to actually reduce the invention to practice before filing and thus deprive him of the right to constructive reduction to practice.

The grant of patents containing speculative disclosures of what may be done inevitably results in the grant of invalid patents. The subject matter must be new, and being untried it can never be foretold with certainty that a proposed expedient will operate as suggested. 

The very nature of such disclosures is necessarily hazy and indefinite inasmuch as the drafter of the application does not have sure and definite knowledge of his subject matter. 

Speculative patents may well impose a burden upon a new development. The utilization of atomic energy for generating power provides an example. The methods of transforming heat energy into mechanical power are limited and well known. The reactions of substances to the various forms of radioactive energy is a physical property of the particular elements and a description of such properties can be found in scientific literature. The general theory of atomic fission is well known in scientific circles. Due to danger of radioactive contamination of surroundings, the choices of heat transfer systems and media are limited. A half dozen broad, generalized, speculative patents could probably monopolize the entire field of possibilities in transforming the heat energy generated by atomic fission into mechanical power, without solving any of the innumerable problems that are encountered in this field.

Mr. Wolffe's suggestion that there is a right to reduce an invention "to practice" by filing an application in the Patent Office and thereby attaining a so-called "constructive reduction to practice," involves an error of logic. "Constructive reduction to practice" is a rule of evi- • Wolffe, "Adequacy of Disclosure as Regards Specific Embodiment and Use of Invention," 41 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 61-69 (1959).

dence, resulting from the proposition that an inventor must have completed his invention before he filed his application and the filing date, being an official record, is unassailable. It should be noted, however, that the date and fact of filing are the only unassailable portions of the document. The sufficiency of the description may still be challenged.

Unless the doctrine of awarding patents for speculative disclosures is to be approved, there is no reason whatsoever why all of the details of a specific form of the invention, including any specialized “knowhow," should not be included in the patent description. Patent descriptions are not rendered unduly lengthy by a detailed description of particular examples, but by speculation as to what may be done. 

B. REQUIREMENTS FOR DESCRIPTIONS 

The test applied as to the sufficiency of a description to support a patent is whether "any person skilled in the art to which it (the invention) pertains or with which it is most nearly connected” (35 U.S.C. sec. 112) may attain the subject of the patent without further invention. The application of this test and the burden on the examiner is well illustrated by In re Patrick, 189 F. 2d 614, 38 C.C.P.A. 

1105, 90 U.S.P.Q. 28 (1951). Patrick's claims were directed to a chemical process of preparing a rubber-like product and to the product thereof. This subject matter was described in a single paragraph in rather general language, as a modification of the subject matter more completely disclosed. The decision of the Patent Office Board of Appeals refusing the patent as based on an inadequate description was sustained by a divided court. The court was unanimous in holding that the examiner must particularize in pointing out the deficiency which justifies the refusal of a patent. The majority sustained the refusal by reason of the inventor's failure to state a suitable temperature for reaction, a deficiency which had been set forth by the examiner. The dissenting opinion suggested that determination of an operative temperature was within the skill of the art, and reiterated the rule that the examiner must sustain the burden of establishing insufficiency or inoperativeness of the description. The difficulties confronting an examiner in sustaining this burden of establishing a particularized fatal omission are tremendous. The examiner does not have the facilities for independent testing, to say nothing of the time required; nor does he have the required special skills. He must sustain the burden of establishing the absence of details which he must show to be essential.

Recent decisions of appellate tribunals on the question of sufficiency of the description lend little support to the examiner in his efforts to obtain a more definite description. The decision of In re Franck, 255 

F.2d 931, 45 C.C.P.A. 975, 118 U.S.P.Q. 284 (1958), was apparently the first court consideration of the question of sufficiency of description under the new code (35 U.S.C. sec. 112). The invention related to a particular method of applying a strip of magnetic material for sound recording and reproducing purposes, using certain described instrumentalities similar to a draftsman's ruling pen. The strip was to be applied as a coating or inking composition, and the controversy was over the sufficiency of the description of this composition for

carrying out the novel method of application. It was said that the magnetic material had to be pulverized to a fine degree, but neither the optimum nor a limiting degree of fineness was specified. It was also stated that this powder had to be incorporated in a vehicle to obtain a material of "paint-like" or "paste-like" consistency. There was no illustrative example of a composition which could be employed in the unique process. The court held that the description of the consistency in the terms quoted above was sufficient to comply with 35 U.S.C. sec. 112, including, presumably, the last clause which requires a description of "the best mode contemplated by the inventor of carrying out his invention.' The writer can see no sound reason why 

Franck, et al., could not have presented an illustrative example of a composition which could be used in their process, unless they wished to withhold some of the vital information essential in carrying out the process. If the composition was one that was available in the art, no harm would be done by adequate identification. If the composition was not available in the art, and would require extensive experimentation or invention to devise, the description was not sufficient to give the invention to the public at the end of the 17-year monopoly. 

In Rem-Cru Titanium v. Watson (Commissioner), 152 F. Supp. 

282, 114 U.S.P.Q. 529 (Dist. Col. 1957), the court held that a statement of a range of analyses of the components of an alloy was sufficient to comply with 35 U.S.C. sec. 112.

C. REMOVAL OF EXTRANEOUS MATTER 

The examiner may also require the removal of extraneous matter not necessary to the description or relating to subject matter not included in the claim. The examiner's procedure in this respect is set forth in paragraph 1302.01 of the Manual of Patent Examining Pro cedure. Any attempt to force removal of subject matter not germane to the claims of the prospective patent must await final determination of the terms and scope of the claims allowed, and this, of course, comes only when the prosecution of the case is otherwise completed. The pressure toward final disposition of applications is so great that further controversies not directly affecting the validity are rarely raised. 

There is no direct statutory authority for requiring removal of extraneous matter-and under the conditions obtaining in the Office, efforts in that direction are considered largely a waste of time. Such efforts are ordinarily confined to suggestions, which may be followed by the inventor-applicant depending upon the reasonableness of the suggestion and also on the views of the inventor-applicant or his counsel. Reasons for removal of extraneous subject matter are found in Ex parte Sears, 115 U.S.P.Q. 369 (Commissioner 1958). 

D. SCOPE OF DISCLOSURE 

There is another branch in the development of the law which has its effect on the character of description presented. To provide the maximum possible protection, counsel for the inventor generally presents broad claims attempting to include variants and equivalents of the disclosed invention. There is more leeway in mechanical than in chemical inventions, but all broad claims are subject to attack as not adequately supported by the description, unless that description

includes corresponding supporting language. In chemical cases, additional examples and additional explanatory subject matter are essential. See In re Oppenauer, 143 F. 2d 974, 31 C.C.P.A. 1248, 62 

U.S.P.Q. 297 (1944); In re Spengler, 147 F. 2d 1013, 32 C.C.P.A. 855, 64 U.S.P.Q. 560 (1945); Schering Corporation v. Gilbert, 153 F. 2d 

428, 68 U.S.P.Q. 84 (2d Cir. 1946). A facet of this problem is dis cussed in Goodman, "The Invalidation of Generic Claims by Inclusion of a Small Number of Inoperative Species," 40 Jour. Pat. Off. 

Soc. 745-51 (1958). 

E. DISCLOSURE OF EQUIVALENTS 

There is another aspect of the development of law peculiar to patents and especially the chemical field, which relates to possible equivalents. This aspect is well illustrated by In re Bloomer, 178 

F.2d 407, 37 C.C.P.A. 770, 84 U.S.P.Q. 135 (1950), wherein Bloomer described a method of separating certain fractions of fatty acids from the fatty acid mixtures of natural oils A and B by the use of extractants X or Y. The examiner cited references showing a corresponding method of extraction from natural oil A with extractant X. 

Bloomer then confined his claim to the use of extractant Y with source B. The court held that the reference, coupled with Bloomer's concession of the equivalence of A and B as sources and X and Y as extractants, would defeat the claims. This is in accordance with well-established principles that the substitution of equivalents is noninventive and a concession of factual matters by a contending party need not be established by further evidence. This doctrine, of almost universal application in civil law, has also been applied by the courts in testing the validity of patents. See In re Forstrum, 111 F. 2d 181, 27 C.C.P.A. 1160, 519 O.G. 525 (1940); In re Borcherdt, 197 F. 2d 

550, 39 C.C.P.A. 1045, 94 U.S.P.Q. 175 (1952); Shackell v. Watson, affd. 258 F.2d 410, 112 U.S.P.Q. 249 (D.C. 1957); J. R. Clarke Co. v.

Murray Metal Products, 114 F. Supp. 224, 99 U.S.P.Q. 209 (S.D. Tex. 

1953); Jungerson v. Baden, 166 F. 2d 807, 76 U.S.P.Q. 488 (2d Cir. 

1948), affd. 335 U.S. 560 (1949).

The doctrine has been recently restricted by the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals in the decision of In re Ruff and Dukeshire, 256 

F.2d 590, 45 C.C.P.A. 1037, 118 U.S.P.Q. 340, which, in the writer's opinion, will further increase the burden on the examiner. The application of the doctrine of conceded equivalence was confined to situations where such equivalence was known or obvious prior to the filing of the application. The doctrine of the Ruff case, as applied to Bloomer, might well develop in at best the third consideration wherein Bloomer, having restricted his claims to the use of the extractant Y with the source B, could deny that the equivalence of A and B, and of X and Y was known or obvious prior to filing of his application. The examiner would then be confronted with the necessity for an additional search to establish this equivalence. His only other alternative would be to allow the application, with the probability that the patent would later be held invalid by the courts.

It must be remembered that the decisions of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals, and not those of the Federal courts, generally are controlling with respect to the Office. See Ex parte Mohr, 77 

U.S.P.Q. 510 (Board of Appeals 1948).

The results of defects in description may be illustrated by events occuring at the time of World War I (1914-18). Prior to that time the manufacture of organic chemicals was much further advanced in 

Germany than in the United States. Dyestuffs and photographic developers were manufactured in Germany and imported into this country. Many of these were protected by U.S. patents. The embargo, prior to our entry into the war, caused a scarcity of such chemicals. 

An organic compound called "metol" was an especially valuable photographic developer of that time which was protected by a U.S. patent. The specification was of course available. Attempts at the 

University of Michigan (and undoubtedly elsewhere) to synthesize the compound were unsuccessful. Undoubtedly some vital element or condition was omitted. Such omission could not be detected by the examiner. While the standards for an adequate description are now higher than prior to 1914, the arts have become more complex and abstruse.

The case law with respect to sufficiency of the specification was developed when inventions were more simple and less abstruse than at present. It has been the writer's view that new Rule 71, paragraph b (first adopted March 1, 1949) and 35 U.S.C. sec. 112 present far more definite and rigid requirements than the old rule based on "skill of the art." Enforcement of this stricter requirement_would relieve the examiner of some of the impossible, negative burdens of establishing the absence of an essential element or condition. appears, however, that some of the recent legal interpretations, reviewed above, tend to reestablish the old and indefinite "skill of the art" test as the final criterion. 

VII. PERFORMANCE OF THE PATENT OFFICE 

It

The available information does not permit of an accurate evaluation of the performance of the Patent Office in the administration of the examination system. Justice Douglas in his concurring opinion in 

Great Atlantic and Pacific Tea Company v. Supermarket Equipment 

Corporation, 340 U.S. 147, 87 U.S.P.Q. 303 (1950), severely criticized the Office. A more recent criticism is found in Guide v. Desperak, 144 F. Supp. 182, 111 U.S.P.Q. 32 (S.D.N.Y. 1956). In affirming the district court decision, the court of appeals disavowed the district court's criticism of the Office, and suggested that only doubtful patents were involved in litigation, 249 F. 2d 145, 115 U.S.P.Q. 156 (2d Cir. 

1957). This question of invalidated patents was discussed in Senate 

Report 1430, 85th Congress, 2d session. This report shows that during 

1956, 97 patents were considered in the published decisions of the district courts, and 58 of these patents were held invalid. The courts of appeal considered 74 patents and held 40 of these invalid. Thus, of a total of 171 patents, 98 were invalidated, if it be assumed that there was no duplication. This is a very small proportion of the total number of patents issued annually or outstanding, being less than 0.4% of the number of patents issued annually. As suggested by the court of appeals in Guide v. Desperak, supra, these patents are not a fair sample of the patents outstanding, only doubtful patents being litigated. As has previously been stated, there are currently nearly 40,000 patents issued annually. The total number of live patents may be approximated by subtracting the serial number of the

last expired patent from the serial number of the most recently issued patent. As of January 6, 1959, this total amounted to 596,296 patents.

To obtain the number of currently live, presumptively valid patents, the omitted numbers and the invalidated patents must be subtracted from this total. The numbers to be thus subtracted are relatively small and it may be said there are currently in existence approximately 590,000 live presumptively valid patents.

The Patent, Trademark, and Copyright Foundation of George 

Washington University is presently making a study of the actual use of these currently live patents. Interim reports of this study have been published. While the statistics are incomplete and are subject to criticism because of the small sample and the relatively small percentage of responses to questionnaires, these reports show a surprisingly widespread utilization of patents. The report shows that 57% of the assigned patents are subject to past, current, or planned future use. The figure is 49% for the unassigned patents. These figures suggest that at least 150,000 of the currently live patents are in use and possibly as many as 300,000 or more of such patents are being utilized. The current number of patents invalidated annually (less than 100) represents a very small fraction of the patents utilized, if any weight can be given to the interim reports of the Foundation. 

Not all of the relatively small number of invalidated patents are chargeable as error on the part of the Office, under the prevailing law which limits prosecution to ex parte presentation and requires a resolution of doubts in favor of the inventor. The Turnham patent invalidated by the Supreme Court in the A & P v. Supermarket case, supra, was held valid by the district court and the court of appeals, after a full inter partes presentation. Resolution of doubt in favor of the inventor on a question of patentability would have compelled the Office to grant this patent if the facts presented in the district court were made of record in the Office. 

The question of possible disparity of views between the Patent Office and the courts on questions of patentability, and the steps currently being taken by the Office to avoid the issuance of invalid patents, are discussed in Patent Study No. 25, Patent Office, "Court 

Decisions as Guides to Patent Office" (1960). The question of standards of patentability applied by the Office was discussed by Mr. E. L. Reynolds in a paper entitled "Standard of Inventions," delivered September 30, 1957, at the Patent Law Institute held at Villanova University. Mr. Reynolds concluded that the 

Patent Office is more liberal than is warranted by the tests laid down by the courts that directly review its decisions, to wit, the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals and the District Court for the District of Columbia. His conclusion is tempered by the suggestion that only some of the divisions of the Patent Office are too liberal, the stricter divisions being properly limited by the decisions of the appellate courts. He suggests that the standards of the more liberal divisions can be raised by action of the supervisory officials of the Office. 

7 The Patent, Trade-Mark, and Copyright Journal of Research and Education, "The Patent Utilization Study," by Dr. J. Rossman and Dr. B. S. Sanders, vol. 1, No. 1-1957, pp. 74-99; vol. 1, Conf. Supp. 1957, pp. 65-83; vol. 2, No. 1-March 1958, pp. 1-60; vol. 2, 1958 Conf. Supp., pp. 179-198; vol. 3, Conf. Supp. 1959, pp. 54-55 with tables, pp. 110-114. 

8 Mr. Reynolds' paper and parts of the ensuing floor discussion thereof, are being currently republished as Patent Study No. 30 of this Subcommittee.

The validity of the vast majority of patents issued by the Patent Office has never been tested in the courts. It cannot be assumed that all of these unlitigated patents are valid. The Patent, Trademark, and Copyright Foundation reports suggest that at least one-half and possibly three-fourths of the currently live patents are not in active use. A part of this failure to utilize the patents is undoubtedly due to the absence of any commercial or financial advantage. Patents that do not possess any commercial or financial advantage will not be infringed and there will be no occasion for litigation. The law does not provide any tests of commercial or financial advantage before allowance of a patent. Some of the unlitigated patents are also undoubtedly invalid because of a reference which the Office failed to discover. The patentee will rarely litigate or continue the litigation of his patent after being confronted with an unassailable anticipating reference. There is a very large number of valid, presently used patents, which are recognized by the industry, either by the payment of a royalty or by the avoidance of infringement. 

A recent decision, In re Ratti, 270 F. 2d 810, 123 U.S.P.Q. 349, 749 

O.G. 830 (C.C.P.A. 1959), presents an interesting discussion of patentability. The controlling opinion was written by Judge Smith, with Judge Rich in agreement and Judge Martin concurring in the result. The other two members of the court (Worley and Kirkpatrick) dissented. The Board of Appeals, in affirming the examiner's denial of rather detailed claims to a shaft seal, conceded that the prior art references did not show the exact combination of elements in the claims, but held that such claims did not define a patentable invention. The Board went on to emphasize a failure to show any improvement or advantage over the prior art. Judge Smith suggests that unobvious differences without advantages may be patentable, and further that the omission from the Patent Act of 1952 of the former provision (R.S. 4893) giving the Commissioner discretion to refuse a patent unless "the same is sufficiently useful and important," removed any authority to refuse a patent because of an absence of advantage. While the court did find an advantage in Ratti's structure, it is evident that the opinion suggests a very low standard of patentability.

In the Ratti case, the court referred to a paper by M. F. Bailey entitled, "A Proposal for: A Standard of Patentability; Consonant Statutory Changes; A Manual of Determination of Patentability.” ⁹ 

Mr. Bailey suggests that the criterion of "some advantageous progress" be made a part of the test for patentability.

As has already been suggested, the writer finds it impossible to draw any firm conclusions from the available statistics as to the character of performance of the Patent Office. He is of the opinion that, considering the immensity and complexity of the patents and the legal requirements, the Office performs its task excellently. 

Nevertheless, as with all human institutions, there is room for improvements. Undoubtedly, trivial patents have been granted. References have been missed by the Office. The Office has at times failed to apply proper standards of invention. The descriptive portions of many patents are not as clear and concise as would be desirable. 

Claims of unduly broad scope have been granted. A lapse of time 

9 41 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 192-225, 231–257 (1959).

I between the filing of the application and the grant of the patent is inherent in the examination system, but such delays should be minimized.

Certain practical considerations must be kept in mind in any proposals to alter the present examination system. In the writer's experience of over 30 years with the Patent Office, there has never been an adequate supply of competent and experienced personnel. 

With increasing costs and also more varied demands on public funds, it is unlikely that this situation will be altered in the near future. 

Another consideration is the present practice of preserving the secrecy of inventions involved in applications for patent (35 U.S.C. sec. 122).

In our competitive society, any relaxation of this confidential status will invoke controversies and also probably some abuses. 

VIII. PROBLEMS AND POSSIBLE REMEDIES 

The preceding discussion is devoted entirely to the patent system as presently administered and the difficulties encountered in such administration. Certain outstanding problems exist. The writer proposes to note some of these problems and suggest possible answers to some of them. He is of the opinion that the patent system as it has been administered in the past has been a most significant factor in the industrial development of the United States and accordingly suggests only such changes in administration as will, in his opinion, improve such administration without destroying or even substantially changing the system.

The primary purpose of this paper is to present a description of the patent examining system as presently administered. A detailed knowledge of this system is essential to an intelligent consideration of any proposed changes. Detailed discussion of interference proceedings and classification problems is outside the scope of the paper. 

Interference proceedings involve only a relatively small number of applications, being only a little over 1% of the total number of pending applications. Classification is a separate, highly specialized field. 

The Office is primarily an administrative agency, with only such judicial functions as are inseparable from a determination of an inventor-applicant's right to a patent. In discussing possible remedies, certain assumptions are made. There will always be budgetary restrictions. By reason of the specialized knowledge in dual fields that is called for in examiners, there never can be an adequate supply of competent, experienced personnel either for the Office or the profession. In the foreseeable future, the Office, and each examiner, will be confronted with an accumulation of applications awaiting action. 

With these assumptions, it is believed desirable to restrict the areas of controversy to the greatest possible extent. 

Certain remedial steps are obvious and will only be briefly noted. 

These steps are primarily those which will improve the quality of the examiner's work and facilitate such work. Ex parte presentation is inherent in the present procedure for granting patents. A possible modification is in opposition proceedings. Such proceedings, in theory, invite the public to assist the examiner in his work, or provide a preliminary forum to settle disputes between interested parties. 

This proposal will be briefly discussed, but for reasons set forth the writer is of the opinion that such proceedings will not materially

improve the administration of the patent system. The other possible remedial steps must look to increasing the examiner's authority, facilitating his work, and decreasing any harassment. It is believed that some improvement may be attained by the remedies suggested later in this paper. These include restricting any relief by way of petition, abolishing civil action under 35 U.S.C. sec. 145, spelling out more definite requirements as to illustration in specifications, and restricting the number of claims.

The right to a patent is viewed as wholly a statutory right. In setting up the requirements that are to be the prerequisites to the grant of a patent there must be some balance reached between the public interest and the rights of the inventor. Assistance and relief for the incompetent inventor or his counsel also involves assistance and encouragement for the overreaching or overly contentious inventor or counsel. Increasing liberality with respect to the inventor necessarily increases the controversies in the courts on the patents thus granted. As already suggested, the writer would not significantly alter our patent system, which he believes has been a primary factor in our industrial development. The changes proposed are essentially minor alterations looking toward improvement in administration. 

Some balance must be attained between the interests of the inventor and the general public interest. In theory the inventor contributes something new, not theretofore available to the general public, and to encourage him he is given a monopoly for a limited time on his contribution. This monopoly does not detract from what is available to the public since, in theory at least, it covers only that which was unknown and unavailable to the public, and after the 17-year monopoly expires, it becomes an addition to what would not otherwise be available in the public domain. On the other hand, the too liberal grant of patents on any modification of what is readily available in prior patents and printed publications operates as a burden on the public, unduly restricting the ordinary artisan or manufacturer in making any modification or improvement in manufacturing processes or manufactured products. Overreaching claims, even when based on meritorious advances, also tend to restrict progress or development of the useful arts.

These are conflicting interests, and it is impossible to draw a sharp line of distinction between the two. A too liberal policy in the grant of patents to inventors would result in interference with the freedom of ordinary artisans and manufacturers to modify or improve their processes and products. A grant of broad or overreaching claims stifles competitors in devising other means or instrumentalities to accomplish similar results. On the other hand, a too harsh policy with respect to the grant of patents fails to encourage inventors and their financial supporters. It is impossible to define invention, or to draw a distinct line of demarcation between what is an inventive change and what is merely an obvious modification within the skill of any artisan. Any line between the two must be at best an indefinite compromise between the two concepts. The writer would not alter presently accepted lines of demarcation between invention and obvious modification. He suggests only such changes as would tend to clarify and simplify the conflicts of interest. Because of the ex parte nature of proceedings to obtain a patent, there will inevitably be a

residuum of conflict of interests which can only be settled after an inter partes hearing before some court. Such controversies in the courts are inevitable unless the officials of the Patent Office are to be granted arbitrary authority not subject to later review. This arbitrary authority, to be effective, would necessarily extend to favorable as well as unfavorable decisions on the grant of patents. The writer does not advocate such arbitrary authority. The differences of opinion so often found in the reports of litigated cases between the district courts, the courts of appeals, and the U.S. Supreme Court are illustrative of the difficulties encountered. See also the differences of opinion expressed by members of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals in the In re Ratti case, supra. 

Remedies, short of granting the examiner arbitrary authority or alleviating ex parte presentation by opposition proceedings, must involve improving the quality of the examiner's work and restricting the possible fields of controversy. The steps required for the former are substantially self-obvious and are discussed below under the headings of "Personnel," "Search Facilities," and "Classification." Restricting the possible fields of controversy requires statutory modifications in the proposal for a separate fee for each claim. This proposal will decrease the number of possible elements of controversy without penalizing the indigent or the honest inventor. Another proposal is a requirement for a more adequate factual background by a requirement for exemplary description and drawings supporting each claim, of such a nature as to enable anyone skilled in the art to attain the subject matter without significant experimentation. With a requirement for drawings substantially to scale, with materials of construction identified in mechanical cases, and complete illustrative examples in process and chemical cases, the field of controversy based on speculation as to absent facts would be narrowly limited. Another field of controversy could be narrowed by imposing a restriction in time as to any assertion of priority. These proposals are further elaborated later in this paper.

A. PERSONNEL 

The most obvious remedial step is the retention of an adequate staff of competent examiners. The competent patent examiner must possess both a scientific and a legal education equivalent to a bachelor's degree in each of these fields, and he must have some experience in the patent field. This twofold professional education should be recognized in personnel classification and resulting compensation. Industry will inevitably more than meet any Patent Office compensation, and the Office will inevitably serve to some extent as a training school for patent attorneys. The conditions of work, the compensation, and promotion policies of the Office should be such as to make it possible to retain an adequate staff of experienced and competent examiners. It is suggested that civil service rules could well recognize the exceptional nature of the examiner's education and duties in fixing the general scale of salaries and promotion policy. The nature of this problem and the general answer thereto are so obvious as hardly to merit detailed discussion. 63416-61— 4

B. SEARCH FACILITIES 

Even the most competent examiner must confine his search to facili ties that are available to him within the limited time he can afford to devote to this task. A complete library of all sources of scientific and industrial knowledge is obviously impossible. Within reasonably practical limits, the Office should maintain a complete library of textbooks, treatises, and periodicals of all significant scientific and industrial subjects. This should include adequate indexes insofar as available and also a staff for translation of works in foreign languages. 

C. CLASSIFICATION 

Adequate classification of all prior knowledge is a necessity. The problem of classification is an abstruse subject which the writer does not pretend to solve. An adequate classification staff is an obvious essential. A possibility for machine coding and searching in a limited field is reported in the Journal of the Patent Office Society for October 1958.10 Office developments in this field have been reported in the 

Annual Report of the Commissioner of Patents for Fiscal Year 1957 

and subsequent reports.

D. THE PATENT DESCRIPTION 

The ideal description in a patent should be of such nature as to enable anyone skilled in this art to practice the invention with a minimum of experimentation and a minimum of specialized know-how. 

The patented subject matter passes to the public domain at the end of the 17-year monopoly. The prime consideration for the patent monopoly is this contribution of previously unknown subject matter to the useful arts. Inventive subject matter relates essentially to useful and practical constructions, machines, compositions, or methods. An ideal description of such subject matter should include a detailed exposition of illustrations of components, structure, procedural steps, and results, and also permissible alternatives or variants. Explanation of the mode of operation is also desirable. 

The inventor's primary interest in the description (aside from compliance with the statutes) is to provide the basis for claiming an adequate scope of protection. The permissible scope of the patent claims is dependent to a large extent on the character of the generalized portion of the description. This is especially true in cases involving chemical and other abstruse subject matter where the equivalent modifications are not immediately obvious. The specification writer desiring the maximum possible claim coverage is confronted with difficulties in generalizing beyond the specific embodiments established by the inventor. He may include modifications which are inoperative and thereby invalidate claims inclusive thereof. See Goodman, "The In validation of Generic Claims by Inclusion of a Small Number of In operative Species," 40 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 745-750 (1958). Or he may generalize to an extent that includes prior practices and thus invalidate any generic claims, and possibly all the claims. These con- 

10 Milmore, "The Samroin Coding and Searching System," 40 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 698-726 (1958).

siderations have led some specification writers to include abstruse, ambiguous, or conditioned language, especially where the object is to obtain the maximum possible coverage. 

In addition to the small minority of specification writers who may deliberately use unclear language, there are, of course, specification writers of less than maximum competence who, through inadequate command of the language, a confusing style of exposition, or even an inadequate knowledge of the subject matter, produce descriptions which do not adequately set forth the invention. 

No rules or legislation can compel competence in an incompetent or a deliberately confusing writer. It is believed, however, that improvement may be attained by more detailed requirements as to illustrative subject matter. The codified statute (35 U.S.C. sec. 112, first par.) and the new rule (Rule 71, first adopted March 1, 1949) are steps in this direction. There has been some controversy as to this rule and the supporting statute. It is suggested that a more adequate description would be attained by a more detailed requirement in the rules, supported by statutory modification if necessary, spelling out the necessity for clear and detailed exemplary subject matter. 

This might take the form of a rule to the effect that every claim of a patent must be supported by a detailed description of a specific exemplary embodiment included within the terms of the claim, and setting forth the method of constructing or compounding and using, as well as the particular results attained. 

The rule should require drawings substantially to scale, with a statement of materials of construction employed in mechanical cases. 

Process cases would require a statement of instrumentalities and materials used, as well as conditions employed and the results secured. 

Cases relating to composition would require complete illustrative examples of the components employed, methods of compounding, and the results attained.

Each claim purports to be a definition inclusive of inventive subject matter. A study of the specifications is required for a proper understanding of the claims. Present Rule 83 requires the drawing to show every feature specified in the claims. A requirement that every claim be supported by at least one completely described exemplary embodiment, whether chemical, electrical, or mechanical, will lessen areas of possible controversy on the question of adequate support for the claims.

This proposal would be in the public interest as it would most clearly put the public in possession of the invention on expiration of the patent. It would remove some of the burden on the examiner in avoiding controversies as to sufficiency of description and enable him to sustain at least a part of the burden of pointing out deficiencies or impossibilities. It would be of assistance to the real inventor in spelling out for him the requirements of a proper description. 

The proposal would tend to correct certain abuses of the patent system, wherein an applicant for a patent attempts to forecast future developments in an art and lay a burden on such future development through generalized claims. Speculative patents based on suppositions of what might be developed in the future, such as illustrated by Moffet v. Fiske, supra, would be outlawed. The difficulties presented in such cases as In re Chilowsky and In re Patrick, supra, would also be avoided.

Definite requirements for detailed drawings and examples, in the writer's opinion, would tend to decrease possibilities of later misrepresentation or incomplete representation by means of later-filed ex parte affidavits. A paucity of factual details in the application and in reference patents lends itself to the presentation of half-truths in ex parte evidence during the prosecution.

During the writer's service on the Board of Appeals, he was frequently impressed with the paucity of detailed information which, if present, would easily resolve many of the issues presented on appeal. 

E. CLAIMS 

Each claim of a patent purports to be a statement defining the subject matter and scope of the asserted invention. Some of the earlier patents had a simple statement somewhat as follows: "I claim: the invention as described." This form is not considered to comply with the requirements of 35 U.S.C. sec. 112, or its predecessor R.S. 4888.

There has grown up an immense body of technical case law relating to the permissible scope and form of claims. A review of the history of this development or a treatment of the technical requirements is beyond the scope of this paper. The subject matter will be only sufficiently reviewed to present the problem under discussion. 

As a general rule, subject matter outside the literal scope of the claim definition is not within the patent monopoly. Adequate language exactly defining the subject matter to be monopolized by the patent cannot at the same time serve as a description of an illustrated form of the invention. Since the function of the claims is definition of scope, criticism of the form as failing to provide a simple description of the invention is beside the point. Suitable generic language is customarily used to encompass obvious variants and equivalents of the specific invention described. The inventor is interested in securing as broad or comprehensive protection as possible. 

It is in this field of the scope and adequacy of claim definition that most of the efforts of patent examination and prosecution are expended. Inventions cannot be considered in the abstract. It is the asserted invention defined in the claim or claims that must be considered from the standpoint of anticipation or patentability. Questions of definiteness, support, scope, and functionality arise, independently of novelty and patentability. A very few of the leading cases are here noted to illustrate the problem. In O'Reilly v. Morse, 15 How. (U.S.) 62 (1853), the Supreme Court invalidated Morse's claims relating to the telegraph as being functional, i.e., including all instrumentalities that would accomplish the result. In the Incandescent Lamp Patent suit, 159 U.S. 465 (1895), the broad claims of the early incandescent lamp patent (No. 317,676) were held to be too indefinite to be the subject of a valid monopoly and therefore not infringed by the carbonized bamboo fibers contained in the commercial lamp covered by the later Edison patent (No. 223,898).

Further development of this subject matter is found in General Elec tric Co. v. Wabash Electric Co., 304 U.S. 364 (1938)'; United Carbon 

Co. v. Binney and Smith Co., 317 U.S. 228 (1943); Halliburton Oil

Well Cementing Co. v. Walker, 329 U.S. 1 (1946); Faulkner v. Gibbs, 338 U.S. 267 (1949).

The reports of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals include a large number of cases in which the turning point involved the scope and terms of the claims. These cases are too voluminous and the subject matter too technical for review in this paper. Attention may be directed, however, to Kropa v. Robie, 187 F. 2d 150, 38 C.C.P.A. 

858, 88 U.S.P.Q. 478 (1951), reviewing a large number of cases dealing with the question of the significance of the introductory clause, and In re Jolly, 172 F. 2d 566, 36 C.C.P.A. 825 (1949); In re Lauks, 86 F. 2d 833, 24 C.C.P.A. 812 (1937); In re Jurgeleit, 194 F. 2d 120, 92 U.S.P.Q. 323 (C.C.P.A. 1952); and In re Ferguson, 83 F. 2d 693, 23 C.C.P.A. 1143 (1936). 

The special abstruse nature of the chemical field has generated a special type of claim designed to avoid the problem of undue breadth. 

This type is now designated a "Markush claim," from the leading decision, Ex parte Markush, 1925 C.D. 126 (Commissioner). In this form of claim the operative members of a class of chemical compounds are named, instead of employing generic language. The ramifications and technicalities of this aspect of the practice are beyond the scope of this paper.

A paragraph concerning the interpretation of functional claims was included in the Patent Act of 1952 (35 U.S.C. sec. 112, 3d par.). 

The effect of this new provision is discussed in Ex parte Ball, 99 

U.S.P.Q. 143 (Board of Appeals 1953). 

At the present time, the courts do consider the permissible scope of claims and extend the literal terms of patented claims to cover obvious or equivalent variants. In Graver Tank and Manufacturing Co. v. 

Linde Air Products Co., 336 U.S. 271 (1949) and 339 U.S. 605 (1950), the Supreme Court completely discarded the claim structure of the Jones patent involved in the litigation. The broad claims, inclusive of the infringing composition were held invalid, in the first of the two decisions, and a holding of validity and infringement of the narrow claims was sustained. In the second decision, after rehearing, the holding of infringement of the narrower claims was again sustained by a divided court. The validated narrow claims excluded the infringing composition. The court thus removed the broad claims by invalidation and in effect rewrote the narrow claims to include the infringing composition. The dissenting opinions contended that the claim language was the only proper measure of protection and while the infringing composition was mentioned in the specification it was not included in the valid claims. 

The doctrine of "file wrapper estoppel" is a further complicating factor which results from a consideration of the numerous mutations undergone by a plurality of claims during pendency in the Office. 

This concept denies protection of an accused modification, where such modification was once included within the terms of a claim presented during the prosecution of the application but was later excluded therefrom in order to secure the allowance of the patent. The key here seems to be whether the claim restriction was a deliberate response to the examiner's ruling or inadvertent. The possible correctness of the examiner's ruling is not significant. See I.T.S. Rubber Co. v. Essex 

Rubber Co., 272 U.S. 429 (1936).

F. MULTIPLICITY OF CLAIMS 

The number of claims presented in an application and in the resulting patent are in part a result of an antiquated system, reminiscent of the antiquated system of common law pleading which permitted a restatement of complaints and possible answers in a multitude of guises, often inconsistent with each other, but all in technical and involved language. No alleviation was attained by modification of sec. 4934 R.S. in 1927, requiring an additional fee of $1 for each claim in addition to 20 in the application as filed and again in the final fee on issuance. Nor was paragraph (b) of new Rule 75 (1949), requiring substantial differences in the claims and prohibiting undue multiplicity, of much help in this respect. 

The statutory fee modification of 1927 had the practical effect of suggesting 20 claims as a standard, and a number of practitioners have insisted on this as a minimum number. It is to be remembered that the examiner is compelled to consider each claim individually. While there is a very general practice of presenting more claims than required, real abuse in the matter of presenting a multiplicity of claims occurs on the part of only a relatively small minority of patent practitioners. 

Rule 75 (b) reads:

More than one claim may be presented, provided they differ substantially from each other and are not unduly multiplied. 

This rule is little more than an expression of existing case law and is difficult to apply. Often it takes as much effort to enforce the rule as it takes to examine each claim individually. Since the examiner's decision is reviewable by the Board of Appeals and by the courts, all reasons for refusal must be entered. The decision of In re Beltz, 181 F.2d 203, 37 C.C.P.A. 1004 (1950), is illustrative. The examiner's rejection of all the claims for undue multiplicity as well as rejection of each of the claims as unpatentable over the prior art was sustained by the Board of Appeals. On appeal to the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals, Beltz removed some of the claims in an attempt to meet the multiplicity rejection. The court stated that this action was a concession of the multiplicity rejection and was sufficient to dispose of the case, but it went on to consider the retained claims on the merits. This approach is not very helpful in lending support to an examiner's efforts to enforce the rule. Attention may also be directed to In re Spencer, 47 F. 2d 806, 407 O.G. 861, 18 C.C.P.A. 1041 (1931); 

In re Mays, 175 F. 2d 570, 36 C.C.P.A. 1188 (1949); In re Barnett, 155 F. 2d 540, 33 C.C.P.A. 1119 (1946). See also Electrical Engineers

Equipment Co. v. Champion, 23 F. 2d 600, 379 O.G. 697 (2d Cir. 

1928). Multiplicity is universally condemned, but no weapon has been forged to destroy it.

A rejection of one claim as not substantially different from another is equally difficult to sustain and usually involves a controversy as to the significance of added words. The decision of In re Thuau, 135 F. 2d 344, 30 C.C.P.A. 979 (1943), is illustrative. Thuau presented two sets of claims. One set defined a class of chemical compound, differing in scope as to the class. A duplicate set differed by the inclusion of an introductory clause referring to the compounds as remedial agents for a specified pathological condition. The rejec

tion was sustained by the court, but it will be evident that more additional work was required than would have been the case had the examiner confined his efforts to examining the individual claims on the merits.

It is suggested that only a radical departure from present philosophy and present practice will provide a cure for this burden of unnecessary duplication of claims. Such departure might take the form of a new scale of fees, in which the minimum fees for filing an application, issuing a patent, and appealing an adverse decision, respectively, would be based on the presence of a single claim. A substantial additional fee would be required for each additional claim. For example, the fees presently required (35 U.S.C. sec. 41, pars. (a) 1, 2, 4, and 

6) could be made applicable to a single claim application, with an additional fee of $10 required for each additional claim. 

This proposal would bring a gradual change on the part of the practitioner from a philosophy of a multiplicity of claims with a minimum of 20, to a philosophy of viewing any claim over one as being presumptively excessive.

The indigent inventor without financial backing would ordinarily not be adversely affected, as his efforts of necessity are usually confined to relatively simple and inexpensive developments which can be adequately defined by a single claim. Financially supported inventors and corporation-supported inventors, developing complex inventions or a multiplicity of illustrative modifications, would be prepared to pay a fair share in terms of the amount of work imposed on the Office.

The patentee could place greater reliance on the doctrine of equiva lents (see Graver Tank and Manufacturing Co. v. Linde Air Products 

Co., supra). The feasibility of a statutory modification, spelling out protection for the patentee for obvious or named equivalents, might be considered in connection with the proposed modification of the fees. The courts, after an inter partes presentation, would be in a better position to determine the fair scope of a patent than is the examiner after a wholly one-sided ex parte presentation. 

An additional advantage of the above proposal as to fees would be the avoidance of most of the questions of division or restriction (35 U.S.C. sec. 121) and accompanying troublesome questions of double patenting. Division or restriction would be necessitated only in the most flagrant cases of inclusion of diverse subject matter. 

It is the writer's opinion that a very large proportion of the applications could as well be presented with one, rather than more than one, claim without jeopardy to an inventor's rights. His experience as a member of the Patent Office Board of Appeals convinced him of the truth of an old adage that the same mistake is usually repeated in all of the claims, where a multiplicity is presented. The philosophy that a plurality of claims is necessary has so permeated the profession that in one instance counsel apologized for the presence of a single claim, suggesting that the absence of a plurality of claims should not prejudice his client's position. It is appreciated that there will be vigorous initial opposition to this fee proposal as a radical departure from present practices. It is urged, however, that a significant additional fee for each additional claim, a scale of fees commensurate with the labors of the Office and not out of line with present-day costs of

prosecution as well as examination, may be attained. It is the writer's opinion that no other expedient will significantly mitigate this burden of unnecessary multiplication of claims. 

As set forth in Study No. 13, entitled "Patent Office Fees-A Leg islative History," proposals have recently been made suggesting an increase in the basic fees and a further provision reducing from 20 to 5 or 10 the maximum number of claims that could be filed without paying an extra fee, an additional fee being charged for each claim above the maximum. The Study contains a discussion of the question of what proportion of the total cost of administration of the Office should be paid by the applicants for patents. No opinion is offered as to this question. There appears to be a general recognition of the desirability for some increase in fees, consistent with rising costs. The writer believes that all of the increase in fees should be levied on claims in excess of one claim. Any maximum, be it 5, 10, or 15 claims, also becomes a minimum in the view of the profession. 

In a significant number of inventions, a single claim is adequate. An additional fee for claims over a maximum of 5, 10, or 15 is also viewed as a penalty for a multiplicity of claims, whereas the above proposal for a fee for each claim should be viewed as graduated fees based on the complexity of the invention and the amount of work involved in its examination.

A statistical study of the number of claims presented in applications as filed, and also in patents, was presented in the Journal of the Patent Office Society by P. J. Federico.11 While the median number of claims per application as filed is 8, it is evident from the data, as graphically presented in figure 1 (p. 805) of that study, that there was an attempt to write 20 claims in a large number of applications. 

The median number of claims in patents as issued was 412. The patents show a less artificial distribution in the numbers, the stimulus created by the maximum limit of 20 without additional fee being much less effective. Three factors probably contribute to the significant differences between the number of claims found in applications as filed and in patents as granted. There is the withdrawal of claims defeated by the prior art cited. Counsel for the inventor-applicant probably finds the task of answering the examiner's rejections and objections for each of a large number of claims too burdensome and difficult and eliminates some of the claims to make his task easier. In some instances, the number of claims is reduced as the result of a direct attack on the number by the examiner.

The examiner may take one of two possible lines of attack on the unreasonable number of claims. He may analyze the claims individually and point out virtual duplicates, explaining why differences are inconsequential, and require the removal of the duplicates. In the alternative, he may attack the claims as a whole as being so numerous as to confuse and conceal the subject matter. This attack is sustainable only in very aggravated cases of multiplied claims. Any attack on the number of claims does not advance the prosecution of the case toward final disposition, and is thus in the nature of extra work, which in many cases requires more effort than examining the claims. 

11 Federico, "Size of Applications and Patents," 36 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 804-811, 814826 (1954).

gua a 10 

G. PROPOSED TIME RESTRICTIONS 

A number of proposals for further time restrictions have been advanced. Some of these proposals were discussed by George E. Frost in Study No. 2, entitled "The Patent System and the Modern Econ omy" (pp. 66, et seq.). 

A frequently mentioned proposal is the "20-year" proposal, which would limit the patent term to 17 years from date of issue or 20 years from the date of filing the application, whichever is less. An objection to the proposal is its result in penalizing an inventor-applicant for unavoidable Office delays. Deliberate delays caused by an applicant's dilatory tactics are often difficult to distinguish from delays occurring through no fault of the applicant. It would, for example, be impossible to classify an appeal to the Board of Appeals in terms of whether it was a dilatory action without merit, or an action necessary to rule on a meritorious issue. An appeal presently adds about 

2 years to the pendency of an application. While the proposed 20year restriction would undoubtedly reduce the number of frivolous appeals and also tend to discourage other dilatory tactics, it would also penalize unavoidable delays.
