Any attempt to qualify the proposed restriction would raise impossible administrative problems, or so qualify the restriction as to render it ineffective. Attention may be directed to M. J. Levy's discussion of this proposal in "The Mischievous Twenty Year Bills," 40 Jour. Pat. Off. Soc. 759–768 (1958).

In the writer's opinion, the 20-year proposals do have some merit. 

Some of the deliberate delay in the Office would be avoided by such proposals. However, an arbitrary 20-year limit, without exceptions, will lead to injustice to some inventors whose applications are unavoidably delayed in the Office through arrears in the Office work or by interference proceedings. Mistakes on the part of the examiner also cause delays. In the writer's opinion, any attempt to qualify the 

20-year limit would involve administrative problems greater than any benefits derived from the restriction. An inventor is ordinarily entitled to an opportunity for response and review of any adverse ruling. It would be difficult, if not impossible, to differentiate between deliberate delays on the part of the inventor and delays caused by mistakes or other circumstances in the Office. Most of the deliberately delaying tactics, such as the addition or maintenance of unsupportable claims, insufficient response, trivial arguments, etc., have either an element of bad faith or are due to incompetence. Any ruling suggesting unethical conduct or incompetence would be reluctantly entered and difficult to establish. Contest might develop as to the relative competence of the examiner and counsel. There are also applications which are kept pending as long as possible without any real hope of obtaining a patent, as a justification for using the "patent pending" label. It is the writer's view that the proposed 20-year limitation would be inequitable to inventors unless tempered by a requirement for a ruling apportioning the blame for delays. At the present time about half of the patents being issued had been pending for at least 3 years in the Office. Any attempt to apportion the source of the delay would add an additional field of controversy and thus increase the burden of the already overburdened examiner.

It is suggested that sound practice and competent workmanship at the examination level, together with a staff sufficiently large to keep the Office work reasonably current, will accomplish most of the results sought in the 20-year limitations. 

H. TIME LIMITATIONS ON PRIORITY 

Another proposal has been to provide a statutory limitation as to the earliest date of invention that can be shown. The "Preliminary 

Draft of the Proposed Revisions and Amendment of the Patent 

Laws" (January 10, 1950, Committee on the Judiciary, House of

Representatives, par. 25) included a provision which barred showing a date of invention more than 2 years prior to the filing date. To be effective the limitation should be applicable to situations in which an applicant relies on earlier filed applications (35 U.S.C. secs. 120 and 121) as well as to the presentation of other evidence of priority (35 U.S.C. secs. 102(g) and 135). It is believed that a period of 3 years rather than the 2 years proposed in the "Preliminary Draft" would be preferable.

Much of the abuse of long pendency results from the presentation of substitute, divisional, and continuation cases as permitted by 35 

U.S.C. secs. 120 and 121. The statute accords the inventor the benefit of the date of the first filing of an application provided successive cases are filed during the pendency of the previous case, and the same subject matter is adequately disclosed in each case. 

One of the common abuses results from delay in filing a divisional case until the first application is about to issue as a patent or become abandoned. The inventor may file a single application disclosing a number of distinct inventions. He may present different claims for each of these distinct inventions. He will be met with a requirement to restrict the claims to a single invention. On compliance with this requirement, the examination proceeds on the elected invention. The nonelected subject matter is maintained dormant in the application until the examination process on the elected invention is completed. 

The inventor then files a second application for the dormant subject matter. This process may be repeated until all the subject matter of the first application is exhausted. It is evident that the possibilities for delays where the subject matter deals with a complex machine having a number of novel elements are very great. 

Another abuse occurs in the refiling of an application on substantially the same subject matter so as to afford a chance to relitigate substantially the same or similar issues. In Ex parte Smith, 1888 Č.D. 

131, the Commissioner held that the second application need not be examined except as to subject matter patentably distinct from that claimed in the first, but that holding fails as soon as the first becomes abandoned. The legal doctrines of lite pendens and res judicata do not seem to be effective in avoiding unnecessary work in the Office. 

In re Isler, 152 F. 2d 1002, 68 U.S.P.Q. 198 (1946); In re Prutton, 205 F.2d 198, 98 U.S.P.Q. 269 (1953).

As has already been stated, a maximum period of 3 years is suggested. The particular period of time that might be selected becomes a matter of compromise between the possible interests of the inventor and the public interest. The public interest is best served by keeping at a minimum the delay in patenting a completed invention. Expe-

dition also simplifies the examination system to some extent. Some hardship cases may occur, just as they do at times under the 1-year rule as to publication, public use, etc. However, 3 years should be ample to finish testing an invention and determine the feasibility of filing an application. Three years should also be ample to determine the desirability of filing substitute, continuation, or divisional applications. There is presently a real hardship to a second independent inventor when confronted with an earlier rival who has maintained the same subject matter in a dormant state through a series of pending applications. 

I. DUAL APPELLATE PROCEDURES 

As has already been suggested, an inventor may select one of two entirely distinct paths for review of an unfavorable decision by the Board of Appeals of the Patent Office. One of these paths is by way of direct appeal to the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals, in which case the inventor is confined to the record made in the Office (35 U.S.C. secs. 141, 142, 143, 144). The other path for appeal is by way of civil action in the U.S. District Court for the District of 

Columbia (35 U.S.C. sec. 145). 

Some consideration should be given to a revocation of section 145 of title 35, United States Code, giving a right of review by way of civil action in the U.S. District Court for the District of Columbia. These suits are in form inter partes, the Commissioner of Patents being the defendant. As a practical matter, the Solicitor for the Patent Office must content himself with a defense of the Patent Office position as expressed by the Board of Appeals in its decision. The Office does not have the facilities to present independent evidence or independent experts. The judges of the district court are usually relatively unfamiliar with the patent laws. Where there are differences in interpretation of the statutes between the district court and the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals, the Office is bound by the interpretations of the last mentioned court (Ex parte Mohr, supra). It is evident that difficulties are bound to follow in any instance where the district court interpretation is more liberal than that of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals. As a practical matter, the Office generally follows the more liberal interpretation where the two courts are not in agreement. There was for a time a divergence of views as to estoppel arising from failure to assert additional claims during an interference proceeding, International Cellucotton v. Coe, 85 F. 2d 869, 471 O.G. 

243 (D.C. Cir. 1936), being more liberal than In re Ellis, 86 F. 2d 412, 24 C.C.P.A. 759 (1927). Subsequent modification of the Office rule 

(then Rule 109) to follow the more liberal court removed the difficulty. 

In presenting evidence of prior invention to overcome cited references, corroboration is required in the civil action just as it is in infringement and interference cases. Schering v. Marzall, 101 F. Supp. 

571, 91 U.S.P.Q. 358 (Dist. Col. 1951). The Office, however, does not require such corroboration (Rule 131) and insofar as the writer is aware the question has not been raised before the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals. 

On the admission of evidence of advantages not clearly spelled out in the original description, the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals seems more liberal than the district court. Compare In re Mc- 

Kenna, 203 F. 2d 717, 40 C.C.P.A. 937 (1953), and In re Stewart, 222

F.2d 747, 42 C.C.P.A. 937 (1955), with Abbott v. Coe, 104 F. 2d 449 (D.C. Cir. 1940). However, there is an advantage to the inventor in the civil action, in that he may present additional or more elaborate evidence and exhibits than in the ex parte presentation before the Office. This advantage may, to some extent, encourage partial or incomplete presentation before the Office, but such practice has its limitations. Schering Corp. v. Marzall, supra, Killian v. Watson, 743 

O.G. 8 (Dist. Col. 1958). In a civil action, the court will not admit evidence, even though it may clearly be pertinent, where the inventors should have presented it in the Office and failed to do so, the theory being that such evidence was suppressed. 

Although there is a distinct impression that success is more likely in a civil action under section 145 than by direct appeal under section 141, where close questions of patentability are involved and where impressive technical demonstrations can be arranged, it is suggested that the right of a civil action under 35 U.S.C. sec. 145 might well be abolished without substantial disadvantage to inventor-applicants, and with distinct advantages to the Office and the patent system. At least, extended litigation of the type involved in connection with the McDonough patent (Helene Curtis v. Sales Affiliates, supra), briefly reviewed above, might be avoided. 

J. OPPOSITION PROCEEDINGS 

The use of opposition or revocation proceedings in certain foreign countries, and proposals for such proceedings in the United States, were reviewed in Study No. 4, entitled "Opposition and Revocation 

Proceedings in Patent Cases." Such proceedings have been suggested as a means of avoiding the later invalidation of patents by reason of prior art references which were not discovered by the examiner. 

The writer is not in favor of such proceedings, and is of the opinion that they would result in delays and an increased burden of work for the Office-much greater than any benefits that might be derived therefrom. As already stated, the examiner cannot undertake a socalled validity search on every application for a patent. Possible competitors of the inventor will not go to the expense of such validity search unless or until they are directly threatened. Rule 292 of the 

Rules of Practice relating to public use proceedings now provides for a limited type of opposition to the grant of a patent, where a competitor has knowledge of an application for a patent which might affect his interests. This knowledge may come through an interference proceeding or by way of an announcement by the inventor-applicant, sometimes accompanied by threats of infringement prosecution upon issuance of the expected patent. Besides submitting such evidence as he has of prior public use, the threatened competitor may also notify the Office of such prior art references as he is aware of, thereby directing the examiner's attention to such citations. Public use proceedings, however, rarely develop any prior art of more significance than that already developed by the examiner. On the other hand, they are often productive of further ancillary and nondeterminative controversies. Reference may be had to the history of the McDonough patent (Helene Curtis v. Sales Affiliates, supra) reviewed earlier in

01 ! this paper. In that situation, a threatened competitor was charged with having unjustly and improperly interfered with the grant of the patent to McDonough. While these charges were held to be without foundation, the possible representations provided another point of controversy in the case.

Institution of an opposition proceedings would pose the problem of restricting its scope. Maximum results would best be obtained in a completely unrestricted inter partes conflict. Such a conflict, however, could lead to abuses by anyone sufficiently interested in preventing the issuance of the patent, and the expenses might well run as high as those that attend an infringement suit in court. I am convinced that such conflicts would be generated even though there were no real merit in the opposition. On the other hand, an opposition of the most restricted scope would involve only noting of possible references or other obstacles not already in the record. While this undoubtedly would result in uncovering significant references in a relatively small number of cases, I believe that the number would be so small as not to warrant the procedure. It must be remembered that the examiner would be required to obtain the file and restudy it with the new references in every case, even though the reference turned out to be without pertinence. Another question with respect to proposals for opposition proceedings is that of the relative number of invalid patents that would be avoided and also the amount of litigation that would be avoided. It is the writer's opinion that the number of patents held obviously invalid by reason of a reference that escaped the examiner is relatively small and that the number can be lowered by a personnel policy that provides an adequate complement of competent and experienced examiners. Another question to be weighed in proposals for opposition proceedings is that of the weight to be accorded to the examiner's decision in such opposition proceedings. Problems of laches, estoppel, and res judicata must inevitably arise. If the dangers of repetitious and duplicative controversy are to be avoided, the opposer should necessarily be bound by his actions in the opposition proceedings. This would merely result in transferring the controversy from the court to the already overburdened Patent Office. 

In the consideration of any proposals for modifying the present ex parte nature of the examination process, certain factors should be remembered. The most important is the sheer volume of work done by the Office as explained earlier in this paper (ch. II, sec. B). As has already been suggested, the Office does not have an adequate staff of experienced and competent examiners and probably will not in the foreseeable future. Adequate consideration of a problem in any case requires highly specialized knowledge of the science or art. The decisions of the courts on the same subject matter, after an inter partes presentation, show a diversity of opinions on the question of patentability. The writer is of the opinion that there are relatively few glaring errors of a type that would be caught by a restricted opposition proceedings, compared to the volume of applications examined. (See ch. VII on "Performance".) Having these factors in mind, the writer does not favor opposition proceedings.

K. APPEALS 

An appeal to the Board of Appeals from a final refusal of a patent by the primary examiner is a statutory right (35 U.S.C. sec. 134). 

While the relative proportion of cases appealed is rather smallpossibly less than 7% of all applications filed-the impact of the Board's work on the Office and the patent system is much greater. 

The foremost problem now facing the Board of Appeals is the tremendous workload, which places an unreasonable pressure on all members of the Board and also results in a present delay of almost 2 years from the time an appeal is filed until a decision is reached. 

The Annual Report for the Fiscal Year 1958 shows that over 8,000 appeals were filed during the year and over 6,000 were disposed of, leaving something over 8,000 still pending. About half of the appeals filed are dismissed prior to decision, but the remainder (about 

3,000) constitutes a formidable load. Approximately 70% of the decisions result in affirmance of the examiner's decision, and just under 20% are reversals, the remaining 10% being affirmances in part, i.e., some of the rejected claims are allowed and others are refused. The ratio of affirmances to reversals remains remarkably uniform. The report for October 1959, appearing at 749 O.G. I, shows a total of 295 decisions, with 206 affirmances, 31 affirmances in part, and 58 reversals. There are presently 12 regular members (examiners-in-chief) on the Board of Appeals, and a varying number of temporary members from the Office staff (35 U.S.C. sec. 7). 

The delay that exists encourages the presentation of frivolous appeals by any inventor-applicant wishing to delay the issuance of a patent or the final disposition of an application. An application without merit may be kept pending in the Office for an additional 2 years, and would-be competitors discouraged by marking goods with the term "patent pending" for this additional period of time. The issuance of a patent on proper, allowable claims may be delayed for a like period by the inclusion of obviously nonallowable claims. It is impossible to draw a line between such frivolous appeals and those having some merit.

The delay in rendering decisions also increases the number of legitimate appeals. It has been observed that particular aspects of the interpretation of law periodically give rise to numerous appeals involving substantially the same question. For example, the language employed by the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals in the decision of In re Bremner, 182 F. 2d 216, 37 C.C.P.A. 1032, 638 O.G. 6 (1950), suggested to many examiners in the chemical fields that the Office had not been strict enough in interpreting the statutory requirement for a description of utility. A large number of appeals involving similar aspects of this problem were filed before any decisions were reached by the Board of Appeals, and still further appeals were filed before such decisions became known to the various examiners and the profession. More recently a similar multiplicity of appeals, involving interpretation of new Rule 71 (b) of 1949 and 35 U.S.C. sec. 112, were filed. Attention may be directed to the following decisions of the Board of Appeals, all reported in volume 116 of United States Patent Quarterly and issued in late 1956 and early 

1957: Ex parte Butler, 116 U.S.P.Q. 527 (1956); Ex parte David-

I 

son, 116 U.S.P.Q. 529 (1957); Ex parte Musselman, 116 U.S.P.Q. 

530 (1957); Ex parte Butler, 116 U.S.P.Q. 532 (1957); Ex parte Wilson, 116 U.S.P.Q. 595 (1957); Ex parte Butler, 116 U.S.P.Q. 597 (1957). Similar multiplicity of appeals on questions involving other sections of the 1952 Code have also appeared. 

The unavailability to the examiners and the public of most of the decisions of the Board of Appeals also increases the number of ap peals. The number of appeals to the Court of Customs and Patent 

Appeals is relatively small, so that the examiners and patent attorneys look to the Board (as well as the court) for guidance in interpreting the law. The decisions of the Board which are favorable to the inventor usually result in issuance of a patent, and the decision thus becomes available to the public in the patent file. Furthermore, many of these decisions are published in the United States Patents Quarterly and elsewhere. On the other hand, decisions of the Board which are unfavorable to the inventor do not result in patents unless some of the claims have already been allowed, or unless a further appeal is taken. The usual result of an affirmance by the Board is abandonment of the application. Such abandoned applications are preserved in secrecy (35 U.S.C. sec. 122) with a few exceptions not important here. These decisions of the Board, affirming the primary examiner's action in refusing a patent, and which constitute about 70% of the Board's decisions, are seen only by the examiners of the particular division forwarding the appeal, and are not available for citation as authority even to such examiners. The Administrative 

Procedure Act (5 U.S.C. sec. 1002) includes the following provision: 

Every agency shall publish, or in accordance with published rule, make available to public inspection all final opinions or orders in the adjudication of cases (except those required for good cause to be held confidential and not cited as precedents) and all rules.

This statute has been construed as preventing reliance on Board decisions in pending or abandoned applications. Ex parte Wiseman, 98 U.S.P.Q. 277 (Board Of Appeals 1953). The picture resulting from the available and published decisions of the Board of Appeals is, therefore, a one-sided picture presenting for the most part only those decisions which are favorable to the grant of a patent, thus increasing the number of appeals and the pressure on the examiner for allowance.

The Board members write about 3,000 decisions annually. About 

2,000 of these result in abandoned applications and consequently the decisions are unavailable to the public. A very limited number of these otherwise confidential decisions are published in the Official 

Gazette or United States Patent Quarterly, after securing the inventor's permission. However, this practice poses difficulties. Some inventors refuse the requested permission. Others accord the permission, but refuse to permit general access to the abandoned file for study of the factual situation. This was the case in Ex parte Hutch ins, 99 U.S.P.Q. 288 (Board of Appeals 1953). 

Publication of all of the Board decisions would be useful only if headnotes and indexes were added. This would be a formidable task. 

Publication of only those which involve significant points of proce-

dural or substantive law would be very helpful. Rendering all of the records available under conditions prescribed by 5 U.S.C. sec. 

1002b would also be desirable. It is not possible to forecast what would be the subsequent significance of a decision under the doctrine of stare decisis-a doctrine that is useful in appropriate cases to shorten controversies and ensure equal treatment to all comers. The writer recalls at least three instances in which identical facts were presented in at least two different applications claiming the same invention. In these instances the previous decisions were buried in the confidential files of the Office. There were many more in which the facts and asserted inventions were not identical, but were sufficiently similar to render the doctrine of stare decisis applicable. The actual facts and decisions are often available to the examiner, as in Ex parte Wiseman, supra, but cannot be used by him to dispose of the controversy unless the facts and decisions are available to the public.

There is another factor which tends to overburden the Board of Appeals. The very great overload of work faced by the examining divisions creates excessive pressure toward the final disposition of pending applications. This pressure and the lack of adequate experience of a large number of the assistant examiners presently in the Office results in the appeal of cases which have not been adequately processed initially.

The Board has both original and appellate jurisdiction under the terms of Rule 196, although the scope of original jurisdiction was somewhat limited by Watson v. Bruns, 239 F. 2d 948, 714 O.G. 4 (D.C. Cir. 1956). Inadequate prosecution and processing by both the examiner and counsel for the inventor results in appeals which would have been unnecessary had there been adequate consideration and adequate exposition of views. This inadequacy of prosecution often requires an almost complete reexamination of the record, rather than a mere appellate review of the examiner's decision. Moreover, the inadequacy of prosecution and processing, coupled with the long waiting period before decision and reinforced by the possibilities of review by petition, encourages further attempts to prosecute after the supposedly final decision of the examiner.

The statement of these difficulties suggests certain remedial steps: (1) Retention of competent and experienced assistant examiners will result in more competent prosecution of applications prior to appeal and decrease the number of unnecessary appeals. (2) Steps should be taken to make all of the decisions of the Board available to the public. Since a majority of the Board's decisions, primarily those unfavorable to the inventor, are hidden in abandoned applications, some statutory authority would appear necessary. This authority might take the form of making an exception to the "confidential status" requirement of section 122 of Title 35, United States Code, in cases where applications have become abandoned following a decision of the Board of Appeals. The Office could then take appropriate steps looking toward the publication of either all, or a selected number, of the decisions of the Board of Appeals. All of these decisions would be available for citation, regardless of publication (5 U.S.C. sec. 1002b).

(3) Elsewhere in this paper it has been suggested that the basic fees provided for in paragraph (a) (1), (2), (4), and (6) of 35

U.S.Č. sec. 41 be charged for an application with a single claim, with a significant additional fee for each additional claim. I am convinced that the reasoning in support of this proposal, as applied to filing fees and final fees, is also applicable to appeal fees. Questions presented would be simplified, and frivolous questions minimized, if financial penalties were attached to appeals on a multiplicity of claims. The indigent inventor, with a necessarily simple type of invention, could be heard without undue financial sacrifice. Meritorious appeals could be heard without the presentation of a multiplicity of claims. The writer's experience as a member of the Board of 

Appeals convinced him that the presentation of a multiplicity of claims on appeal invariably involved the inclusion of frivolous, unnecessary, or repetitious questions. While some of the additional or unnecessary claims could easily be disposed of by determination of a single controlling issue, every additional claim required some additional work. The Board is overburdened with work. A fee system of the type suggested would eliminate at least some of the additional unnecessary effort.

IX. SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS 

This paper presents a description of the examination system as presently administered by the Patent Office. It would appear that detailed knowledge of the present methods is required for any intelligent consideration of proposed alterations. The immensity of the task and the detailed specialized skills employed are seldom fully realized. Only such statistical information as is necessary to illustrate the problems has been included. References may be had to the 

Commissioner's annual reports for more complete statistical information. The law has been referred to only to the extent necessary to illustrate the various aspects of the examination system, and is in no sense a complete exposition of any particular aspect thereof. The activities of members of the patent profession have been considered only to the extent that such activities relate to the examination process.

In an examination system for the grant of patents the ideal goals include the following: (1) a complete search of all prior art developments; (2) the citation and intelligent application of such prior art to the claims of the application for patent; (3) a consistent and sustainable determination of the patentable merit of each claim for invention presented; (4) a speedy final disposition of each application for a patent. Each resulting patent should include (a) a clear and adequate description of the asserted invention, and (b) clear and definite claims of "determinable scope, defining inventive subject matter.

The sheer volume of the task, budgetary restrictions, and the limitations of human nature prevent complete attainment of the goals suggested. Considering all of these factors, it is believed that the Office does remarkably well. At the same time, it is also urged that further improvement can be attained by adopting the changes here suggested. 

The proposed changes do not involve any fundamental changes in the present examination system. 63416 0-61——5

A complete, so-called "validity search" is out of the question. 

However, the goal of an adequate search can best be achieved through extension of library facilities, a more adequate classification force, and retention of a larger proportion of competent and experienced examiners.

Retention of experienced and competent examiners will also promote more intelligent comparison of the prior art developments with the claims.

The problem of sustainable determination of patentable merit will also be met in part by retention of an able and experienced staff. 

However, this problem cannot be wholly met by procedural changes in the examination system. During the writer's service as a member of the Board of Appeals, differing views were often expressed by members of a panel considering a particular record. While such differing views were usually reconciled by further discussion, having in mind the rule that doubts must be resolved in favor of the inventor, dissenting opinions were presented in a significant number of cases. 

Reports of the Court of Customs and Patent Appeals also frequently show differences of opinion on the questions of patentability. Attention may be directed to In re Ratti, supra. The reports of the district courts and Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia also show diversity of views in proceedings under 35 U.S.C. sec. 145. The decisions of the Federal courts after a full inter partes consideration of the validity of a patent also show a diversity of views as to patentability. Reference may be had to Great Atlantic and Pacific Tea Co. v. Supermarket Equipment Corp., supra.

The problem of definition of patentability or invention and possible changes in substantive law on this question are beyond the scope of this paper. It is trite to observe that the terms are not capable of exact definition. Some improvement might be attained by attempts at further statutory definition. Whether the standards of patentability should be different from those presently applied by the courts is a matter of policy outside the scope of this paper. Statutory definition will tend to decrease areas of conflict, but with a subject as abstruse as this there will inevitably be areas of conflict. Under our system of laws, every person is afforded an opportunity to be heard before a final ruling on anything impinging on his interests is entered. It appears that our citizens are more inclined to question rulings of governmental authorities than are the citizens of most other countries. 

Enactment of stricter standards will not eliminate the presentation of applications that are obviously without merit. The Office has uniformly refused to allow patents for perpetual motion devices (Ex parte Payne, 1904 C.D. 42), yet applications for patents on such devices are still filed.

A speedy disposition of applications for patents is an obviously desirable aim both from the standpoint of the industries involved and the public interest. In view of the very high degree of specialization and also the complexity of the examination system, a reduction of the gap to about 90 days is probably the best that can be done. Other than the obvious step of retaining an adequate force of experienced examiners, the proposal here suggested for a restriction in the number of claims and for a more specific description would expedite consideration of necessary issues. The time restrictions on priority showings

and the filing of additional applications would also contribute to a speedy disposition.

The obviously desirable characteristics of the resulting patent can be more nearly achieved through the statutory changes here suggested. 

With the increasing complexity of the arts it seems more desirable to replace the old and indeterminate test of supposed "skill of the art" with definite requirements for a full exposition of the particular invention developed. Restriction in the number of claims will tend to focus attention on the smaller number of claims. 

In conclusion it should be again noted that the writer believes that the present patent system has been a vitally significant factor in the industrial leadership of the United States. This paper is devoted primarily to an exposition of the present system. The changes proposed will improve the operation of the system without significantly altering it.
