APPENDIX E COMMENTARY ON THE DOHRN AND DIEDRICH FILE WRAPPER (APPENDIX F, INFRA) 

All of the papers relating to a particular application for patent are filed together. On allowance of the application, this set of papers becomes the patent file and is retained by the Office during the life of the patent. In any litigation on the patent, a complete copy of these papers is invariably presented in the court and referred to as the "file wrapper." A copy of the complete record of the patent to Dohrn and Diedrich, No. 2,345,384, issued March 23, 1944, is reproduced herewith (Appendix F, infra). This file may illustrate the proceedings in the Patent Office looking toward the allowance of a patent. 

It cannot be said to be typical because every file seems to be unique. It was selected because the writer was familiar with the file and because the patent was subsequently involved in litigation. 

In the ensuing commentary the page citations are to the "file wrapper," while the references in parentheses are to the page numbers of the printed appendix.

A. PROSECUTION OF THE APPLICATION: CONTENTS OF THE FILE WRAPPER 

1. The file covers 99 Page I (p. 91) of the copy of the file is taken from the face of the cover or file wrapper. (The word "cover" is used instead of "file wrapper" to avoid confusion with the technical meaning of the last-quoted term which is used to include the contents.) The front cover includes information primarily of clerical and indexing importance. The application was given a serial number 328,236 of the series of 1935. It is stamped "return to record room.' All patent files are retained in this "record room," which is a branch of the Patent Office Library. 

The patent number and the date of issue were stamped on the file at the appropriate time. The typewritten entries were placed on the cover at the time the application was filed. These entries include the names of the inventors, their residences, the title of the invention, and the name and address of the attorney. As the cover shows, the com plete application was filed April 6, 1940, and included a petition, specification, oath, and the first fee of $30, but no drawings. The designation "Germany, May 20, 1939" at the bottom of the cover indicates that a corresponding application was filed in Germany on the indicated date and presumably that the inventors were entitled to the benefit of that date under 35 U.S.C. sec. 119. Entries after filing are made with a rubber stamp or in longhand. The first of these following the term "DIV." shows the number 59 crossed out and followed by the number 6. This indicates that the application was first sent to Division 59 and then transferred to Division 6. The entry on the opposite side indicates the place of recording in the examiner's books. 63416 0-61——6 73

These books have now been superseded by a duplicate card index system. The name of the assignee, Schering Corporation, immediately follows the names of the inventors. The assignment is recorded in the 

Office and the date of this record is also placed on a portion of the cover not here reproduced. The cover includes a notation "Examined and passed for issue, December 1, 1943," followed by the signature of the primary examiner of Division 6. This signature constitutes the sole official action taken by the examiner on finding that the application is in condition for issuance as a patent. The following line shows that the notice of allowance, which is a circular letter, was mailed on December 1, 1943. Further entries show that four claims were allowed, and that claim four was selected as illustrative for publication in the Official Gazette. The patent was to be classified in Class 

260-520. The particular claim for publication in the Official Gazette is one selected by the examiner as illustrative or descriptive of the invention involved. Each weekly issue of the Official Gazette includes an identification of each of the patents issued that week, including the name of the inventor, the title of the invention, and an illustrative claim. It also includes a copy of one of the figures of the drawing where the application includes drawings. The final entry on the cover is that of the title of the invention as allowed. In the present instance, the title is the same as that used by the inventors in filing the application. Under appropriate circumstances the title may be modified by the inventor or at the insistence of the examiner. The cover also shows that a disclaimer was filed on February 18, 1946. 

This disclaimer will be further discussed in connection with a discussion of the events leading to the disclaimer. 

2. Back cover

The back cover of the file wrapper, page II (p. 92), is used as an index of the contents of the file. Each indexed paper is given a number, its contents are designated, and the date of receipt or mailing is entered. During examination of the application, a glance at the last significant entry on this cover will show the condition of the application. All of the formal papers constituting the complete application, including a letter of transmittal, the petition, specification, and oath are lumped together as No. 1 and merely checked, being indexed on the front cover. The first rejection, paper No. 2, is dated August 14, 1940, a little over 4 months after filing the application. This is a shorter period than now obtains in Division 6. The inventors responded thereto on February 13, 1941, the period lacking 1 day of the 6 months allowed for response. This paper is entered as No. 3 and designated as amendment "A." (In addition to receiving a number, as do all the pages entered in the file, each successive amendatory paper is also identified by an alphabetical letter to facilitate indexing the changes as entered in the body of the specification.) This paper was followed by subsequent amendments designated "B" and "C" and numbered 

4 and 5 and dated February 17 and May 5, 1941, respectively. The application was reconsidered by the examiner and another rejection, paper No. 6, was mailed on July 17, 1941, a little over 4 months after the first amendment of February 13, 1941. The first of the amendments is a controlling date. This period is again less than that presently required for the reconsideration in Division 6. This rejection was answered by a paper filed January 16, 1942, and designated amendment "D." The time taken for response was again 1 day less

=d 40 than the permitted 6 months. The application was again considered by the examiner and on June 1, 1942, he mailed a final rejection, 42 months after receiving the inventor's latest response.

The final rejection presumably closes the prosecution of the application before the examiner, but the index on the back cover now being considered shows that there was an exchange of letters, papers 9 and 

10; a further amendment and an appeal, paper 11; another rejection, paper 12; a telegram and a further amendment, paper 13; and another final rejection, paper 14. This final rejection was followed by affidavits and amendments, papers 15 and 16; and an appeal, paper 17. 

The last-mentioned paper, No. 17, is dated November 26, 1943, exactly

G 6 months after the final rejection. As already indicated, the front cover shows that the application was formally allowed on December 1, 1943, and the entries subsequent to that time involve papers filed after allowance or after patenting. The contents of these papers will be explained later. 

3. Search notes

The inside flap of the file cover is reproduced as page III (p. 93). 

This page provides a table for a record of the search and also a numerical index of claims. The field of search covered includes subclass 520 of class 260. This is the proper subclass for the compounds claimed, as shown by subsequent classification of the patent. The successive significant class, subgroup, and subclass titles, each subordinate under the preceding title, was as follows: 

class 260-CHEMISTRY, CARBON COMPOUNDS -350 CARBOCÝCLIC OR ACYCLIC 

-500-Acids or salts thereof -514- -Carboxylic acids -515- -520- -521- 

-Aromatic 

-Oxy 

-Monocyclic 

Those familiar with organic chemistry will immediately recognize that the compounds defined in the claims, discussed later, possess qualifications coming within the titles listed, down to 520, but do not qualify for the next subordinate class, 521.

The files of subclass 520 in the division should include all U.S. patents relating to compounds within its title and more detailed definition. Foreign patents from England, France, Germany, Switzerland, Austria, Australia, and the Scandinavian countries are also included.

In addition to searching the prior patents the examiner also searched the two available general indexes of organic compounds, "Beilstein" with its "Supplement" and "Chemical Abstracts," indicating volume or index heading. The search notes were completed by adding the date and initials of the assistant examiner. 

4. Claim index

The index of claims is simply a column of numbers. A line is drawn below the highest claim number presented. The numerals of canceled claims are crossed out. As thus shown, 11 claims were originally presented, claims 12, 13, and 14 were submitted with amendment "A," and so on: A total of 34 claims were ultimately presented, although all but claims 6, 7, 15, and 34 were eventually deleted. As

will be noted later, a mistake would have been avoided had the examiner noted this claim index at the time he made the final rejection of June 1, 1942, assuming that it had been kept current by the division clerk. 

5. The application papers 

The application papers include a letter of transmittal referring to the payment of the $30 filing fee. This paper is wholly superfluous, but is usually included. The petition includes the names, residences, and post office addresses of the two inventors and requests that a patent be granted. The addresses are required so that direct communication may be had with the inventors should circumstances require it. A power of attorney is generally included and is in this instance included with the petition. The petition is followed by an introductory paragraph referring to the specification. The letter of transmittal, the petition and power of attorney are not printed as part of the patent and are not significant parts of the record, and therefore are not given regular page numbers, being numbered 1/4 (p. 96) and 12 (p. 97), respectively. 

6. The description 

Pages 1 (p. 98) to 6 (p. 103) show the description of the invention.

Attention should, at the present time, be confined to the original typewriting without noting the handwritten changes. These longhand revisions were entered by the division clerk, the examiner, or the printer and will be explained later. The description on page 1 (p. 98) includes a reference to prior art developments, including a U.S. patent. 

This is followed by a broad, general statement of the asserted invention, including a general statement of the class of compounds discovered by the inventors and possessing good qualities as contrast agents for taking X-ray pictures of the gall bladder. This general statement is followed by eight specific examples, each describing a specific method of making a particular specific compound. Since examples 1 and 3 describe different methods of making the same compound, the specification describes methods of making seven different specific and related compounds, all said to be useful for the same purpose. All of the examples relate to the addition of iodine to otherwise known organic compounds. The paragraph bridging pages 5 (p. 102) and 6 (p. 103) suggests that other methods of adding iodine may be em- ployed, citing a textbook for such methods. The next paragraph, on page 6 (p. 103), gives the dosage required for use as an X-ray contrast agent for the gall bladder and states the time required before the X-ray picture may be taken. The concluding paragraph of the specification expresses, in substance, a hope that the patent will embrace obvious variants of the invention disclosed.

Most of the revisions entered in longhand (in red ink in the original) have been entered by the clerk of the division. The authority for the entry, indicated in the margin as "per A," refers to amendment "A" which is paper 3 of the contents of the application. Page 1 (p. 

98) also shows that the examiner corrected a misspelling. Other entries have been made by the printer. The printing of patent specifications which involve chemical names and chemical formulas presents problems to the printer and the proofs are transmitted to the examiner to check the printing.

7. The original claims 

The specification of the application included 11 claims found on pages 7 (p. 104), 8 (p. 105), and 9 (p. 106) of the reproduction (35

U.S.C. sec. 112). Attention should be directed to the original typewritten text without at present noting the handwritten changes. 

Claims purport to define the scope of the invention to be protected by the patent. Claims 1, 2, 3, and 4 relate to the process of manufacturing the chemical compounds. Claim 1 is in generic form, referring to a class of starting material and describing the process as a single step of "iodizing." Claims 2, 3, and 4 are in form dependent on claim 

1 and name specific different starting materials to be iodized. Claims 

5 to 11 are directed to products. Claim 5 is in generic form, describing a class of compounds containing two or four iodine atoms and one or two hydroxyl radicals. Claims 6, 7, 8, and 9 are directed individually to specific compounds, all coming within the broad terms of claim 5. 

These claims employ conventional chemical language to identify the specific compound involved. Claim 10 is a generic claim of scope similar to claim 5, but refers to the compounds as "X-ray contrast agents for making the gall bladder visible." The scope of the claim is otherwise identical to that of claim 5. Claim 11 is similar to claim 10 and differs therefrom only in referring to "a salt of" the compounds otherwise defined in claim 10.

A study of the claim structure suggests that process claims 1, 2, 3, and 4 and product claims 10 and 11 are unnecessary. The process claims constitute a bare recital in functional terms of a conventional step of adding iodine to known compounds. Product claims alone would protect the patentee by excluding others from making the product (35 U.S.C. sec. 154). Moreover, the general process "iodizing" is conceded to be a conventional method, as shown by the paragraph bridging pages 5 (p. 102) and 6 (p. 103) of the description. Claim 10 differs from claim 5 solely in its inclusion of an introductory clause stating the intended use of the otherwise described product. This clause is without significant value. In re Thuau, 30 C.C.P.A. 979 (1943). Claim 11 differs from claim 10 by reference to "salts of” the class of compound otherwise described in claim 10. The salts cannot be obtained without first obtaining the compounds themselves. It should be evident that all of the subject matter of all of the original 11 claims is adequately encompassed in the five claims, numbered 5 to 

9, inclusive.

This record shows that the original claims were in part modified by a subsequent amendment and afterward all claims except claims 6 and 7 were removed. Original claims 6 and 7 became claims 1 and 2 of the patent. 

8. The oath

The final page of the application papers, page 10 (p. 107), includes the signatures of the inventors and the required oath. This oath meets the requirements of the statutes, now 35 U.S.C. secs. 115 and 116, and the more specific requirements of the rules, now Rules 65 and 66. The oath gives the citizenship and residence of each of the joint applicants. It refers to the subject matter of the application and asserts that the applicants are the inventors and that the subject matter complies with the formal requirement for a patent. The oath asserts that there has been no description in a printed publication more than

2 years prior to the filing of the application. (The application was filed prior to the effective date of the amendment of August 5, 1939, which reduced the time limitation set forth in R.S. 4886 to 1 year.)

The oath also identifies two applications for patent filed in Germany prior to the filing of the U.S. application. Reference to the earlier filed foreign applications is required if one wishes to obtain the priority benefits of the International Convention for the Protection of 

Industrial Property, under the then applicable statute R.S. 4887.

The codified statute (35 U.S.C. sec. 119) now requires a certified copy of the foreign application, together with a translation into the English language, to obtain the priority benefits. 

9. The first rejection

Paper No. 2, pages 11 (p. 108) and 12 (p. 109), reports the result of the examiner's first consideration of the application. The search had failed to reveal any significant references and accordingly the examiner reported that no pertinent art had been found. The first two paragraphs and the last paragraph of this paper, page 11 (p. 108), are taken up with errors in language and include suggested corrections. 

The objection to the use of the alternative clauses in claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 is based on the premise that each claim must constitute a unitary definition of inventive subject matter. The use of alternatives very often imparts ambiguity or indefiniteness to the definition. The discussion of claims 10 and 11 "as being directed to the obvious use of the compounds of the invention" is not as clear as might be but expresses the same concept as was later developed in the In re Thuau case, supra. 

Two distinct reasons were advanced for the rejection of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 as being too broad. One of these reasons is based on the definition of X in these claims and the other on the failure to define the position of the hydroxyl groups and the iodine groups on the two nuclei of the structural formula presented. Each of these claims included two hexagons which are representations of the benzene nucleus. 

Different compounds result from different positioning of the substituents, such as the hydroxyl (OH) and iodine on the nucleus. The final portion of the examiner's action holds that the claims are directed to five distinct species of the asserted invention and requires restriction to not more than three species and tentative election of a single species. 

At the time of pendency of the Dohrn, et al., application, the rules permitted claims to a maximum of three distinct and mutually exclusive species, but only in the presence of an allowable generic claim inclusive of those species. In the absence of the allowable generic claim, the claims were all required to be readable on a single species. The initials of the assistant examiner who actually prepared the action, and the signature of the primary examiner who takes the responsibility for the action, are found near the bottom of page 12 (p. 109). This original paper with the examiner's signature is retained in the Office and becomes a part of the file. A carbon copy is mailed to the attorney for the inventor. A mailing stamp shows the date of this action. 

10. Amendment "A" 

On February 12, 1941, just 2 days short of the permitted 6 months, the inventors, through their attorneys, presented a response to the examiner's rejection, pages 13 (p. 110) to 17 (p. 114). This response becomes paper No. 3 and is also designated amendment "A." The

paper proposes numerous minor corrections in the description and also in some of the claims. Most of these corrections are entered in longhand by the clerk of the division, the authority for the change being marked in the margin as "per A." (Changes authorized by amendment "B" are marked "per B" and the origin of each succeeding change likewise designated by reference to the letter assigned to the amendatory paper.) If the proposed modification involves rewriting or the addition of more than a few words, it is identified as an "insert 

A-1," "A-2," etc., with appropriate marking. (Inserts ordered by amendment "B" are correspondingly identified as "insert B-1," etc.)

It will be seen that definitions of the generic claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 were rewritten to meet some of the examiner's criticism of the form. 

Claims 8 and 9 were removed and three new claims, Nos. 12, 13, and 

14, were added. The removal of claims 8 and 9 removed the claims to species in excess of the permissible three. A short explanation of the proposed changes is submitted and counsel devotes most of the remainder of the paper, pages 15 (p. 112), 16 (p. 113), and 17 (p. 117), to arguments to the effect that the rejections on the merits are in error and should be withdrawn. There is an election of a particular species, namely that of process claim 3, but there appears to be some error in the election of a product claim which was corrected by a subsequent communication. 

11. Amendment “B”

Shortly after filing the above discussed amendment "A" (paper 3), counsel for the inventors discovered an error in their paper and filed the supplemental amendment marked "B" and designated paper No. 4, page 18 (p. 115), correcting errors in their earlier paper. This amendment corrected the confusion in the election of the single species of claim and was entered by the clerk of the division. 

12. Amendment "C"

On May 5, 1941, a further amendment was received which became paper 5 of the record, pages 19 (p. 116) and 20 (p. 117), and was designated amendment "C." This paper proposed the removal of claims 

4 and 12 and the addition of two new claims 15 and 16. The change removed one of the three permissible species and substituted another without stating a reason for this substitution. Under the circumstances, the substitution had no significant effect on the examination procedure and was permitted. 

13. The second rejection

In paper 6, page 21 (p. 118), a copy of which was mailed July 17, 1941, the examiner reconsidered the application as amended and reiterated his rejection of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 as being indefinite and too broad in the definition of the term "X." Claims 10 and 11 were also again rejected by reason of the statement of use, it being said that the claims failed to distinguish from claim 5, which described the same compounds but omitted the statement of use. The Thuau decision, discussed above, was not then available. Since no generic claim was allowable, claims 2, 6, 15, and 16 addressed to nonelected species were refused consideration. Claim 7 restricted to a single chemical compound was said to be allowable. This paper was signed by an acting examiner. The former primary examiner of Division 6 retired in 

April 1941 and the assistant chief performed the duties of primary

examiner during the interim until a successor was appointed. The successor was appointed in August 1941 and it will be noted that all of the subsequent official papers were signed by him. It may well be that some of the inconsistency and changes of view expressed by the examiner in this file were due to these changes in the primary exam iner of Division 6. 

14. The fourth amendment 

On January 16, 1942, the inventors filed their response to the previous Office action (paper 7). This response included a rewriting of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 as claims 17, 18, 19, and 20, pages 22 (p. 119) and 23 (p. 120) of the file. In response to the rejection for indefiniteness and undue breadth, it was asserted that the rewritten claims were definite and commensurate in scope with the description. An argument is presented with respect to the merit of the rewritten use claims, now numbered 19 and 20. 

15. Final rejection 

A third rejection was entered on June 1, 1942 (paper 8). The examiner again reconsidered the application and reiterated the previous rejections. His decision is found in paper 8, page 25 (p. 122), of the file. The rejection was made final. A rejection may be made final only if it reiterates previous rejections and does not introduce new reasons for refusal of the claims. The present rule (Rule 113, adopted 

March 1949) is more detailed and precise than the former rule (Rule 

68). The examiner is required to "repeat or state all grounds of rejection then considered applicable to the claims in the case, clearly stating the reasons therefor. 

16. Correction letters On September 3, 1942, the attorney wrote a letter calling attention to a mistake in the enumeration of claims in the previous official action and requested information or a correction. This letter was entered in the file as paper 9, page 26 (p. 123). The examiner immediately entered a response correcting the error in enumeration of the claims and stated that the statutory 6 months for response would run from the date of the letter. This letter was entered in the file as paper 10, page 

27 (p. 124). 

17. Appeal and proposed amendment 

On March 8, 1943, exactly 6 months after the correcting letter, an appeal was filed seeking reversal of the decision of the primary examiner finally rejecting claims Nos. 17, 18, 19, and 20. The appeal includes six specific assignments of error. No. 6 is a premature assignment of error referring to the examiner's expected refusal to substitute claim 21 for finally rejected claims 17, 18, 19, and 20. An amendatory paper proposing this substitution was filed with the appeal. This paper also proposed the addition of new claims 22, 23, 24, 25, 26, 27, and 28, and the cancellation of claims 2, 3, and 16. This amendatory paper includes a reference to an interview with the primary and assistant examiner. It is said that at the interview the primary examiner indicated that claims of the form of proposed claims 21 and 22 might be allowable. An extended argument is presented as to the merit of proposed claims 23 to 28. This appeal and proposed amendment are on file as paper 11, pages 28-36 (pp. 125-133).

Notwithstanding the proposed amendment, the appeal (together with the appeal fee) was necessary to avoid possible abandonment of the application. Amendments after final rejection do not stay the running of the statutory period of 6 months for appeal, unless the examiner formally withdraws the finality of the previous rejection within the period, or it can be later established (by petition) that the final rejection was premature or incomplete (former Rule 68, now

Rule 116). The risk of abandonment is avoided by filing the formal appeal. 

18. A new rejection

On March 20, 1943, which is shortly after the presentation of the appeal and the accompanying proposed amendments, the examiner entered a new rejection. Paper 12, pages 37 (p. 134) and 38 (p. 135).

This paper withdrew the previous final rejection and added a new reference, to wit, a patent issued on July 1, 1941, and thus not available at the time of the first rejection of August 14, 1940. With the withdrawal of the final rejection, the proposed amendment of March 8, 1943, accompanying the appeal was entered as amendment "E." Claim 

7 was stated to be allowable. Claims 6 and 15 and new claim 27 were rejected as not inclusive of the elected species. It was pointed out that claim 27 was a new species in excess of the then permitted 

3. Claims 13, 14, and 21 to 28 were rejected as unpatentable over the new reference, it being stated that the reference compounds were similar to the compounds covered by the claims. Claims 21 to 26 and 28 were also rejected as in improper form. This is a highly technical form of claim developed in chemical cases, wherein an artificial class of substances is created by enumerating operable members, thus avoiding some of the difficulties of generic language which would be inclusive of inoperative substances. Ex parte Markush, 1925 

C.D. 126 (Commissioner). In the paper under discussion, the examiner held that the claims as presented did not conform to proper practice in this respect. Claims 21 to 25 and 28 were also rejected as too broad. A shortened statutory period for response was set with the approval of the supervisory examiner. 

19. Amendment "F"

On the last day of the shortened period, May 13, 1943, the inventors responded with a new amendment which became paper 13, pages 

39 (p. 136) to 45 (p. 142), of the record. Page 45 (p. 145) is a copy of a telegram also received on May 13, 1943. This telegram proposes the cancellation of certain claims, proposes in general language to limit other claims, and requests reconsideration. It is evident that the attorneys feared that the formal response would not reach the Office in time and attempted this stopgap method of presenting a reply within the permitted period. This practice, or expedient, is approved if the text of the telegram constitutes a reasonably adequate response and if it is followed by a confirming letter over the attorneys' signature. The present practice with respect to telegraphic responses is set forth in section 714.08 of the "Manual of Patent Examining Practice." In the present case, it is evident that the text of the telegram did not constitute a response of the character required, but any question with respect to the telegram was avoided because the formal paper was received within the permitted time. The formal paper presented an extensive

rewriting of claims and an extensive argument concerning the form of the claims and also concerning the rejection of the newly cited patent. 

20. A new final rejection

On May 26, 1943, the examiner entered a new final rejection. Paper 

14, pages 46 (p. 143) and 47 (p. 144). In this paper the examiner summarizes and reiterates the rejections previously entered. He also refers to the then recently decided Thuau case as authority for the rejection of claims referring to a particular use as without patentable significance over claims merely describing the compounds. 

21. Affidavits

On November 6, 1943, two affidavits were presented which became a part of paper 15, pages 48-55 (pp. 145-152). These affidavits stated some of the qualities of the claimed compounds and compared the qualities of these compounds with those of the cited Guerbet patent. 

It was said that the compounds of the cited patent were X-ray contrast media for the kidneys and that those of the claimed compounds were for the gall bladder. It was further asserted that the claimed compounds pass into the gall bladder and that the compounds of the patent, being much more soluble, pass into the kidneys. 

22. Amendment "G"

The affidavit was accompanied by a further paper proposing the extensive amendment of a number of claims and the rewriting of claim 25 as new claim 34. An extensive argument, pages 58-64 (pp. 155161), is also presented, reviewing the subject matter of the claims, the propriety of the definitions used, and the merit of such claims, and referring to the affidavits. 

23. Amendment "H" 

On November 25, 1943, a new amendment (paper 16) was filed, proposing the cancellation of claims 21 and 29-33, inclusive. This paper acknowledges an interview with the primary examiner and refers to the affidavits and the previous arguments. Claim 34 was the only generic claim retained in the application. 

24. The second appeal 

On November 26, 1943, the last day for response to the previous final rejection, a new notice of appeal was filed. This notice was accompanied by 10 formal assignments of error. Paper 17, pages 67 (p. 164) and 68 (p. 165). As was stated in connection with paper 11, an appeal was necessary to avoid abandonment, in the absence of a formal action by the examiner within the statutory period for response. 

25. Allowance

The next paper formally entered in the record is a circular notice of allowance, page 72 (p. 169). This paper identifies the application and states the number of claims in the application as allowed. It is not signed by the examiner and includes advice as to the payment of the final fee. As has already been stated, the only formal action of the primary examiner on allowance of an application is his signature on the face cover of the file.

I 26. Proposed amendments after allowance 

Paper 18, pages 69 (p. 166) and 70 (p. 167), presented on February 17, 1944, proposes the modification of allowed claims 6, 7, and 15 by removing the reference to the particular melting points. The amendment was presented under the provisions of old Rule 78, now Rule 312, which permits amendments after allowance on recommendation of the primary examiner and approval of the Commissioner. Such amendments ordinarily cover matters of form and editorial corrections. By paper 19, page 71 (p. 168), the examiner refused to recommend entry of the proposed amendment. The examiner's adverse recommendation was approved by the Assistant Commissioner. 

27. Letter 

On February 26, 1944, a letter was received and entered as paper 

20, pages 73 (p. 170) and 74 (p. 171). This letter protested the reasoning of the examiner in his refusal to recommend entry of the proposed amendment under Rule 78. The letter constitutes only an assertion that the examiner's reasoning was in error and no action was taken by the examiner. However, as noted, the paper was entered as a part of the record. 

28. Final fee

The final fee of $30 was paid on March 1, 1944, as shown by the circular reproduced as page 75 (p. 172). The payment of the final fee completed all of the requirements for issuance of the patent, and the patent was issued on March 28, 1944. The official copy is mailed to the inventor or assignee on the issue date. This date is the beginning of the 17-year monopoly, which in the present instance will end on March 28, 1961. The printed copy of the specification, drawings (if any), and claims constitute the usual "patent," which may be purchased from the Office by anyone. The printed copy is reproduced at pages 

IV (p. 94) and V (p. 95). The patent shows the specification and claims as the record stood at allowance, without identifying the changes made during prosecution. The information for the heading is taken from the front cover of the file, page I (p. 91). 

29. Jurisdiction of patent

With the issuance of the patent, the Office loses general jurisdiction of the subject matter. The Office, after an interference proceeding, may rule that some person other than the patentee is the first inventor of the subject matter claimed and issue another patent on this subject matter to the other party. This is an indirect holding that the first patent is invalid. The Office preserves the entire record of the application for patent, which on issuance of the patent becomes a public record. However, the Office does not have jurisdiction to inquire into the possible validity of the patent. 

30. The infringement suit 

The statute requires the clerks of the Federal courts to notify the 

Commissioner of Patents of the filing of any infringement suit, identifying the parties and the patent. Similar notices are required if other patents are included in the suit as by amendment, cross bill, etc. (now 

35 U.S.C. sec. 290; formerly R. S. 4921, as amended February 18, 1922). These notices are entered in the patent file, being given a paper number and indexed on the back flap.

Four such notices are found in the patent file under consideration, entered as papers 21, 22, 23, and 27, pages 76, 77, 78, and 83 (pp. 173, 174, 175, and 180).

The first of these is a notice that a suit was filed in the District Court for the Southern District of New York on April 22, 1944, on behalf of Schering Corp., owners of the patent, against partners, Gilbert and Bell.

The second notice, page 77 (p. 174), is of the filing of an action, obviously an appeal, in the Circuit Court of Appeals for the Second

Circuit. The United States is also named as a party. 

The third notice, page 78 (p. 175), briefly indicates a decision of the circuit court as "Affirmed in part; modified and remanded." This decision is reported at 68 U.S.P.Q.84 (1946).

The last of these notices, page 83 (p. 180), reports the final judgment of the district court as "in favor of pltf. sustaining patent, adjudging that deft. has infringed upon said patent, and granting an injunction restraining deft. (claim 4 invalid)."

The original opinion of the district court was reported at 67 J.S.P.Q. 42 (1945). Only claim 2 of the patent (claim 7 of the original application), describing a single specific compound, was pleaded. There was no real contest as to infringement nor as to the utility of the compound. The defendant referred to a supposed concession, by the patentees in their corresponding German patent, of a bromine analog of the claimed compound, known since 1908, and asserted that the relationship established absence of invention. The court did not sustain this defense. The patentee's prior patents, Nos. 

1,905,543 and 2,016,104, were also referred to, but not discussed at length. It does not appear that any significant references were found. 

The defendant urged that claim 4, not pleaded in the suit, should be held invalid as covering more than the patentees had invented. 

The court refused to rule on the validity of this claim. Claim 4 is the only generic claim of the patent and was presented as claim 34, paper 15, page 57 (p. 154), in one of the last efforts to frame a satisfactory generic claim and avoid going on with the appeal of the Board of Appeals. 

The court of appeals (68 U.S.P.Q. 84) agreed with the district court as to validity and infringement of claim 2, but disagreed with the disposition of claim 4. It was held that the validity of claim 4 should be determined, and further that claim 4 was invalid as including more than the patentees had invented. The definition of "Z" as a "trivalent hydrocarbon residue of the acetic acid series" was said to cover a host of possible compounds far beyond the field explored by the inventors.

Judge Frank, presenting a separate opinion, agreed with the majority as to the invalidity of claim 4, but contended that claim 2 was also invalid. He suggested that a "mere product" or substance in the form of a chemical compound could not be the subject matter of a valid patent. 

31. Disclaimer

The holding of invalidity as to claim 4 required the patentee to file a disclaimer as to this claim (R.S. 4917; now 35 U.S.C. sec. 253). A failure to file a disclaimer within a reasonable time might have invalidated the entire patent under R.S. 4922. The corresponding

I 1 section of the 1952 act (35 U.S.C. sec. 288) does not require a prompt disclaimer of invalid claims as a condition to recovering on valid claims.

A disclaimer of claim 4 was filed February 18, 1946, shortly after the decision of the court of appeals holding the claim invalid. This was entered in the file as paper 24, pages 79 (p. 176) and 80 (p. 177), together with the transmittal letter tendering the required fee, paper 25, page 81 (p. 178). An Office letter acknowledging the disclaimer was entered as paper 26, page 82 (p. 179). The disclaimer was filed on behalf of the Schering Corporation, owners of the patent. 

32. Title report 

The final paper in the record, page 84 (p. 181), is a title report, dated February 28, 1946, and showing ownership in the Schering Corporation. This paper was not indexed or given a number. It was necessitated by the disclaimer as only a party holding an interest in the patent may file an effective disclaimer. The Assignment Division of the Office records all instruments transferring ownership of all or a part of a patent or of an application for patent. 

B. COMMENTARY ON THE EXAMINATION PROCESS 

1. Elapsed time

The examiner formally considered the Dohrn, et al., application six separate times (papers 2, 6, 8, 12, and 14 and the allowance). This is nearly twice the average of 3 to 31½ considerations per application. 

The elapsed time from filing (April 6, 1940) to the date of issue (March 23, 1944) is also greater than the average time. The waiting time, after filing, for the first consideration, of a trifle over 4 months is much less than the more than 72 months required in Division 6 in 1959 (see App. B). The waiting time for the second and third considerations after response by the inventors-about 5 months in each instance is also less than the 712 months now required. Every action taken by the examiner after the third rejection was made promptly and without any waiting period other than that required for normal processing, such as study of the file, conference with the primary examiner, preparation of the paper, typing, signing, and mailing. In each of these instances the time was less than 2 weeks. 

The total waiting time in the Office prior to allowance was about 15 months. There was an additional 3 weeks after payment of the final fee, for printing and other formalities of issuing the patent. 

However, there is an additional delay, clearly chargeable to the 

Office. The "final" rejection of June 1, 1942, contained a mistake as to the enumeration of the claims rejected. The attorney's letter of 

September 3, 1942, paper 9, page 26 (p. 123), called attention to this mistake, and it was corrected September 8, 1942. The mistake necessitated a new statutory period running from the date of correction.

Accordingly, of the total of slightly less than 4 years pendency, 19 months must be charged to the Office. 

2. Attorneys' delays

It will be noted that in every instance, except for the payment of the final fee, the attorney's response was filed at substantially the end of the permitted period. In each instance this was 6 months except

for the shortened period set for response to the rejection of March 20, 1943. The final fee was submitted 3 months after the notice of allowance. The total time thus chargeable to the inventors was approximately 2812 months. The effective date of the application was the German filing date of May 20, 1939 (R.S. 4887; now 35 U.S.C. sec. 119), if it be assumed that the German application disclosed all of the subject matter. The total time thus chargeable to the inventors after the first effective filing of an application for a patent was about 

3 years and 3 months. 

3. Performance 

The above discussion of the time element completely ignores any questions of character or adequacy of the work of the examiner or that of the attorneys. No attempt will be made to review the merits of the questions presented, but it is believed desirable to comment briefly on the character of the papers. 

As was previously observed, there were three different chiefs in charge of Division 6 during prosecution of the application. The first incumbent retired in April 1941. The assistant chief acted as primary examiner until the appointment of a new primary examiner in August 1941. Under well-settled practice, a new primary examiner as well as any assistant attempts to carry on without disturbing the actions of his predecessor.

It is evident from paper 12 withdrawing the previous final rejection and citing a reference for the first time, that the entire file had been reviewed, and it was concluded that the application should be reopened to further prosecution. The previous paper No. 11, at page 33 (p. 130), reports an oral interview. Reasons for reopening are not stated. The record suggests that such reasons might have included: (1) the examiner's failure to note or take any action on claims 13 and 

14 in the "final" rejection (paper 8) or correcting letter (paper 10); (2) the desirability of adding the Guerbet reference patent; (3) a feeling that the previous statements of reasons for rejection lacked adequate clarity; or (4) a conclusion that the difficulty of framing a generic claim that would adequately protect the inventors, warranted affording them further opportunity in that respect. In cases dealing with chemical compounds the problem of presenting a generic claim of proper scope is almost insoluble. Reference may be had to the discussion of Ex parte Chipman and Chipman Engineering Co. v. Reade 

Mfg. Co., reviewed in chapter V, section C, and the discussion of the scope of disclosure (ch. VI, sec. B).

The Guerbet patent cited in paper 12 was dated July 1, 1941, and therefore was not available when the examiner made his search preparatory to entering his rejection of August 14, 1940. In all probability, a copy of the patent was not in his files when he prepared his second rejection on July 17, 1941. Some time elapses between the issue date and the actual placing of a copy in the file, and some time also elapses between the examiner's review of papers and the actual mailing of his rejection. 

The withdrawal of the final rejection not only permitted entry of the amendments proposed in paper 11 after the final rejection, but also afforded the inventors an opportunity to respond to the new rejection of paper 12 and required entry of the amendment of paper 13.

The Rules of Practice did not require the examiner to enter or consider the amendments (papers 15 and 16) filed after the second final rejection (paper 14). Nevertheless, it is an established policy of the Office at least to inspect all such papers and to enter any amendments which will (a) decrease or simplify issues presented on appeal or (b) place the application in allowable status. This practice is now expressed in Rule 116, and in the Manual of Patent Examining 

Procedure (secs. 714.12, 714.13, and 1207).

The affidavits filed with paper 15 were also made a part of the record and considered by the examiner. No reason is presented in the record for the delay (after citation of the Guerbet patent) in filing the affidavits. The contents of the affidavits indicate that the subject matter could have been presented immediately after citation of the patent on March 20, 1943 (paper 12), rather than waiting until after the final rejection of May 26, 1943.

Eight amendatory papers were filed by the attorney (papers 3, 4, 5, 7, 11, 13, 15, and 16). Paper 4 is merely a clerical correction of

No. 3, and paper 5 expresses a change of purpose as to election of species claims and makes appropriate substitutions. 

4. Claims

Eleven claims were originally presented, based on a description of seven distinct chemical compounds. (Examples 1 and 3 relate to slight variants in the process of making a single compound.) 

Four of these claims (Nos. 6, 7, 8, and 9) were directed to the four individual specific products of examples 1 and 3, 4, 5, and 7, respectively. This was one species in excess of the then permitted three. 

The present rule (Rule 141) allows claims to five distinct species. 

The former rule, like the present, permitted a plurality of species claims only in the presence of an allowable generic claim. 

Present Rule 146 sets forth the practice in the treatment of claims addressed to generic subject matter and a plurality of species. This rule refers to the presently permissible five species rather than the former three, but otherwise expresses the 1940 practice. The examiner is required to make a complete search and examination of the generic claims. This search would include a search for each of the species included in the generic claim, as a prior art disclosure of a single species within the terms of a generic claim would defeat such claim. In the event that no generic claim was found allowable, the examiner required the applicant to elect a single species for further prosecution. 

In the event that more than the permissible three (now five) species claims were presented, the examiner might require restriction to the permissible number in advance of further examination. The lastmentioned step is generally a time-wasting procedure, and the better practice is to examine the merits of the generic claims and in the event none is found allowable, to require election of a single preferred species and also a restriction to not more than the permissible number as was done in the patent file here discussed (paper 2). 

The requirement for election of a single species permits focusing attention on a specific embodiment of the invention for further discussion. In the event a generic claim is allowed, no further search is required as to the additional species claim, and except as to matters of form and possible specific support in the description, no further consideration of such additional species claims is required. Matters

of form are usually disposed of by consideration of the form of the claim to the elected species. 

In the response (paper 3), the product of process claim 3 and a new species product, claim 12, were elected as preferred species. This was inconsistent as new claim 12 did not represent the product of claim 3 process. The inconsistency was corrected by paper 4, so that product claim 7 (together with process claim 3) represented the elected species. 

Product claim 7 was thereafter allowed without further controversy, and became claim 2 of the patent. This claim represented the commercial development. It was held valid and infringed in the suit. 

The inventors also changed their position as to the additional two permissible species (papers 3 and 5). Claim 8 was removed and afterward restored as 15. Claims 6 and 15 became claims 1 and 3 of the patent. These claims did not provide any additional controversy in the Office, since they were allowed on allowance of a generic claim (claim 34) inclusive thereof. They were not involved in the infringement suit. 

5. Process claims 

Claims 1 to 4 were addressed to a process of preparing the compounds. These claims were addressed to a conventional chemical step of iodine substitution as evidenced by the term "iodizing" used in the claims, and the reference in the description to then known methods (top of page 6 (p. 103)). Opinions have differed as to the merit of such claims. From a strictly legal point of view, they are without merit. In re Foster, 86 F. 2d 981, 24 C.C.P.A. 794 (1937); In re 

Wuertz, 110 F. 2d 854, 27 C.C.P.A. 1039 (1940); In re Lincoln, 126 F. 

2d 477, 29 C.C.P.A. 942 (1942). There has been a view to the effect that the process claims cover no more than the corresponding product claims and all processes of preparing a patented product are protected by the patent (35 U.S.C. 154). It was concluded that a separate effort to cause the removal of process claims is not worthwhile. Process claim 1 was modified and later replaced by claim 17. All process claims were eventually removed (paper 11). 

6. Use claims

Original claims 10 and 11 included an introductory clause, "X-ray contrast agents for making the gall bladder visible," expressing the intended utility of the compounds. Claim 10 was otherwise identical with generic claim 5, and claim 11 specified the "salts" generally of the compounds of claim 10. These claims were known as "use" claims. 

At the time of pendency of the Dohrn, et al., application, there were divergent views as to the merit of these claims. One view was to the effect that the statement of intended use in the preamble was a significant limitation, and that this use (process) was an essential element of the claimed invention. This view was developed to the point where it was suggested that only the substance in a package suitable for the particular use and with an appropriate label would constitute an anticipation, or infringement. This view was overruled by the Court of 

Customs and Patent Appeals in the case of In re Thuau, 135 F. 2d 344, 57 U.S.P.Q. 324, 30 C.C.P.A. 979, decided April 5, 1943, during the pendency of this case. All "use" claims were removed.

16 10 

7. Generic claims 

Original claim 5 was the key generic claim describing a class of chemical compounds which included all of the examples and purported to define the scope of the invention. This claim was first modified and later replaced by claim 18 (paper 7) and successively by 21, 22, 23, 24, and 27 (paper 11), by claims 29, 30, and 31 (paper 13), and finally by claim 34 (papers 15 and 16).

Two difficult problems were involved in the controversy over generic claims.

The first of these problems was that of the permissible scope of a generic claim covering the specific compounds invented but also including obvious equivalents. The inventor is not entitled to claim more than he has invented. The difficulties of this problem in applications dealing with chemical compounds has already been discussed. 

The problem is sometimes avoided by defining the operative class as an artificial group wherein the members are enumerated. Ex parte 

Markush, 1925 C.D. 126 (Commissioner). This practice was so abused as to necessitate restrictions, thereby creating the second problem, namely, the problem of what is the proper technical form for this type of claim. The members of the class had to be selected from a cohesive scientifically recognized class. The presentation of this special "Markush" type of claim was held inconsistent with the presence of a conventional generic claim. The presentation of claims directed to successively diminishing artificial classes was also prohibited. Ex parte Burke, 1934 C.D. 5, and Ex parte Dahlen, 1934 C.D. 9 (both by the Commissioner). The second of these restrictions has since been relaxed to permit a claim to an artificial class in the same application with a conventional generic claim, but the limitation was still being enforced during the period of pendency of the Dohrn, et al., case. The development of the "Markush" type claim has given rise to an extremely technical body of procedural rules, developed in the main by petition.

While most of the controversy in the processing of the application was over the form and scope of the generic claim, that controversy was never adequately solved. The only generic claim accepted by the 

Office (claim 34) became claim 4 of the patent, and that claim was ultimately held invalid as covering more than the patentees had invented.

In summarizing, 33 distinct claims were presented for consideration by the Office. (The total number was 34, but claim 15 was a restoration of canceled claim 8.) Four claims were patented. There was no controversy over the claim 7 to the preferred species, which became claim 2 of the patent and was held valid and infringed. There were also no real controversies over the two permitted additional species, which became claims 1 and 3 of the patent. These were not involved in the infringement suit. The main controversy was over the form and scope of generic claims. The only generic claim allowed was held invalid in the infringement suit. The patentees are now protected from infringement only with respect to claims 1, 2, and 3 (the products of examples 1 and 3, 4 and 5). The products of examples 2, 5, 6, 7, and 8 are not in terms included within the claims of the patent. 63416 O -61 -7

The patented product of claim 2 is still (as of 1959) on the market as "Priodax." Casual inquiry in the field reveals that the owners of the patent (Schering) also market another product, not protected by the patent, for the same use, and that others market competitive products, presumably not covered by the patent. Available information suggests, however, that the patented product "Priodax" attained a wide sale and a substantial monopoly for its use for possibly ten years after its introduction in 1944. The patented product has since been almost entirely replaced by improved products. A survey of the market position of the patented product is beyond the scope of this paper.

The patent file illustrates some of the problems encountered in the examination of applications for patents. The primary problem confronting the inventors, i.e., the problem of a valid generic claim covering the exemplary subject matter and obvious equivalents, was not solved. An after-the-fact review shows that both the examiner and the attorney could have presented a better performance. The file cannot be considered exemplary as the problems presented with each application differ from all others. It is, however, illustrative of the fact that the primary controversy that arises in the vast majority of applications is the problem of formulating a suitable claim to embrace adequately the subject matter and still not include more than has been invented.

UNITED STATES PATENT OFFICE 

2,345,384 

POLY IODIZED DERIVATIVES OF THE HY, DROXY DIPHENYL CARBOXYLIC ACIDS AND A PROCESS FOR PREPARING THE 

SAME 

Max Dohrn, Berlin-Charlottenburg, and Paul 

Diedrich, Finkenkrug, Osthavelland, near Berlin, Germany, assignors to Schering Corporation, Bloomfield, N. J., a corporation of New Jersey

No Drawing. Application April 6, 1940, Serial No. 

328,236. In Germany May 20, 1939 

4 Claims.

This invention relates to poly iodized derivatives of the hydroxy diphenyl carboxylic acids and a process for preparing the same. 

Not only the known tetra iodo phenolphthalein but also iodized derivatives of 2-phenyl cinchonic acid (see U. 8. Patent No. 1,846,321) have been employed for rendering visible the gall bladder by X-rays. All these compounds yield a good picture but they are not sufficiently indifferent physiologically on application in the necessary rather large amounts. (CL260-520) 5 10 Now, the poly iodized derivatives of hydroxy diphenyl carboxylic acids represent lipoid-soluble compounds, which, contrary to the hitherto known contrast agents for the gall bladder com- 15 bine a very good contrasting effect with an excellent compatibility. Said derivatives are made by lodizing o- or p-hydroxy diphenyl carboxylic acids by methods known per se.

We have found as most suitable contrast 20 agents the iodized compounds of hydroxy dit phenyl carboxylic acids of the general formula (OH). wherein X represents a member of the group consisting of the acetic acid radical and the radicals of homologues of acetic acid, while n indicates the numerals 1 or 2 and m the numerals 

2 or 4, e. g. hydroxy dilodo diphenyl acetic acid, hydroxy- or dihydroxy diiodo diphenyl propionic or -butyric acids and others; as especially suitable there have proved 3,5-dilodo-4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid, and 8-(4-hydroxy-3,5-dilodo phenyl)-a-phenyl propionic acid. The salts of these acids are capable of dissolving in water with neutral reaction.

The invention may be illustrated by the following examples without, however, being limited by them:

Example 1 25 30 35 40 45 To a solution of 22.8 grs. of 4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid in 400 ccs. of N-sodium hydroxide solution, a solution of 51 grs. of iodine and 

51 grs. of potassium iodide in 500 ccs. of water is added drop by drop at room temperature while stirring. After the addition, stirring is continued for some time and then the iodized 450 hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid is precipitated by addition of sulfurous acid. The precipitate is removed by suction, washed with water and recrystallized from dilute alcohol. The 3,5-diiodo- 

4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid melts at 197° C. & 

It is readily soluble in alcohol, acetone and ether, difficultly soluble in benzene and toluene, insoluble in chloroform and petrol ether.

Example 2 21 grs. of 2-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid lactone (melting point 113-114° C.) are dissolved in 400 ccs. of N-sodium hydroxide solution and 

250 ccs. of water. Then it is treated according to Example 1 with the same amount of iodine potassium iodide solution. On working up a product is obtained which on recrystallisation from dilute alcohol melts at 160° C. It represents pure 3,5-diiodo-2-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid. Its solubility is similar to that of the p derivative, described in Example 1. 

Example 3 

To a solution of 22.8 grs. of 4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid in 200 ccs. of N-sodium hydroxide solution and 300 ccs. of water a solution of 33 grs. of chlorine-iodine in 50 ccs. of a 

30% hydrochloric acid and 450 ccs. of water is slowly added while stirring. Stirring is continued for several hours. Then the precipitate is separated, dissolved in dilute sodium hydroxide solution, filtered and the filtrate acidified, whereby 3,5-diiodo-4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid, melting at 197° C., is obtained.

Example 4 

To a solution of 24.2 grs. of p-(4-hydroxy phenyl)-a-phenyl propionic acid in 800 ccs. of

N/2-sodium hydroxide solution there is slowly added a solution of 50.8 grs. of iodine and 50.8 grs. of potassium iodide in 250 ccs. of water at room temperature while stirring. From the reaction mixture the iodized product is precipitated by addition of sulfurous acid and hydrochloric acid. After stirring for some time the precipitate is filtered off by suction and washed with water. After drying it is recrystallized from benzene with the aid of charcoal. The p-(4hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)-a-phenyl propionic acid is obtained in the form of colourless prisms melting at 159° C. in a yield of 42 grs., corresponding to 85% of the theoretical yield. It is readily soluble in ether, alcohol and acetone, difficultly soluble in benzene, toluene and chloroform, insoluble in water and petrol ether.

Example 5 

24.2 grs. of 8-(4-hydroxy phenyl)-p-phenyl propionic acid are iodized according to Example 4. By recrystallisation from benzene the p-(4-

4 12 2,845,384 hydroxy-3,5-dilodo phenyl)-p-phenyl propionic acid is obtained forming colourless needles melt.. ing at 171-172° C. Its solubility is similar to that of the compound, described in Example 4. 

Example 6 

24.2 grs. of -(4-hydroxy phenyl)-x-phenyl propionic acid of M. P.-150° C., prepared by condensation of methyl mandelic acid and phenol by means of 73% sulfuric acid,, are iodized accord- 10 ing to Example 4. The «-(4-hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)--phenyl propionic acid is obtained, which forms a crystalline colourless powder, melting at 183-184° C., when twice recrystallized from benzene. Its solubility is similar to that of its isomers, described above.

The crude reaction product is recrystallized from a mixture of chloroform and petrol ether. The y-(4-hydroxy-3,5dilodophenyl)-6-phenyl butyric acid thus obtained melts at 140-141° C.

For using the compounds described and claimed as X-ray contrast agents for rendering visible the gall bladder, they are preferably employed in the form of chocolate-covered granules. 3 grs. of these are usually sufficient in order to give excellent X-ray pictures of the gall bladder 12-14 hours after application. Of course, the contrast agents may also be administered in another form, for example, in the form of tablets or the like. 

Furthermore salt solutions of the acids may be used likewise.

All these changes and variations that may be made by those skilled in the art must comply with the requirements of the principles set forth herein and in the claims annexed hereto.

Claims 1, 5, 10 and 11 are rejected as being alternative in the "or" clauses. 

This is not permissible.

The definitions of n and m would be better as follows: -n is an integer not greater than 2and -m is an integer of from 2 to 4 Claims 10 and 11 are rejected as being directed to the obvious use of the compounds of the invention. To be patentable compounds must have utility. This utility on which other claims rest may not be made the basis of claims in itself. These claims are also too broad in "comprising". The other components must be listed or the term should be changed to consisting of. 

Claims 1, 5, 10 and 11 are rejected as being indefinite and too broad in X. The radical of acetic acid may be "CH3COD" and this is the usual meaning of the term. 

X might be defined as the radical of a lower aliphatic acid of the formula (CH2)y COOH. 

In claims 1, 5, 10 and 11, iodine should be represented by I, which is the American symbol. 328236

Claims 1, 5, 10 and 11 are additionally rejected as being indefinite and too broad. The positions in the nucleus of the hydroxyl and iodine groups should be indicated. As drawn, the claims are indefinite and too broad sine not all permutations and combinations of the compounds have been disclosed nor may be considered to be taught in a representative way.

As drawn the claims are directed to five species as follows: 

(I) Claims 2 and 6 directed to 3,5-diiodo-4hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid. (II) Claims 3 and 7 directed to B-(4-hydroxy- 

3,5-diiodo phenyl)-2-phenyl propionic acid. 

W% (III) Claim directed to Y-(4-hydroxy phenyl)- 

B-phenyl butyric acid. (IV) Claim 8 directed to a B-phenyl propionic acid product. (V) Claim directed to B-(4-hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)-B-phenyl butyric acid.

Restriction to no more than three species and tentative election of a single species is required in advance of a further action on the merits.

Lines 9, 10, and 18 and 19, on page 1, have been revised and are now believed to be clear and correct. 

Claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 have been amended to overcome the Examiner's objections thereto, the new wording being similar to that suggested by the Examiner. 

Reconsideration of the rejection of claims 10 and 11" as being directed to the obvious use of the compounds of the invention" is respectfully requested, In the first place, it is believed that applicants are entitled to claim their invention specifically, and in the present instance a specific embodiment of the invention is an X-ray contrast agent containing one of the new group of compounds as an essential component. Also, it is believed that claims 10 and 11 are patentably differentiated over claims to the compounds themselves because it is not obvious that iodinated compounds are suitable as X-ray contrast agents. 

Of the numerous iodinated organic compounds that are known or are possible, only a relatively few are suitable as X-ray contrast agents. It is not eufficient that a compound contain iodine to make it a satisfactory 328236

contrast agent. It must have the proper degree of solubility, low toxicity or non-toxicity, compatibility, tolerability, etc. These essential properties cannot be assumed from the mere fact that a particular compound is iodinated; quite the contrary, it is much more likely that a compound will split off iodine and produce toxic effects, which result is all the more likely in the case of poly-iodinated compounds.

With reference to the definition of X in claims 1, 5, 10, and 11, applicants wish to thank the Examiner for his suggestion of a more satisfactory formula, but applicants regret that they cannot adopt such suggestion because it is not adequate or correct. The divalent radical (CH2)y does not, in the first place, provide a valence for the carboxylic group. Moreover, it does not provide for alkylidene compounds, 1.8. isomeric compounds such as are referred to at the end of Example 6. 

Thus, the product of Example 6 has the following formula: CH3 C6H2120H 

C6H5 COOH It will be recognized that the form proposed in the official action does not take care of disclosed compounds of the above type. Perhaps the 

Examiner had in mind a radical of the form CH2COOH, but it is believed that the definition of X in the amended claims as a "divalent radical of an aliphatic carboxylic acid" is entirely clear and is commensurate in scope with the disclosure. Withdrawal of the rejection of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 is accordingly requested.

The specification and claims have been amended by substituting the symbol I. (iodine) for the German J. 

The rejection of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 as being too broad is not thought to be justified. Although it is most likely that the iodine radicals become attached in the 3 and 5 positions of the phenyl hydroxy group, they may also add onto the other phenyl group, and also in different positions in the hydroxy phenyl group. Although applicants have indicated the 3,5-position in their specification and in some of

the claims, it is possible that the iodine may occupy other positions, and it is therefore believed that applicants should not be restricted to any particular positions for the iodine groups. The Examiner is reminded that an applicant is not required to know the theory of his reaction, nor should he be bound by any theoretical assumptions, The important thing is that the compounds become poly-iodinated, and in the absence of pertinent art, it is believed that applicants should be permitted claims of the scope of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11. With reference to the hydroxyl groups, these, too, may be located in different positions although the p-position is preferred. It will be noted that applicants' disclosure adequately supports the language of claims 1, 5, 10, and 11,

It is submitted that applicants have made an adequately broad disclosure of the fact that hydroxy diphenyl carboxylic acids can be iodinated to yield useful compounds, and the claims fall within this broad disclosure. 

In response to the Examiner's requirement for restriction to three species, claims 8 and 9 have been cancelled, and of the remaining three species, applicants elect, that represented by process claim 3 and . the corresponding product clam 7; and love added. new product claim 42, which corresponds to process claim. It will be understood that applicants reserve the right to file a divisional application on the cancelled claims.

Of the new claims presented above, claim 12, as has already been indicated, corresponds to process claim 3, being the product of Claims 13 and 14 define the invention in somewhat different language and are believed to be patentably distinct over the other claims in the case and to be fully supported by the disclosure. that process. solicited.

In view of the foregoing, favorable action on the case is да

Claims 1, 5, 10, and 11 have been rewritten and in their present form are believed to be entirely definite and commensurate in 328236

scope with the disclosure.

With reference to claims 10 and 11, now 19 and 20, it is submitted that applicants' compounds may be used in other ways than as a contrast agent, for example as intermediates for the production of other compounds, and it is felt that applicants are entitled to claim specifically the preferred embodiment of their invention. Claims 19 and 20 are drawn along the lines approved in the note to 44 U.S.P.Q. 696 (see 44 U.S.P.Q. 

718). The situation presented by the present case is quite different from that involved in In re Waldron, and in the Scott patent file. In the present case the compound itself is new, so that the claim as a whole is patentable. In the Waldron case, for example it was held that the tool, regardless of its special use, did not involve invention over the prior art. 

Moreover, the decision indicates (page 562, colum 2) that there are exceptions to the general rule that an introductory phrase does not amount to a limitation and it is submitted that one of these exceptions is a chemical invention in which a hitherto unknown utility is discovered for a chemical compound. As the Examiner knows, chemical claims have been sustained to new uses even where the chemical compound was old. In the present case the chemical compound is new, so that there should be no question as to the patentability of claims 19 and 20.

It is requested that claims 2, 6, 15, and 16 be permitted to remain in the application pending final action on the generic claims. 

In view of the foregoing, reconsideration and allowance of the application are respectfully requested, Respectfully,

At an interview with the Primary and Assistant Examiners on February 25th, the courtesy of which is appreciated, the official action of September 8 and the previous communication of June 1, 1942 were discussed. At such interview the Primary Examiner indicated that a Markush claim listing all of the compounds disclosed in the application might be considered allowable, and likewise a claim which defined X as being a trivalent hydrocarbon radical selected from the group consisting of -CH- and -0₂Hz-. Applicants have accordingly cancelled the rejected claims and replaced them with new claims, of which claims 21 and 22 are believed to correspond to the claims which the Examiner indicated as possibly being allowable. In claim 22 n and m have been defined in a manner which, it is believed, the Primary Examiner considered at the hearing to be satisfactory. 

It will be noted that claim 22 (and of course also claim 21) 

Claim 22 thus overcomes specify the positions of the iodine radicals. the objection raised in this connection in the official action of June 1, 1942. 

It is believed that in view of the prior art, applicants are clearly entitled also to claims 23 to 28. In all of these claims the position of the iodine atoms is specified, so that the ground of rejection in the official action of June 1, 1942 in this regard is removed. Also, X is defined, for example, in claim 23, as "a trivalent 328236

The lower aliphatic hydrocarbon radical" and is even more limited in clain 26 which states that the radical contains 1 to 3 carbon atoms. number of substances covered by claims 23 to 28 is accordingly quite -restricted, and it is believed that whatever slight extension any of these claims has beyond the disclosures of the examples themselves, is entirely within the scope of the disclosures on page 1, line 1 to page 2, line 7 of the application. As the Examiners will recall, there was shown to them a dozen or so X-ray photographs of the gall bladders of patients who had been treated with one of the compounds disclosed in the application, from which the sharp delineations of the outlines and diseased conditions of the gall bladder were clearly evident, comparison being made with the X-ray photograph of a patient who had been treated also, at another time, with the most commonly used X-ray contrast agent for gall bladders, and with an X-ray photograph of a normal gall bladder.

In view of this showing it is believed that it is the duty of the Examiner to allow claims which will protect applicants' invention against appropriation by a competitor who, beaming apprised of the principles of the invention, will readily conceive of variations falling within the scope of the original disclosure of the application. It is believed. that applicants are entitled to a claim of broader scope than, for example, claim 21, in order that their invention may be adequately protected.

It is recognized that it is the practice of the Office not to allow both Markush claims and generic claims. However, it is believed that an applicant should be permitted to present both types of claims and should be required to elect between them only after the generic claims have been allowed. This procedure furthers the prosecution of the application, since both types of claims are submitted at the same time. 

Therefore, even though the Examiner should object to the presentati on of both types, it is requested that action be given on both so that the applicants may be in a position to elect between them. 

Attention is respectfully called to the fact that claim 25 328236

contains substantially the wording of the original disclosure at the bottom of page 1 of the application. At the interview the Primary 

Examiner pointed out that the acetic acid radical is normally CH3COO. 

This is conceded to be true, but only where there are no other indications that a different radical is meant. From the name of the compounds ("diphenyl carboxylic acids") and from the formula, it is evident that an what is meant is/acetic acid radical having a free carboxylic group and in which two hydrogen atoms of the alkyl group of the acid are replaced by phenyl, as shown in the formula. That applicants had in mind this type of a radical is evident not only from the formula itself but also from the fact that they list a 3,5-diiodo-4-hydroxy diphenyl acetic acid and 8-(4-hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)--phenyl propionic acid as embraced by the formula (top of page 2 of specification). 

With reference to claims 26 and 28 which call for X-ray contrast agents, it is submitted that the Examiner's speculation in the official communication of June 1, 1942 that claims of this type cover inoperative compounds, is not justified. The opacifying action is due largely to the iodine and as the different acids covered by the claims are not toxic, there is no reason for believing that any of the compounds covered by the claims will not be satisfactory, at least to some degree, although some may be preferred to others. Unless the Examiner can give good reasons why any compounds covered by the claims are inoperative, it is believed that he should accept the assertions of the applicants, supported as they are by the fact that one of the compounds of the invention is now on the market. 

If the Examiner does not see his way clear to allowing the above claims, he is respectfully requested to enter this amendment for purposes of appeal.

It is noted that the official communications above referred 328236

to do not mention claims 13 and 14 and it is accordingly assumed that they have been allowed in view of the limitations contained therein. 

Respectfully submitted,

The final rejection of Sept. 8, 1942 is withdrawn.

Claim 7 is allowable so far as is now known. 

Claims 6, 15 and 27 are rejected as failing to read on the elected species. Claim 27 represents a new species in excess of the elected three and is subject to rejection on the ground of misjoinder. 

Claims 13, 14 and 21 to 28 inclusive are rejected as being unpatentable over the newly found reference cited above which discloses similar iodated aromatic carboxylic acid compounds used as X-ray contrast agents. In view of the breadth of the claims no reason is seen for excluding the compounds of the patent from the terms, since they are as close in properties, in view of the essential iodine component, to some compounds covered as the compounds covered are to each other. 

Claims 21 to 26 inclusive and 28 are rejected as being of improper subject matter for a Markush type claim and as employing seven Markush groups of varying scope. 

The compounds of claim 21 are too diverse to be classed together, in view of the utility advanced for them and the divergent physiological reactions of various compounds. 328236

کی 328,236 Sheet 2 For the same reason the methylene and ethylene groups of claim 

2 are too diverse. Claims 22, 23, 24 and 25 are alternative in listing acids "and their water-soluble salts. The "Markush" expression is conventional, but Markush terms within Markush teras make the claims subject to rejection as being so involved as to fail to point out and distinctly claim the invention. Claims 21 to 25 are broader than the disclosure in "water-soluble salts." It is not believed that all watersoluble salts would be operative in the utility proposed. 

The term probably would include "radium" for example. Claims 

13 and 14 are broader than the disclosure and inoperative in the scope given, in the sense of the invention. The disclosure does not give representative examples of all the tremendous number of compounds covered, and since arsenic compounds would be included it is not believed such claims are operative in the scope given. Claims 25 and 28 are considered to be indefinite and too broad in "homologues," since this term would cover fifty carbon atom chains possibly substituted by toxic materials.
