A composition prepared for use as an X-ray contrast agent, and containing as an essential ingredient an iodinated diphenyl Carboxylic acid, the carboxyl group being attached to a trivalent aliphatic group joining the phenyl radicals and containing from 1 to 3 carbon atoms, one of the phenyl groups having iodine in the 3 and 5 positions and a hydroxy group in ortho-position to an iodine atom.-- 

Cancel claims 22, 24 and 27 without prejudice. 

Claim 27 has been cancelled in view of the Examiner's holding that it represents a species in excess of the elected and permissible three. 

Claims 22 and 24 have also been cancelled in order to advance the prosecution of the application, their subject matter being covered by the other claims in the case. 

Reconsideration of the rejection of claims 13, 14 and 21 to 28 on Guerbet is respectfully requested. Although claim 27 has been cancelled (along with claims 22 and 24) it may be mentioned that this claim calls for the salt of the acid defined in allowed claim 7. not

The rejection of claim 27 is therefore at believed/to have been sound, and the same is thought to be true of the other claims. 

The Examiner says that the reference "discloses similar iodated aromatic carboxylic acid compounds used as X-ray contrast agents". 

We respectfully submit that the compounds of the reference are not similar to the applicants for the following reasons: 

1. Applicants' claims are all limited to diphenyl compounds while Guerbet's are strictly monophenyl compounds. 

2. It follows from the foregoing that applicants' comin the carbon skeleton pounds are of considerably higher molecular weight/than those of Guerbet. 

The importance of this feature will be explained below. 328236

3. 

In Guerbet, the carboxylic groups are attached directly to the phenyl radical. In applicants compounds they are attached to the aliphatic carbon bridge between the two phenyl radicals. 

4. Applicants' compounds contain a hydroxyl group while 

Guerbet's do not.

Applicants' compounds differ radically from Guerbet's not only in chemical constitution but also in physiological significance. 

One of the outstanding characteristics of applicants' compounds is that they are soluble in or compatible with lipoids, and while they are soluble in water, the solubility is comparatively low. This relatively low watersolubility is very important. As the Examiner may know, the law known as the Overton-Meyor law is of paramount importance in animal metabolism. 

This law relates to the distribution of substances between the lipoid and aqueous juices or fluids. The ratio between the lipophilic and hydrophilic properties of a substance determines its distribution in the animal body. The more highly soluble a substance is in water, the more readily and the more quickly will it be eliminated from the body through the kidneys and in the urine. Where, however, the solubility in water is relatively low and the substance is lipophilic in character, a greater proportion thereof will remain in the lipoid tissues and juices. 

One of the important features of applicants' compounds is that because of their relatively low water-solubility and high lipophilic character, they seek out and remain in the lipoid tissues and organs. 

The gall bladder represents such an organ and that accounts for the very sharply delineated gall bladder X-ray pictures which are obtained with applicants' material, as shown to the Assistant and Primary Examiners at a conference last February. Just why applicants' substances selectively seek out the gall bladder is not entirely understood, but this property appears to be a function of the relatively high molecular weight of the hydrocarbon residue of applicants' compounds. The hydrocarbon residuo of the compounds of Guerbet is of relatively low molecular weight and hence it has little affinity for lipoid substances. The distribution of Guerbet's compounds in lipoid substances is further reduced by the 328236

very high water-solubility of Guerbet's compounds. 

Reference is had to page 1, column 1, 1ines 8 and 26, from which it is clear that

Guerbet sought compounds which have "a great solubility in water", and which are "very soluble". That Guerbet's compounds show no tendency to remain in any lipoid tissue is evident from the examples of the patent which show that in a few minutes after injection the compound is located in the urinary system. Guerbet's compounds are so soluble that they are injected into the veins, a procedure which cannot be followed with applicants' compounds which, as stated, are of low water-solubility. Applicants' compounds are absorbed through the intestinal tract (where any free acid is converted into the sodium salt) and they are absorbed into lipoid juices and substances and carried to the gall bladder. If Guerbet's substances were taken by mouth they would be quickly eliminated through the kidneys.

It should thus be evident that applicants' compounds are totally different from Guerbet's both chemically and physiologically. 

The Examiner's statement that Guerbet's compounds are as close to some compounds covered by applicants' claims as such compounds are to each other, is believed to be without basis in fact. 

All of applicants' compounds are of relatively high molecular weight. in C atoms, are diphenyl compounds, contain an aliphatic bridge between the phenyl groups, to which bridge the carboxyl group is joined, and contain a hydroxyl group. The structural pictures of all of applicants' substances are very similar to each other. To make Guerbet's compounds look like any of applicants', one would have to "see double" so far as the phenyl group is concerned, be blind as to the position of the carboxyl group, and imagine an aliphatic bridge and a hydroxyl substituent. 

The claims have been revised and have all been limited to compounds in which the trivalent group, to which the carboxyl group is attached, contains from one to three carbon atoms. These claims are clearly supported by the disclosure and the seven examples are believed to be adequate to support the broadest of the claims. Accordingly, of the application and allowance of the claims now reconsideration 1 328236

in the case are requested.

With respect to the Markush groups set forth in the claims, the Examiner is reminded that claim 21 is drawn along the lines suggested by the Primary Examiner at the interview last February. Applicants are, however, prepared to cancel this claim if claims like 31 and 32 are allowed. 

It is believed, however, that action on the merits should be given on all of the claims regardless of their Markush form in order that applicants may be in a better position to determine which Markush form to retain. 

If the Examiner will allow the other claims in the case, he is authorized to cancel claim 21.

It may be mentioned that claims 29 and 30 do not differ in the scope of Markush groups from, for example, claims 31 and 32, since all of the claims are limited to one to three carbon atoms in the group between the phenyl radicals. The Examiner's objection to claiming the alkali metal salts along with the acids has, it is believed, been overcome by defining the carboxyl group-as COOY. It will be noted that the salts have been limited to the alkali metal salts. 

Claims 13 and 14, which have been replaced by claims 29 and 30, exclude substituents like arsenic and other elements other than C, H, O, and I.

An earnest effort has been made to place this case in condition for allowance and it is thought that in the absence of more pertinent art, the case is ready for issus, possibly with an Examiner's amendment cancelling claim 21. 

New York, N. Y., May 12, 1943. 

Respectfully submitted,

MAX DOHEN et al

MAX DOHRN et al For a Patent for POLY IODIZED DERIVATIVES OF THE HYDROXY DIPHENYL CARBOXYLIC ACIDS AND A PROCESS FOR PREPARING THE SAME

MAX GILBERT, being duly sworn, deposes and says:

I am a doctor of medicine, which degree I received in 1931. 

For the past seven years I have been a member of the medical research staff of Schering Corporation of Bloomfield, New Jersey, and in that connection, among other research work that I have done, I have worked in the field of X-ray contrast agents. Prior to going with Schering Corporation 

I worked for a period of two years in the same field with another pharmaceutical concern also interested in the medical phases of X-ray work. I worked under a fellowship for two years at St. Luke's Hospital in Chicago in the field of radiology. In this eleven-year period I have, of course, familiarized myself with the different types of contrast agents that were used and developed from time to time, and I believe that I am qualified to speak authoritatively on the subject of the relation of contrast agents to X-ray work.

I personally represented the Schering Corporation in the introduction of the subject matter of the above-entitled application to the medical profession, which work was conducted over a period of approximately three years. The subject matter of the invention has been approved by the Federal Food & Drug Administration and the material has been nationally distributed and used by physicians in taking X-ray pictures of gall-bladders. 

From my experience with the invention of this application I can state that 328236

the invention thereof is considered by the medical profession as a highly valuable and successful contrast agent in taking X-rays of gall-bladders. 

The inventors of the subject matter of the above-named application, Drs. Max Dohrn and Paul Diedrich, are well known scientists in the contrast agent field and their work in this field, which I have followed attentively, has attracted international attention. 

The compounds which are the subject matter of Drs. Dohrn and 

Diedrich's patent application, function on the basis that they are introduced from the liver with the bile and into and concentrate in the gallbladder. The material of Drs. Dohrn and Diedrich's invention has been introduced to the medical profession under the name of PRIODAX in tablet form. A dose consists of 3 Gms. which in a time space of from 12 to 15 hours after they are taken by mouth, are sufficiently concentrated in the gall-bladder as to permit X-ray visualizations and the production of highly satisfactory pictures of the gall-bladder. The material is taken by mouth, which is a great advantage as compared with contrast agents which are injected into the blood stream, and finds its way through the blood stream to the liver. When PRIODAX reaches the liver, it mixes with the bile which is produced by the liver, and with the bile passes to the gallbladder. The liver has a peculiar characteristic of extracting PRIODAX from the blood stream and mixing it with the bile. The bile which passes from the liver to the gall-bladder carries PRIODAX, thus mixed with the bile, to the gall-bladder, where the bile and PRIODAX concentrate and ultimately fill the gall-bladder. PRIODAX renders the gall-bladder opaque and provides a contrast agent by which excellent photographs may readily be made. PRIODAX, when it reaches the liver, does not in any wise set up chemical reaction in the liver with the bile or any part thereof, nor does it, when it reaches the gall-bladder with the bile, set up any chemical reaction. Its relation to the bile is a mere physical one, namely, a mixture with the bile. The bile has a ready affinity for PRIODAX, and as 

PRIODAX accumulates in the gall-bladder, that organ fills homogeneously, thereby avoiding variations in density. The use of PRIODAX in taking I-rays of the gall-bladder avoids superimposed intestinal shadows, which tend to 328236

cause confusing interpretations of the X-rays and necessitate additional study thereof, which is quite burdensome to the physician. 

One of the characteristics of PRIODAX resides in the fact that it is taken by mouth, and the only precaution which need be taken is to avoid eating fried or fatty foods at the time PRIODAX is taken and the 12-or 15-hour period thereafter when the X-rays are made. PRIODAX has the characteristic that in its physical mixture with the bile, it travels with the bile and it will concentrate in the gall-bladder with the bile, and as long as the bile is not ejected from the gall-bladder PRIODAX will remain in the gall-bladder until there is a movement of bile, depending upon the demands of the system for bile for the digestive process. One of the occasions which requires the movement of bile from the gall-bladder to other parts of the human system is the introduction of fatty substances in the form of food in the human system. In order to cause digestion thereof, bile is required. Inasmuch as it is necessary to retain the PRIODAX in the gall-bladder, the patient is instructed not to eat fried or fatty substances, but after the X-rays have been taken, the patient is then instructed to eat fatty materials which bring about movement of the bile from the galibladder and consequently the gradual elimination with the bile of the PRIODAX. 

There is no chemical action of PRIODAX in the system. It is merely present in the bile in a physical mixture and the bile, having an affinity for PRIODAX, the PRIODAX is evenly and uniformly distributed throughout the bile. 

In connection with the advantages of PRIODAX, it will be of interest to note that certain contrast agents, when taken by mouth or administered by injection, are of value as such in taking pictures of portions of the kidneys, ureters, urinary bladder, and other parts of the urinary tract.

Inasmuch as they have the characteristic of being water-soluble, they therefore pass to these organisms. The Guerbet patent No. 2,247,880, to which the

Examiner makes reference, represents a form of contrast agent of the type that I have just mentioned. Guerbet's material does not have the charac teristic of remaining in the liver and passing with the bile to the gallbladder, but rather of passing to the kidneys directly. Guerbet's material, in my opinion, would have no value as a contrast agent for gall-bladder X-ray . 1 328236

work, excepting, of course, if it were introduced directly, as would be possible in the course of surgical procedure, into the natural or pathological cavities of the organism to which Guerbet makes reference. That, however, is not what Drs. Dohrn and Diedrich wish to do and would have no relation to their invention.

It is the theory of those who have worked with PRIODAX that the characteristics of these materials to mix in the liver with the bile rests upon the hydroxy diphenyl group, and that the presence of the hydroxy diphenyl group in the series of compounds which Drs. Dohrn and Diedrich mention in their application provide that characteristic of the material whereby it is carried along with the bile. The carboxylic group, converted into the corresponding sodium salt in the intestinal tract, provides suf ficient degree of water-solubility necessary to carry the compound through · the intestinal wall and into the blood stream. The Lodine component, of course, is the agent which provides the contrast characteristic of the entire compound.

When PRIODAX is finally eliminated from the human system, it is in the same chemical form in which it was introduced through the mouth. 

Sworn to before me this 

23rd day of September, 1943.

DIVISION 8 

ERWIN SCHWENK, being duly sworn, deposes and says:

I am a citizen of the United States and reside at Montclair, New Jersey. I am an officer of Schering Corporation of Bloomfield,

New Jersey, and director of its Chemical Research Department. I hold the degree of Chemical Engineer and Dr. Techn. rer. of the Technische

Hochschule in Vienna (Institute of Technology). 

I have been familiar with the manufacture of X-Ray contrast agents since about 1928 and since that time have done considerable research work on the chemical side of this field. I am also generally familiar with the medical phases of X-ray work inasmuch as I have for at least the past ten years conferred with physicians engaged in that branch of the medical field. 

Among the products which have been sold extensively in the X-ray field is one known as NEO-IOPAX used to secure X-ray pictures 328236

of the kidneys and urinary tract. NEO-IOPAX is intravenously administered. The preparation is extremely soluble. It is possible to dissolve 75 and more grams of NEO-IOPAX in 100 co. of water. The 

X-ray exposures are taken in from 10 to 50 minutes, after injection. 

NEO-IOPAX, by reason of its high solubility, readily passes from the liver to the kidneys, and when the kidneys are filled with the solution an X-ray is taken.

I have been familiar with the above-named patent application of Dohrn and Diedrich since it was filed and have taken part in the preparation of the material of that application for use in taking X-ray pictures of the gall bladder. The material was intro duced to the medical profession by Schering Corporation under the trade mark PRIODAX and has been widely and favorably received. Its composition is ẞ(4-hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)-4-phenyl-propionic acid.

PRIODAX was developed and introduced as a gall bladder contrast medium, in contradistinction to NEO-IOPAX, which is a kidney contrast medium, and while the latter passes from the liver to the kidneys, PRIODAX passes to the gall bladder from the liver.

The characteristic of FRIODAX to pass from the liver to the gall bladder is explained as follows!

The liver is the organ which secretes bile, the bile passing from the liver to the gall bladder and accumulates in the gall bladder where it is available as an aid to other organs in the process of digestion and is drawn upon by such organs particularly for emulsifying fats.

The bile has an affinity for PRIODAX and as PRIODAX is withdrawn from the blood stream by the liver it mixes or disperses in the bile and passes with the bile into the gall bladder. The mixture or dispersion of PRIODAX with the bile is wholly physical. 

No chemical reaction takes place between the bile and PRIODAX. 328236

In fact, when the PRIODAX is ultimately eliminated from the human system it is unchanged and exists in the same form in which it was originally introduced.

The bile and PRIODAX accumulate in the gall bladder and in about 12 to 15 hours, when the gall bladder is filled, X-rays may be taken. In the period from the introduction of PRIODAX to the taking of the X-ray pictures, the patient refrains from eating fatty foods inasmuch as such fatty foods in the digestive organs would result in the withdrawal of bile from the gall bladder and the with.. drawal of PRIODAX along with the bile. 

The explanation of the affinity of PRIODAX for the bile from the chemical viewpoint is as follows:

Such products as NEO-IOPAX, which is N-methyl-3, 5-diiodo chelidamic acid sodium salt, as I have explained, are highly soluble in water and because of such high solubility NEO-IOPAX pazzes to the kidneys with the fluids which normally enter the kidneys. NEO-IOPAX has two carboxylic groups and this component, together with the specifie nature of the pyridine ring system, in my opinion, is the factor which renders it highly soluble. PRIODAX also has a carboxylic acid component which renders the material sufficiently soluble to pass into the blood stream and in addition has a hydroxy diphenyl group which, because of its lipoid nature, in my opinion, is a factor contributing to the bile's affinity for PRIODAX. The iodine component is the material which produces the opaque condition in the gall bladder. 

The Dohrn and Diedrich specification sets forth eight examples as illustrative of the invention. Each of these examples includes a hydroxy diphenyl component. The material of Example 4 has been introduced into the market, but there is no reason whatsoever for stating or assuming that the other products named in the application would not be operative. The use of the material is wholly physical and no chemical reaction takes place. Dohrn and Diedrich 328236

are well known workers in the contrast agent field and scientists of standing and their work in this field has been authoratative and scientifically accepted. 

I have examined the Guerbet patent No. 2,247,880 cited by the Examiner as a reference. The Guerbet patent discloses a contrast agent which has value only for carrying out X-ray diagnosis of the urinary tract. The materials of the Guerbet patent have a high water-solubility and would not enter the gall bladder but would pass to the kidneys. The Guerbet products are of the same class as 

NEO-IOPAX. The diphenyl compounds are entirely absent from Guerbet.

The Guerbet material functions entirely differently from the Dohrn and Diedrich compounds and differs also chemically from Dohrn and 

Diedrich's in the following particulars: 

1. Dohrn and Diedrich's invention involves the use of diphenyl compounds, whereas Guerbet's are monophenyl compounds. 

2.In Dohrn and Diedrich's compounds the carboxylic groupa are attached to the aliphatic carbon bridge between two phenyl radicals, whereas in Guerbet the carboxylic groups are attached directly to the phenyl group. 

3. Dohrn and Diedrich's compounds contain a hydroxyl group, whereas Guerbet's do not. 

Emin Schwenk 

Sworn to before me this 2nd day of November, 1943.

Claims 29, 30 and 31 have been amended to define Z (it was thought best to change X to Z to avoid confusion with the X in the formula on page 1 of the specification which stands for a different radical) as having no more than three carbon atoms. This makes it clearer that, with respect to the definition of Z, these claims are generic in scope. Claims 29 and 30 contain no Markush groups and hence have no relationship with claims 21 and 31 on that score.

The definition of the symbol n in claim 31 has been amended to state that it is an integer having a maximum value of 2; while the symbol m has been replaced by 2n. The definition of the symbols n and 2n is therefore generic or sub-generic in character and not in the form of a Markush expression. 

The only symbol that is defined in Markush terms in claim 31 is Y. There is no such term in claims 29 and 30, so that these latter claims on the one hand and claim 31 on the other do not differ among themselves in respect of Markush groups of varying scope. 

Claim 21 is a Markush claim, but, in view of the amendment thereto whereby it calls also for the alkali metal salts, it does not differ from claim 31 so far as Y is concerned, since both claims cover the acids and their alkali metal' salts. It is conceded, however, that claim 21 is a Markush type of claim in listing the specific compounds covered therein and it is recognized that ordinarily claims like 21 and 31 will not be allowed in the same application. However, it is felt that applicants should be permitted to present both types of claims (Just as it is permissible to submit generic and sub-generic claims of different scope) in order to have a ruling from the Patent Office on whether both types or only one type is allowable. When such ruling is received, applicants will then be in a position to elect which type of claim to retain, if both are held allowable, or to appeal. 328236

It is believed that the Examiner will recognize that it would be unfair to compel the applicants to make an election as to which type of claim to present before the Patent Office has indicated which type, or whether both types, are allowable; for if the applicants should elect to present only the broad claims and these are rejected by the Board of Appeals, applicants may not be permitted to file Markush claims, which might nevertheless have been held allowable in the first instance; while if applicants elect the Markush type of claim, they may lose valuable rights if it should turn out that broader claims were patentable.

If the Examiner will allow claims 29, 30 and 31, he is bereby authorized to cancel clain 21; if he refuses to allow claims 

29, 30 and 31, it is believed that he should have no objection to having both types of claims passed on by the Board of Appeals. 

Claim 25 has been cancelled and replaced by claim 34 which is identical with clain 31 so far as the definitions of Y and n are concerned but is more generic with respect to the definition of Z. It is submitted that claims 34 and 31 bear the relation of generic and sub-generic claims, and not of claims of different Markush scope. 

Clain 34 has the same relationship, explained above, to claims 29 and 

30 as has claim 31. 

Summarizing, it is believed that claims 29, 30, 31 and 34 contain only Markush groups of the same scope, where they contain any Markush groups; while claim 21 will be cancelled if the other clains are allowed.

The objection to claims 29 and 30 "as being inept in 'diphenyl carboxylic acid'" is not understood. The Examiner's statement that the "#diphenyl'radical'is one in which two phenyl rings are attached directly to each other", is true only when the word "diphenyl" is used as a noun and not as a prefix. For example in the expression "3,5-diiodo- 

4-hydroxy-diphenyl" the definition of the Examiner applies, but it does not apply in the expression "diphenyl carboxylic acid" or in any other 328236

expression in which the word "diphenyl" is a prefix. 

Hackh's Chemical Dictionary, 1929 edition, defines "diphenyl", the prefix, as indicating the presence of two phenyl radicals. The radical that the Examiner has in mind is not composed of two phenyl radicals but rather of a phenyl and a phenylene radical. That the prefix "diphenyl" means two separate phenyl radicals, which is the meaning in applicants' nomenclature, and that such is the accepted definition of the term, will be evident from the following compounds:

Diphenyl acetylene Ph. Ci C.Ph Diphenyl ethylene Ph. CH: C.Ph Diphenyl hydrazine Ph. MH.NH.Ph 

Diphenyl guanidine (Ph.NH)2C: NH

Diphenyl methane Ph.CH Ph 

It is believed further, that the statement in claims 29 and 30 that a trivalent aliphatic group is located between the phenyl radicals makes it even clearer that the term "diphenyl" in the first line of the claims means two separate phenyl radicals. However, to make it clearer that the aliphatic group referred to in line 3 of claims 29 and 30 forms part of the carboxylic acid group, line 1 of the claims has been amended an to call for/"aliphatic" carboxylic acid.

If the Examiner still objects to the present nomenclature despite the above explanation, he is respectfully requested to give us the benefit of his suggestions, bearing in mind that the scope of the claims should not be unduly restricted by the mode of naming the compounds. 

The rejection of claims 21, 25 (replaced by 34) and 29 to 

33, as being unpatentable over Guerbet, is not understood. It is not seen first of all, what the alleged diversity of the Markush terms has to do with the question of anticipation by or upatentability over Guerbet. So far as the alleged breadth of the claims is concerned, it is submitted that the broadest of applicants' claims is still limited to a poly-iodinated hydroxy diphenyl aliphatic carboxylic acid. compounds contain no hydroxyl group, do not contain two phenyl groups, Guerbet's 328236

and are not aliphatic carboxylic acids. Guerbet's compounds are poly iodinated phthalic acids.

Aside from these very critical and, in our opinion, decidedly controlling differences, there are very important differences between applicants' products and those of Guerbet from the standpoint of solu bility and physiological behavior. In support of this assertion reference is respectfully had to the attached affidavit of Dr. Max Gilbert, a physician who has had long experience in the field of contrast agents for 

X-ray diagnosis.

As explained on page 2 of Dr. Gilbert's affidavit, the compounds of the present invention have the peculiarity that they are absorbed from the blood stream by the liver and pass from the liver. along with the bile, into the gall bladder wherein concentration takes place. The affidavit describes the method of administration and it will be noted that the tablets are taken by mouth. Dr. Gilbert explains that the preferred compound which is now on the market under the name "PRIODAX", described on page 2 of the attached affidavit of Dr. Erwin Schwenk as being (4-hydroxy-3,5-diiodo phenyl)-a-phenyl-propionic acid, does not enter into any chemical reaction in any part of the body, and its selectivity for the liver depends on physical phenomena, that is, it depends upon its compatibility with or solubility in the bile of the liver. For that reason the contrast agent fills the gall bladder homogeneously, which is of course highly important for reliable pictures. At the bottom of page 3 of his affidavit, Dr. Gilbert explains the differences between the action of Guerbet's products and those of the present invention. Guerbet's compounds are highly soluble (which is not true of applicants' compounds) and are of value only for taking pictures of the kidneys, ureters and urinary bladder. Guerbet's compounds are not attracted to and retained by the liver and bile but pass rather directly to the kidneys. For that reason, as Dr. Gilbert states, Guerbet's material would have no value as a contrast agent for gall bladders unless it were introduced directly into the gall bladder, for example, after opening of the abdominal wall. 328236

Where Guerbet speaks of employing his compounds for rendering the biliary vesicle and the hepatic canals opaque to X-rays, he points out that they are "introduced directly" into such organs (page 2, column 1、 line 61).

Reference is had also to the attached affidavit of Dr. Schwenk which, among other things supports Dr. Gilbert's explanation of the manner in which "PRIODAX" reaches the gall bladder. Dr. Schwenk further explains that "PRIODAX" has a carboxylic acid component which makes it sufficiently soluble to pass into the blood stream from the alimentary canal, and, in addition has a hydroxy diphenyl group which is lipoid in nature, which contributes to the bile's affinity for it. These chemical characteristics are of course common to all of applicants' compounds. 

Attention is invited also to Dr. Schwenk's comments with respect to the Guerbet patent. His comments support the definite and important differences of applicants' products over those of the reference. 

The Examiner makes reference to the grounds of rejection on Guerbet given in the official action of March 20, 1943. It appears from the previous official action that all that the Examiner found in common between the two groups of compounds is the presence of "the essential iodine component". Te submit, however, that the iodine is not the component which determines the affinity of the compound for any particular organ. Such affinity is determined by the other parts of the molecule, and in such other parts, applicants' compounds are entirely different from Guerbet's. The Examiner stated in the last official action that Guerbet discloses "similar iodated aromatic carboxylic acid compounds"; but it will be clear from applicants' claims that they are directed to aliphatic carboxylic acids and not aromatic carboxylic acids. In other words, applicants' carboxyl group is attached to aliphatic carbon and not to aromatic carbon. This is a further chemical distinction between the two sets of compounds. 328236

We wish to emphasize that applicants' claims are limited to a rather restricted group of compounds, the same being aliphatic carboxylic acids substituted by two phenyl radicals, one or both of which contains hydroxy and iodine groups. Guerbet's compounds are of a radically different composition. It should be noted also that applicants' claims are entirely within their original disclosure, as can be seen by comparing them with the bottom of page 1 and top of page 2 of the specification, and also with the original claims. 

If the Examiner insists on his rejection of clains 21 and 29 to 34 on Guerbet, he is respectfully requested to cite authority for rejecting claims directed to new compounds which are different from those of a reference both chemically and in their physiological behavior. It is believed that the affidavits filed herewith establish the close family relationship of the claimed compounds and give ample reason for believing that all such compounds not only will have opacifying properties, will be non-toxic and will be capable of administration by mouth, but will have a selective affinity or compatibility with the bile of the liver by reason of the lipoid-character of the molecule. If the Examiner repeats his rejection he is requested also to give his reasons for believing that any compounds falling within applicants' claims will behave like Guerbet's compounds rather than like "PRIODAX" when introduced by mouth. 

The rejection of claims 32 and 33 over claims 21, 25, 29 and 30, or vice versa, is noted. The Examiner appears to treat each group of claims as prior art with respect to the other, which of course is quite unjustified. The case of In re Thuau. 57 U.S.P.Q. 324, involved a situstion wherein the compounds themselves were known and the applicant was attempting to claim them as applied to a new use. The C.C.P.A. held the claims unpatentable in view of the prior use of the very same compounds in tanning. The situation is very materially different here, since the compounds are new.

We submit that an applicant is entitled to claim his invention in more ways than one in order to ensure adequate protection. Courts 328236

have, on a number of occasions, held that an invention was patentable but that the claims were in the wrong category of inventions, e.g. were machine claims instead of being process claims and for that reason held the patent invalid. It is conceivable that in the present case one court might hold that the invention lay in the chemical compounds themselves; while another court might take the position that the invention lay in the X-ray contrast agents containing the novel compounds. Especially in view of the fact that applicants' claims are by no means large in number, it is believed that both groups of claims should be allowed here. 

The objection to claim 33 is not understood.

The claim speci fies that the composition contains as an essential ingredient the compound defined. It is the presence of this compound which makes the composition new. There is nothing in the claim which indicates that it is anything else which makes the composition new. What else the composition contains, whether it is water (in a solution) or a binder (in a tablet), or a bulkproducing diluent, etc., is immaterial. What is material, is that the compound defined it put up in a form in which it is administrable for X-ray diagnosis, and it would not be fair to compel applicants to restrict their claim by specifying any particular and known materials with which ⠀ a diagnostic agent might be administered. of the claim are accordingly requested.

Reconsideration and allowance 

In view of the foregoing, favorable action on claims 21, 29 to 34 and non-elected claims 6 and 15, is respectfully requested. 

If the Examiner insists on his rejection, he is respectfully requested to enter this amendment for purposes of appeal and to forward the appeal filed on or about March 6, 1943. The present amendment does not introduce any new issues into the case and is believed to present the claims in better condition for consideration on appeal. The Examiner is respectfully requested, in the event that he repeats his rejection of any of the claims, so to notify the undersigned attorneys by November 20, so that new notice of appeal may be filed. 

New York, N. Y.,

Respectfully submitted,

AMENDMENT UNDER RULE 78. 

Sir:

Please amend the above-identified application, under the provisions of Rule 78, as follows: 

Claim 6, line 2, cancel "melting at 197°C.". 

Claim 7, line 2, cancel "melting at 159°C.". 

Claim 15, line 2, cancel "melting at 171-172°C.".

Since the compounds defined in claims 6, 7 and 15 are new, it is believed that the reference to the melting points are unnecessary limitations and such references are cancelled by the above amendments. As the Examiner will appreciate the presence of even traces of impurities may considerably alter the melting points and it is believed that the Examiner would agree that an infringer, who has in fact appropriated the invention, should not be 'given an opportunity to argue that the melting point of his product, by reason of the presence of impurities, does not correspond to that set out in the claims. #28236

In view of the fact that the broader claim 34 is not restricted to any melting points, it is believed that there should be no objection to the above amendments and it is requested that they be entered without withdrawing the case from issue.

DIVISION 8 

In view of the reason given by the Examiner for disapproving the proposed amendment under Rule 78, it is thought desirable to make of record the following explanation:

The presence or absence of the melting point in the claims is not considered to be of any consequence so far as the breadth of the claims is concerned, for it is the compound that is claimed as new and not its melting point. The statement in the official communication of 

February 22, 1944 (the Patent Office stamp incorrectly gives "March 22, 1943") that the proposed amendment "broadens the claims concerned by removing limitations which were considered in the allowance thereof" i hardly consistent with the allowance of claim 34 which makes no mention of melting points and is generic to each of the three compounds covered by claims 6, 7 and 15.

It is therefore apparent that in disapproving the proposed amendment the Examiner did not consider all of the facts in the case. How ever, as applicants do not wish to delay further the issuance of the 328236

patent, the final fee will be paid in a few days in this case. Respectfully,
